# On the global well-posedness for the nonlocal Fokas-Lenells equation with the weighted Sobolev initial data on the line

**arXiv ID**: 2607.19649v1
**Authors**: Zhenya Yan, Guoqiang Zhang, Guancheng Zhu
**Published**: 2026-07-22
**Categories**: nlin.SI, math-ph, math.AP, physics.optics, quant-ph
**Comments**: 60 pages, 2 figures
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.19649v1

## Abstract

We establish the global well-posedness of the Cauchy problem for the reverse space-time nonlocal Fokas-Lenells equation with the weighted Sobolev initial data $q_0(x)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R}) \cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})$ on the line. We develop the inverse scattering transform formulated via the associated Riemann-Hilbert problems to study this issue. A spectral uniformization transform is introduced to resolve the singular behavior inherent in the KN-type negative flow spectral problem. Owing to the reverse space-time reduction, reflection coefficients no longer satisfy the usual Hermitian conjugation symmetry, and the coercivity of the jump matrix is therefore not available a priori. The quantitative smallness condition on the initial data yields uniform bounds on the reflection coefficients and ensures the uniform positive definiteness of the Hermitian part of the associated jump matrix. The resulting coercivity allows us to establish the bounded invertibility of the associated singular integral operator through a Fredholm and vanishing-lemma argument. Under this condition, we prove an $L^{2}$-Sobolev bijective correspondence between the potential and scattering data, exclude spectral singularities on continuous spectra, and obtain the global existence and uniqueness of solutions. Moreover, the associated solution map is Lipschitz continuous on the admissible initial-data class.

On the global well-posedness for the nonlocal Fokas-Lenells equation with the weighted Sobolev initial data on the line

Zhenya Yana,b,c,∗,  Guoqiang Zhangb,c,  Guancheng Zhub,c,∗Email address: zyyan@mmrc.iss.ac.cn (Corresponding author)

aSchool of Mathematics and Information Science, Zhongyuan University of Technology, Zhengzhou 450007, China

bState Key Laboratory of Mathematical Sciences, Academy of Mathematics and Systems Science,
Chinese Academy of Sciences, Beijing 100190, China
cSchool of Mathematical Sciences, University of Chinese Academy of Sciences, Beijing 100049, China

Abstract

We establish the global well-posedness of the Cauchy problem for the reverse space-time nonlocal Fokas-Lenells equation with the weighted Sobolev initial dataq0​(x)∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q_{0}(x)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})on the line. We develop the inverse scattering transform formulated via the associated Riemann-Hilbert problems to study this issue. A spectral uniformization transform is introduced to resolve the singular behavior inherent in the KN-type negative flow spectral problem. Owing to the reverse space-time reduction, reflection coefficients no longer satisfy the usual Hermitian conjugation symmetry, and the coercivity of the jump matrix is therefore not available a priori. The quantitative smallness condition on the initial data yields uniform bounds on the reflection coefficients and ensures the uniform positive definiteness of the Hermitian part of the associated jump matrix. The resulting coercivity allows us to establish the bounded invertibility of the associated singular integral operator through a Fredholm and vanishing-lemma argument. Under this condition, we prove anL2L^{2}-Sobolev bijective correspondence between the potential and scattering data, exclude spectral singularities on continuous spectra, and obtain the global existence and uniqueness of solutions. Moreover, the associated solution map is Lipschitz continuous on the admissible initial-data class.

MSC:35P25, 35Q51, 35Q15, 35A01, 35G25, 37K15

Keywords:Reverse space-time nonlocal Fokas-Lenells equation; Cauchy problem; Lipschitz continuous; Riemann-Hilbert problem; Scattering data; Global well-posedness.

## 1Introduction

The study of completely integrable nonlinear systems has long been a central theme in the fields of mathematical physics and applied mathematics, owing to their rich algebraic structures and deep physical significance[1,25]. A prototypical example is the integrable nonlinear Schrödinger (NLS) equation[63]i​qt+qx​x+2​σ​|q|2​q=0,q=q​(x,t),σ=±1,\mathrm{i}q_{t}+q_{xx}+2\sigma|q|^{2}q=0,\quad q=q(x,t),\quad\sigma=\pm 1,(1.1)

which has served for decades as a fundamental model for the propagation of picosecond optical pulses in single-mode optical fibers[7,31]. However, as experimental techniques entered the femtosecond regime, the standard NLS equation (1.1) became insufficient to capture all relevant dynamics, since higher-order nonlinear dispersive effects, including spatio-temporal coupling, can no longer be neglected[14,50]. In this context, the Fokas–Lenells (FL) equation[36]qx​t−q+i​|q|2​qx=0q_{xt}-q+\mathrm{i}|q|^{2}q_{x}=0(1.2)

arose as an integrable model incorporating such higher-order effects, whereqx​tq_{xt}is the spatio-temporal dispersion,|q|2​qx|q|^{2}q_{x}corresponds to the nonlinear self-steepening effect of the Kerr medium. It was first derived by Fokas[27]through two Hamiltonian operators, and was later obtained by Lenells[36]via a canonical transformation from the Kaup-Newell (KN) spectral problem[33]. Therefore, the FL equation (1.2) may be viewed as an integrable generalization of the NLS equation (1.1), the first negative flow of the KN hierarchy, and is particularly relevant to the modelling of ultrashort optical pulses[45].
In fact, Eq. (1.2) can be equivalent to the general form[27,38,37]i​q^τ+α​q^ξ​ξ−β​q^ξ​τ+γ​|q^|2​q^+i​β​γ​|q^|2​q^ξ=0,\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\hat{q}_{\tau}+\alpha\hat{q}_{\xi\xi}-\beta\hat{q}_{\xi\tau}+\gamma|\hat{q}|^{2}\hat{q}+\mathrm{i}\beta\gamma|\hat{q}|^{2}\hat{q}_{\xi}=0,(1.3)

via the gauge transformation[36]q^​(ξ,τ)=1β​α2​β​|γ|​q​(x,t)​exp⁡[i​(1β​ξ+2​αβ2​τ)],x=2​ξ+2​αβ​τ,t=−αβ3​τ.\displaystyle\hat{q}(\xi,\tau)=\frac{1}{\beta}\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{2\beta|\gamma|}}q(x,t)\exp\left[\mathrm{i}\left(\frac{1}{\beta}\xi+\frac{2\alpha}{\beta^{2}}\tau\right)\right],\quad x=2\xi+\frac{2\alpha}{\beta}\tau,\quad t=-\frac{\alpha}{\beta^{3}}\tau.(1.4)

whereα​β>0\alpha\beta>0, otherwise, one can make the transformξ→−ξ\xi\to-\xito make the condition hold.q^ξ​ξ\hat{q}_{\xi\xi}denotes the group velocity dispersion,q^ξ​τ\hat{q}_{\xi\tau}is the spatio-temporal dispersion,|q^|2​q^|\hat{q}|^{2}\hat{q}is the Kerr nonlinear term,|q^|2​q^ξ|\hat{q}|^{2}\hat{q}_{\xi}corresponds to the nonlinear self-steepening effect of the Kerr medium. Eq. (1.3) is sometime simplified as[39,37]qx​t−qx​x+q−2​i​qx∓i​|q|2​qx=0\displaystyle q_{xt}-q_{xx}+q-2\mathrm{i}q_{x}\mp\mathrm{i}|q|^{2}q_{x}=0(1.5)

viaq^​(ξ,τ)=ei​ξ​q​(x,t)\hat{q}(\xi,\tau)=e^{\mathrm{i}\xi}q(x,t)withx=ξ,t=τx=\xi,\,t=\tauandα=β=±γ=1\alpha=\beta=\pm\gamma=1.
Asβ=0\beta=0, Eq. (1.3) reduces to the NLS equation (1.1). However, asβ≠0\beta\not=0, The properties of Eq. (1.3) or (1.2) ) are rather different from ones of the NLS equation (1.1).

In recent years, the theory of integrable systems has been substantially enriched by the work of Ablowitz and Musslimani[4,5], who introduced a class ofnonlocal integrable equations(e.g., nonlocal NLS equation)i​qt+qx​x+2​σ​q2​q¯​(−x,t)=0,q=q​(x,t),σ=±1\mathrm{i}q_{t}+q_{xx}+2\sigma q^{2}\bar{q}(-x,t)=0,\quad q=q(x,t),\quad\sigma=\pm 1(1.6)

involving the parity-time(𝒫​𝒯)(\mathcal{PT})symmetry[10], where the bar denotes the complex conjugate (Notice that (1.6) reduces to (1.1) asq​(−x,t)=q​(x,t)q(-x,t)=q(x,t)).
The notion of𝒫​𝒯\mathcal{PT}symmetry originates in quantum mechanics, where certain non-Hermitian Hamiltonians may possess entirely real spectra under suitable symmetry conditions[10,11,34,54,58]. It has since found physical realizations in classical optics and metamaterials, especially in systems whose refractive indices are engineered to exhibit balanced gain and loss[44,51]. Following these developments, nonlocal reductions involving reverse space, reverse time, and reverse space-time symmetries have been systematically explored[3,55,56,57], and their physical relevance has also been investigated in multi-component settings[60,54,55]. Representative examples include the nonlocal NLS equation[4,53], the nonlocal modified Korteweg-de Vries (mKdV) equation[42], and the nonlocal derivative NLS (DNLS) equation[41]; related multidimensional and general nonlocal integrable models have also been developed[28,6]. These equations exhibit a variety of wave phenomena, such as unconventional soliton structures and singularity formation, that differ markedly from those of their local counterparts[34,53,61].

Over the past several decades, the inverse scattering transform (IST)[29,35], in its Riemann–Hilbert (RH) formulation[12,64], has become one of the principal rigorous tools for analyzing the global dynamics and well-posedness of integrable partial differential equations[46,20]. TheL2L^{2}-Sobolev bijectivity theory developed by Zhou[68,69], together with the Deift–Zhou approach[21,22], provides a powerful analytical framework for constructing direct and inverse scattering maps and for controlling the time evolution of the associated scattering data. In a number of local self-adjoint settings, this framework permits the treatment of large initial data without imposing a small-norm assumption on the potential[23]. It also forms a fundamental basis for the nonlinear steepest descent method and for the analysis of long-time asymptotics and asymptotic stability of solitons[19,13,18].

Aided by these RH techniques, substantial progress has been made for classical local integrable equations. Global well-posedness results for the DNLS equation were obtained by Pelinovskyet al[48,49], Liuet al[43], and Jenkinset al[32]. For the local FL equation (1.2), the global well-posedness in the weighted Sobolev spaceH3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})has been established under various assumptions on the discrete spectrum and spectral singularities[16,17,40]. Moreover, Zhou’sL2L^{2}-Sobolev RH methodology has been applied to several other integrable systems, including the massive Thirring model[47], the modified Camassa–Holm equation[62], and matrix NLS equations[26]. In many local models, self-adjointness or an appropriate Hermitian reduction yields Schwarz reflection symmetries for the scattering data and coercivity properties for the associated jump matrices. These features are central to the unique solvability of the corresponding RH problems.

The passage from local to nonlocal integrable systems introduces substantial analytical differences. Under nonlocal reductions, the standard involution symmetries of the scattering matrix are generally modified[30]. In particular, discrete eigenvalues need not occur in complex-conjugate pairs, and the jump matrices on the continuous spectrum are typically non-Hermitian. Consequently, the coercivity mechanisms available in classical local problems are no longer automatic[5,60]. Despite these difficulties, Ablowitz and Musslimani[5]developed the IST for the reverse-space nonlocal NLS equation, and Rybalko and Shepelsky[52]subsequently derived its long-time asymptotics by the nonlinear steepest descent method. More recently, global well-posedness has been established for several spatially nonlocal equations, including the nonlocal NLS equation[67], the nonlocal mKdV equation[42], and the nonlocal DNLS equation[41], in suitable weighted Sobolev spaces.

While purely spatial nonlocalities have received considerable attention, the incorporation of time-reversal symmetry introduces an additional layer of difficulty. Ablowitzet al[3,2]studied the IST for the reverse space-time nonlocal NLS and sine–Gordon equations, showing that the simultaneous coupling of spatial and temporal reflections leads to a nonstandard interaction between the spatial scattering problem and the time evolution. In particular, the potential entering the scattering problem at timettinvolves the value of the field at the reflected time−t-t. However, to the best of our knowledge, the global well-posedness of the reverse space-time nonlocal FL equation has not yet been fully addressed.

In this paper, we consider the existence of global solutions of the Cauchy problem for the reverse space-time nonlocal Fokas-Lenells (nFL) equation[65]{qx​t−q+i​q​q𝒫​𝒯​qx=0,q​(x,0)=q0​(x)∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ),\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}q_{xt}-q+\mathrm{i}qq^{\mathcal{PT}}q_{x}=0,\vskip 7.22743pt\\
q(x,0)=q_{0}(x)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}),\end{array}\right.(1.7)

where the complex envelope fieldq=q​(x,t)q=q(x,t), the nonlocal termq𝒫​𝒯​(x,t):=q​(−x,−t)q^{\mathcal{PT}}(x,t):=q(-x,-t), no complex conjugation is involved in the definition ofq𝒫​𝒯q^{\mathcal{PT}}, which differs from the nonlocal termq¯​(−x,t)\bar{q}(-x,t)in the nonlocal NLS equation (1.6). Notice that the termq​q𝒫​𝒯qq^{\mathcal{PT}}in the nonlocal FL equation (1.7) must not be real and differs from the similar real-valued term|q|2|q|^{2}in the FL equation (1.2). The nFL Eq. (1.7) possesses the Kaup–Newell-type Lax pair{ϕx=X​(x,t;λ)​ϕ,X=i​λ2​σ3+i​λ​Qx,ϕt=T​(x,t;λ)​ϕ,T=−i4​λ2​σ3+12​λ​Q​σ3+i2​Q2​σ3,\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\phi_{x}=X(x,t;\lambda)\phi,&X=\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}\sigma_{3}+\mathrm{i}\lambda Q_{x},\\[5.0pt]
\phi_{t}=T(x,t;\lambda)\phi,&T=-\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{4\lambda^{2}}\sigma_{3}+\dfrac{1}{2\lambda}Q\sigma_{3}+\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}Q^{2}\sigma_{3},\end{array}\right.(1.8)

whereϕ=ϕ​(x,t;λ)\phi=\phi(x,t;\lambda)is a matrix-valued eigenfunction,λ∈ℂ∖{0}\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}is the spectral parameter, andQ=Q​(x,t)=(0q𝒫​𝒯​(x,t)q​(x,t)0),σ3=(100−1).Q=Q(x,t)=\begin{pmatrix}0&q^{\mathcal{PT}}(x,t)\vskip 4.33601pt\\
q(x,t)&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\vskip 4.33601pt\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.(1.9)

The compatibility condition of the Lax pair (1.8),Xt−Tx+[X,T]=0X_{t}-T_{x}+[X,T]=0,
just yields the nFL equation (1.7) together with its reverse space-time reflected counterpart.

The main analytical difficulties arise in the nonlocal FL equation (1.7) from two sources: The first one is the singular spectral dependence of the Kaup-Newell-type Lax pair, especially nearλ=0\lambda=0andλ=∞\lambda=\infty; The second one is the reverse space-time reduction itself, which modifies the usual symmetry relations among the scattering coefficients. As a result, the RH jump matrix is generally non-Hermitian, and the positivity and coercivity properties available in standard local RH formulations cannot be invoked directly. Recovering an appropriate coercive structure is therefore one of the central issues addressed in this paper.

To address these difficulties, we develop an IST framework adapted to the reverse space-time nonlocal FL equation. We establish a direct and inverse scattering correspondence ofL2L^{2}-Sobolev type between the potential and the associated reflection data[69]for initial data in the weighted Sobolev spaceH3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}).
TheH3H^{3}regularity provides the derivative control needed for the effective potential and for the reconstruction formulas, whereas theH2,1H^{2,1}regularity supplies the spatial decay and spectral regularity required in the direct and inverse scattering maps. These assumptions yield, in particular, the Sobolev regularity, decay properties, and Lipschitz estimates for the reflection coefficients and for the reconstructed potential.

The analysis proceeds in several steps. In the direct scattering transform, the Kaup–Newell-type spectral problem[33]exhibits singular behavior at both the origin and infinity in the naturalλ\lambda-plane. Following the strategies developed for local FL equations[16,17], we combine a suitable gauge transformation with the spectral change of variablesz=λ2z=\lambda^{2},
thereby converting the problem into a Zakharov–Shabat-type spectral problem on thezz-plane. We then construct the Jost solutions, establish their analytic and asymptotic properties, and derive the symmetry relations imposed on the scattering data by the reverse space-time reduction.

In the inverse scattering transform, we formulate a regularized RH problem on the real axis of the transformedzz-plane and reduce it, via the Cauchy projection operators, to a Beals–Coifman singular integral equation. Since the reverse space-time reduction does not impose the usual Hermitian conjugation relation between the two reflection coefficients, the coercivity of the associated jump matrix is not automatic. To recover this property, we impose an explicit smallness condition on the initial effective potential. Volterra estimates for the scattering matrix then show that the two reflection coefficients are uniformly bounded in modulus by a constant strictly smaller than one. This bound implies the uniform positive definiteness of the Hermitian part of the RH jump matrix.q0​(x)∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)I​n​i​t​i​a​l​c​o​n​d​i​t​i​o​n\begin{array}[]{c}q_{0}(x)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\vskip 3.61371pt\\
\footnotesize{Initial\,\,condition}\end{array}r1,2​(0;z)∈𝒲​(ℝ)S​t​a​t​i​o​n​a​r​y​r​e​f​l​e​c​t​i​o​n​c​o​e​f​f​i​c​i​e​n​t​s\begin{array}[]{c}r_{1,2}(0;z)\in\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})\vskip 3.61371pt\\
\footnotesize{Stationary\,\,reflection\,\,coefficients}\end{array}q​(x,t)∈C​(H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ),ℝ)G​l​o​b​a​l​s​o​l​u​t​i​o​n\begin{array}[]{c}q(x,t)\in C(H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}),\mathbb{R})\vskip 3.61371pt\\
Global\,\,solution\end{array}r1​(0;z)​e−i​t2​z,r2​(0;z)​ei​t2​z∈𝒲​(ℝ)T​i​m​e​-​d​e​p​e​n​d​e​n​t​r​e​f​l​e​c​t​i​o​n​c​o​e​f​f​i​c​i​e​n​t​s\begin{array}[]{c}r_{1}(0;z)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z}},r_{2}(0;z)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z}}\in\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})\vskip 3.61371pt\\
Time$-$dependent\,\,reflection\,\,coefficients\end{array}Lipschitz  continuousStep  1: direct scatteringStep  2Time  propagationLipschitz   continuousStep  3: inverse scatteringFigure 1:The general scheme for the global well-posedness of the nFL equation (1.7).

The resulting coercivity enables a Fredholm alternative and vanishing-lemma argument for the corresponding Beals–Coifman singular integral equation[8]. More precisely, the singular integral operator is shown to be Fredholm of index zero, while the coercivity estimate excludes nontrivial solutions of the associated homogeneous problem. The Fredholm alternative then gives bounded invertibility and hence the unique solvability of the RH problem. The same effective-potential estimates also provide a uniform lower bound for the relevant scattering coefficients on the continuous spectrum, thereby excluding spectral singularities. Thus, the imposed smallness condition is a sufficient technical hypothesis for the inverse-scattering framework developed here, rather than a necessary condition for the well-posedness of the equation itself.

The primary objective of this paper is to rigorously establish the global well-posedness of the reverse space-time nonlocal FL equation. The time evolution of the reflection coefficients preserves their moduli and the coercivity bounds for the jump matrix. Consequently, the inverse problem remains uniquely solvable for everyt∈ℝt\in\mathbb{R}, allowing the solution to be reconstructed globally in time (see Fig.1).

Our main result is stated as follows.

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. In Section2, we develop the direct scattering transform, including the construction and analyticity of the Jost solutions, as well as the symmetry and regularity properties of the scattering coefficients under the reverse space-time reduction. In Section3, we formulate the associated RH problems, beginning with a basic RH problem on theλ\lambda-plane and subsequently transforming it into a regularized problem on thezz-plane. In Section4, we develop the inverse scattering transform, derive coercive estimates for the associated jump matrix, and prove the bounded invertibility of the corresponding Beals-Coifman singular integral operator under the effective-potential smallness condition. In Section5, we determine the time evolution of the scattering data, formulate the corresponding time-dependent RH problems, and verify that the coercivity bounds are preserved for all time. Finally, in Section6, we complete the proof of Theorem1.1by establishing global existence, uniqueness, and the Lipschitz continuity of the solution map.

where⟨x⟩:=1+|x|2\langle x\rangle:=\sqrt{1+|x|^{2}},1≤p≤∞1\leq p\leq\inftyandk,s∈ℕ∪{0}k,s\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}. Furthermore, to characterize the regularity of the scattering data, we introduce the space𝒲​(ℝ)\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})by𝒲​(ℝ):={u∈H1​(ℝ)∩L2,1​(ℝ)|x−2​u​(x)∈L2​(ℝ)}.\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R}):=\left\{u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\;\big|\;x^{-2}u(x)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\right\}.(1.24)

TheL1L^{1}matrix norm of the 2-by-2 matrix functionA=(Ai​j)2×2A=(A_{ij})_{2\times 2}is defined as‖A‖L1:=Σi,j=12​‖Ai​j‖L1.\|A\|_{L^{1}}:=\Sigma_{i,j=1}^{2}\|A_{ij}\|_{L^{1}}.And two column vectorsej​(j=1,2)e_{j}\,(j=1,2)are defined ase1=(1,0)T,e2=(0,1)T.\displaystyle e_{1}=(1,\,0)^{T},\qquad e_{2}=(0,\,1)^{T}.(1.25)

## 2Direct scattering problem: Jost solutions and scattering data

In this section, we will prove the existence of the eigenfunctions of the Lax pair (1.8).
With the transformationϕ​(x,t;λ)=ψ​(x,t;λ)​exp⁡[i​(λ2​x−14​λ2​t)​σ3],\phi(x,t;\lambda)=\psi(x,t;\lambda)\exp\left[\mathrm{i}\left(\lambda^{2}x-\frac{1}{4\lambda^{2}}t\right)\sigma_{3}\right],(2.1)

ψ​(x,t;λ)\psi(x,t;\lambda)satisfies a modified Lax pair{ψx=i​λ2​[σ3,ψ]+i​λ​Qx​ψ,ψt=−i4​λ2​[σ3,ψ]+(12​λ​Q+i2​Q2)​σ3​ψ,\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\psi_{x}=\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}[\sigma_{3},\psi]+\mathrm{i}\lambda Q_{x}\psi,\\[5.0pt]
\psi_{t}=-\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{4\lambda^{2}}[\sigma_{3},\psi]+(\dfrac{1}{2\lambda}Q+\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}Q^{2})\sigma_{3}\psi,\end{array}\right.(2.2)

which can be written in the full derivative formd​(ei​(λ2​x−(4​λ)−2​t)​σ^3​ψ)=ei​(λ2​x−(4​λ)−2​t)​σ^3​[i​λ​Qx​d​x+(Q2​λ+i2​Q2)​σ3​d​t]​ψ.d(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\lambda^{2}x-(4\lambda)^{-2}t)\hat{\sigma}_{3}}\psi)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\lambda^{2}x-(4\lambda)^{-2}t)\hat{\sigma}_{3}}\left[\mathrm{i}\lambda Q_{x}\,dx+(\dfrac{Q}{2\lambda}+\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}Q^{2})\sigma_{3}\,dt\right]\psi.(2.3)

andσ^3​(A)=σ3​A​σ3−1\hat{\sigma}_{3}(A)=\sigma_{3}A\sigma_{3}^{-1}. At the same time, this modified Lax pair (2.2) admits the Jost solutions with asymptotics as the potential matrixQxQ_{x}vanishes sufficiently quickly at infinity,ψ±​(x,t,λ)→I,x→±∞.\psi^{\pm}(x,t,\lambda)\to I,\quad x\to\pm\infty.and satisfy Volterra integral equationsψ±​(x,t;λ)=I+i​λ​∫±∞xei​λ2​(x−s)​σ^3​Qs​(s,t)​ψ±​(s,t;λ)​𝑑s.\psi^{\pm}(x,t;\lambda)=I+\mathrm{i}\lambda\int_{\pm\infty}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}(x-s)\hat{\sigma}_{3}}Q_{s}(s,t)\psi^{\pm}(s,t;\lambda)ds.(2.4)

The Lax pair (2.2) has singularities atλ=0\lambda=0andλ=∞\lambda=\infty, which is the same as the FL equation. Here, we will make a transformation to construct the Jost solutions and so on.Figure 2:Analytical regions on theλ\lambda-plane (left) andz=λ2z=\lambda^{2}-plane (right).

## 2.1Jost solutions and analyticity on thezz-plane

As usual, we setttto be fixed and omit it in the following analysis. To more conveniently analyze the Lax pair (2.2), we introduce a transformation defined byΨ​(x;z)=T1​(x;λ)​ψ​(x;λ)​T2​(x;λ),\Psi(x;z)=T_{1}(x;\lambda)\psi(x;\lambda)T_{2}(x;\lambda),(2.5)

withz=λ2z=\lambda^{2}andT1​(x;λ)=(10qx−2​λ),T2​(x;λ)=(100(−2​λ)−1),\displaystyle T_{1}(x;\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\vskip 3.61371pt\\
q_{x}&-2\lambda\end{pmatrix},\quad T_{2}(x;\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\vskip 3.61371pt\\
0&(-2\lambda)^{-1}\end{pmatrix},(2.6)

then the Lax pair (2.2) is changed into a Zakharov-Shabat-type spectral problemΨx=i​z​[σ3,Ψ]+Q^​Ψ,\Psi_{x}=\mathrm{i}z\left[\sigma_{3},\Psi\right]+\widehat{Q}\Psi,(2.7)

whereQ^=i2​(qx​(qP​T)x−(qP​T)xqx2​(qP​T)x−2​i​qx​x−qx​(qP​T)x).\displaystyle\widehat{Q}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\begin{pmatrix}q_{x}(q^{PT})_{x}&-(q^{PT})_{x}\vskip 7.22743pt\\
q_{x}^{2}(q^{PT})_{x}-2\mathrm{i}q_{xx}&-q_{x}(q^{PT})_{x}\end{pmatrix}.(2.8)

Similarly, the time-part of Lax pair (2.2) is also changed into the corresponding form, which is omitted here.

It can be shown thatΨ±​(x;z)\Psi^{\pm}(x;z)satisfies the Volterra integral equationΨ±​(x,t;z)=I+∫±∞xei​z​(x−y)​σ^3​Q^​Ψ±​(s,t;z)​𝑑s.\Psi^{\pm}(x,t;z)=I+\int_{\pm\infty}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(x-y)\hat{\sigma}_{3}}\widehat{Q}\Psi^{\pm}(s,t;z)ds.(2.9)

LetΨ±​(x,t;z)=(Ψ1±​(x,t;z),Ψ2±​(x;z))\Psi^{\pm}(x,t;z)=(\Psi_{1}^{\pm}(x,t;z),\Psi_{2}^{\pm}(x;z)), whereΨj±​(x,t;z)​(j=1,2)\Psi_{j}^{\pm}(x,t;z)\,(j=1,2)denotes the column vectors. Thus, we can show the following proposition (see Fig.2(right)).

## Proposition 2.1.

Letq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}). Then for everyz∈ℝ,z\in\mathbb{R},there exist unique solutionsΨ±​(⋅,t;z)∈L∞​(ℝ)\Psi^{\pm}(\cdot,t;z)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})satisfying (2.9). Moreover, for everyx∈ℝ,Ψ1∓​(x,t;⋅)x\in\mathbb{R},\Psi_{1}^{\mp}(x,t;\cdot)andΨ2±​(x,t;z)\Psi_{2}^{\pm}(x,t;z)are continued analytically inℂ∓\mathbb{C}^{\mp}. Finally, there exists azz-independent constantC>0C>0such that‖Ψ1±​(⋅,t;z)‖L∞+‖Ψ2∓​(⋅,t;z)‖L∞≤Cz∈ℂ±.\|\Psi_{1}^{\pm}(\cdot,t;z)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\Psi_{2}^{\mp}(\cdot,t;z)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\quad z\in\mathbb{C}^{\pm}.(2.10)

## Proof.

It suffices to prove the statement for one of the situations, for example, forΨ1−\Psi_{1}^{-}. The proof for others is analogous. From (2.9), we can obtain thatΨ1−​(x;z)=e1+(K​Ψ1−)​(x;z),e1=(1,0)T,\Psi_{1}^{-}(x;z)=e_{1}+(K\Psi_{1}^{-})(x;z),\qquad e_{1}=(1,0)^{T},(2.11)

with the definition of the integral operatorKKbeing(K​g)​(x;z):=i2​∫−∞x(100e−2​i​z​(x−y))​(qy​(qP​T)y−(qP​T)yqy2​(qP​T)y−2​i​qy​y−qy​(qP​T)y)​g​(y)​𝑑y.(Kg)(x;z):=\dfrac{i}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\vskip 4.33601pt\\
0&\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}q_{y}(q^{PT})_{y}&-(q^{PT})_{y}\vskip 7.22743pt\\
q_{y}^{2}(q^{PT})_{y}-2\mathrm{i}q_{yy}&-q_{y}(q^{PT})_{y}\end{pmatrix}g(y)\,dy.(2.12)

whereg​(x)=(g1,g2)Tg(x)=(g_{1},g_{2})^{T}. We define that‖g‖L∞=‖g1‖L∞+‖g2‖L∞.\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}=\|g_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|g_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}.Thus for everyz∈ℂ−z\in\mathbb{C}^{-}and everyx0∈ℝx_{0}\in\mathbb{R}, we have∥(Kg)(⋅;z)∥L∞​(−∞,x0)≤(∥qx∥L2​(−∞,x0)∥(qP​T)x∥L2​(−∞,x0)+12∥(qP​T)x∥L1​(−∞,x0)+∥qx​x∥L1​(−∞,x0)+12∥qx∥L∞​(−∞,x0)∥qx∥L2​(−∞,x0)∥(qP​T)x∥L2​(−∞,x0))∥g∥L∞​(−∞,x0),\begin{split}\|(Kg)(\cdot;z)\|_{L^{\infty}(-\infty,x_{0})}\leq\bigg(\|q_{x}\|_{L^{2}(-\infty,x_{0})}\|(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{{L^{2}(-\infty,x_{0})}}+\dfrac{1}{2}\|(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{1}(-\infty,x_{0})}+\|q_{xx}\|_{L^{1}(-\infty,x_{0})}\\
+\dfrac{1}{2}\|q_{x}\|_{L^{\infty}(-\infty,x_{0})}\|q_{x}\|_{L^{2}(-\infty,x_{0})}\|(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{{L^{2}(-\infty,x_{0})}}\bigg)\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(-\infty,x_{0})},\end{split}(2.13)

which implies that the operatorKKis contraction ifx0∈ℝx_{0}\in\mathbb{R}is chosen so that‖qx‖L2​‖(qP​T)x‖L2+12​‖(qP​T)x‖L1+‖qx​x‖L1+12​‖qx‖L∞​‖qx‖L2​‖(qP​T)x‖L2<1,\|q_{x}\|_{L^{2}}\|(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{2}}+\dfrac{1}{2}\|(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{1}}+\|q_{xx}\|_{L^{1}}\\
+\dfrac{1}{2}\|q_{x}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|q_{x}\|_{L^{2}}\|(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{2}}<1,(2.14)

here we omit the range to make writing easier. By the Banach fixed point theorem, forx0x_{0}and everyz∈ℂ−z\in\mathbb{C}^{-}, there exist a unique solutionΨ1−​(x;z)∈L∞​(−∞,x0)\Psi_{1}^{-}(x;z)\in L^{\infty}(-\infty,x_{0})to the equation (2.11). To extend this result toL∞​(ℝ)L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}), we can splitℝ\mathbb{R}into a finite number of intervals such that the estimate (2.14) is satisfied in each interval. Unique solutions in each interval can be glued together to obtain the unique solutionΨ1−​(⋅;z)∈L∞​(ℝ)\Psi_{1}^{-}(\cdot;z)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})for everyz∈ℂ−z\in\mathbb{C}^{-}.

The analyticity ofΨ1−​(x;⋅)\Psi_{1}^{-}(x;\cdot)inℂ−\mathbb{C}^{-}for everyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}follows from the absolute and uniform convergence of the Neumann series of analytic functions inzz.
Ifq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), we haveQ^∈L1​(ℝ)\widehat{Q}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}). For everyg​(x;z)∈L∞​(ℝ×ℂ−)g(x;z)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}^{-}), we have‖Kn​g‖L∞≤1n!​‖Q^‖L1n​‖g‖L∞.\|K^{n}g\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\frac{1}{n!}\|\widehat{Q}\|_{L^{1}}^{n}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}.(2.15)

As a result, the Neumann series for the Volterra integral equation (2.11) forΨ1−\Psi_{1}^{-}converges
absolutely and uniformly for everyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}andz∈ℂ−z\in\mathbb{C}^{-}and contains analytic functions ofzzforz∈ℂ−z\in\mathbb{C}^{-}. Therefore,Ψ1​(x;⋅)\Psi_{1}(x;\cdot)is analytic inℂ−\mathbb{C}^{-}for everyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}and it satisfies the bound (2.10). ∎

## Proposition 2.2.

Under the conditions of Proposition2.1, for everyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}, the Jost solutionsΨ±​(x;z)\Psi^{\pm}(x;z)admit the following limits along a contour in the domains of their analyticity extended to|Im​z|→∞|\mathrm{Im}z|\to\inftylim|z|→∞Ψ±​(x;z)=ei2​m±​σ3,m±​(x)=∫±∞xqy​(qP​T)y​𝑑y.\lim_{|z|\to\infty}\Psi^{\pm}(x;z)=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{i}{2}m_{\pm}\sigma_{3}},\quad m_{\pm}(x)=\int_{\pm\infty}^{x}q_{y}(q^{PT})_{y}\,dy.(2.16)

Moreover, ifq∈C2​(ℝ)q\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}), then for everyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}, the Jost solutions satisfy the following limits along a contour in the domains of their analyticity extended to|Im​z|→∞|\mathrm{Im}z|\to\inftylim|z|→∞z​(Ψ1±​(x;z)−ei2​m±​(x)​e1)\displaystyle\lim_{|z|\to\infty}z(\Psi_{1}^{\pm}(x;z)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)}e_{1})=s1±​(x)​e1+s2±​(x)​e2,\displaystyle=s_{1}^{\pm}(x)e_{1}+s_{2}^{\pm}(x)e_{2},(2.17a)lim|z|→∞z​(Ψ2±​(x;z)−e−i2​m±​(x)​e2)\displaystyle\lim_{|z|\to\infty}z(\Psi_{2}^{\pm}(x;z)-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)}e_{2})=ν1±​(x)​e1+ν2±​(x)​e2,\displaystyle=\nu_{1}^{\pm}(x)e_{1}+\nu_{2}^{\pm}(x)e_{2},(2.17b)

wheres1±​(x)\displaystyle s_{1}^{\pm}(x)=−14​ei2​m±​(x)​∫±∞x((qP​T)y​(y)​qy​y​(y)+i2​((qP​T)y)2​(y)​qy2​(y))​𝑑y,\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)}\int_{\pm\infty}^{x}\left((q^{PT})_{y}(y)q_{yy}(y)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}((q^{PT})_{y})^{2}(y)q_{y}^{2}(y)\right)dy,s2±​(x)\displaystyle s_{2}^{\pm}(x)=−i2​∂x(qx​(x)​ei2​m±​(x)),\displaystyle=-\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\partial_{x}(q_{x}(x)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{i}{2}m_{\pm}(x)}),ν1±​(x)\displaystyle\nu_{1}^{\pm}(x)=14​(qP​T)x​(x)​e−i2​m±​(x),\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(q^{PT})_{x}(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)},ν2±​(x)\displaystyle\nu_{2}^{\pm}(x)=14​e−i2​m±​(x)​∫±∞x((qP​T)y​(y)​qy​y​(y)+i2​((qP​T)y)2​(y)​qy2​(y))​𝑑y.\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{4}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)}\int_{\pm\infty}^{x}\left((q^{PT})_{y}(y)q_{yy}(y)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}((q^{PT})_{y})^{2}(y)q_{y}^{2}(y)\right)dy.

## Proof.

Again, we prove the statement for the Jost solutionΨ1−\Psi_{1}^{-}only. The proofs of others are analogous. We rewrite the integral equation (2.9) in the component form:Ψ11−​(x;z)=1+i2​∫−∞x(qP​T)y​[qy​Ψ11−​(y;z)−Ψ21−​(y;z)]​𝑑y,\Psi_{11}^{-}(x;z)=1+\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{x}(q^{PT})_{y}\left[q_{y}\Psi_{11}^{-}(y;z)-\Psi_{21}^{-}(y;z)\right]dy,(2.18)Ψ21−​(x;z)=i2​∫−∞xe−2​i​z​(x−y)​[(qy2​(qP​T)y−2​i​qy​y)​Ψ11−​(y;z)−qy​(qP​T)y​Ψ21−​(y;z)]​𝑑y.\Psi_{21}^{-}(x;z)=\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}\left[\left(q_{y}^{2}(q^{PT})_{y}-2\mathrm{i}q_{yy}\right)\Psi_{11}^{-}(y;z)-q_{y}(q^{PT})_{y}\Psi_{21}^{-}(y;z)\right]dy.(2.19)

By bounds (2.10) in Proposition2.1, for everyq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), the integrand of (2.19) is bounded for everyz∈ℂ−z\in\mathbb{C}^{-}by am absolutely integrablezz-independent function. While forz∈ℂ−z\in\mathbb{C}^{-}, we have|e−2​i​z​(x−y)|=e2​I​m​z​(x−y)→0,|z|→∞|\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}|=\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{Im}z(x-y)}\to 0,\quad|z|\to\infty, by Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, we obtain thatΨ21−​(x;z)→0,|z|→∞\Psi_{21}^{-}(x;z)\to 0,\,\,|z|\to\inftyandlim|z|→∞Ψ11−​(x;z)=1+i2​∫−∞xqy​(qP​T)y​lim|z|→∞Ψ11−​(y;z)​d​y.\lim_{|z|\to\infty}\Psi_{11}^{-}(x;z)=1+\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{x}q_{y}(q^{PT})_{y}\lim_{|z|\to\infty}\Psi_{11}^{-}(y;z)\,dy.(2.20)

By solving the integral equation, we have a unique solutionlim|z|→∞Ψ11−​(x;z)=ei2​m−​(x),\lim_{|z|\to\infty}\Psi_{11}^{-}(x;z)=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)},(2.21)

which meanslim|z|→∞Ψ1−​(x;z)=ei2​m−​(x)​e1.\lim_{|z|\to\infty}\Psi_{1}^{-}(x;z)=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}e_{1}.(2.22)

In a similar way, we can show thatlim|z|→∞Ψ2−​(x;z)=e−i2​m−​(x)​e2.\lim_{|z|\to\infty}\Psi_{2}^{-}(x;z)=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}e_{2}.(2.23)

Combining the above equations, we can get (2.16).

For everyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}and everyδ>0\delta>0, we use the Laplace method of asymptotic analysis, which allows us to split integration for(−∞,x−δ)(-\infty,x-\delta)and(x−δ,x)(x-\delta,x). And we rewrite the integral equation ofΨ21−\Psi_{21}^{-}in the equivalent form:Ψ21−​(x;z)=∫−∞x−δe−2​i​z​(x−y)​μ​(y;z)​𝑑y+μ​(x;z)​∫x−δxe−2​i​z​(x−y)​𝑑y+∫x−δxe−2​i​z​(x−y)​(μ​(y;z)−μ​(x;z))​𝑑y≡I1+I2+I3,\begin{array}[]{rl}\Psi_{21}^{-}(x;z)&\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{x-\delta}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}\mu(y;z)\,dy+\mu(x;z)\int_{x-\delta}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}\,dy\vskip 4.33601pt\\
&\qquad\displaystyle+\int_{x-\delta}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}(\mu(y;z)-\mu(x;z))\,dy\vskip 4.33601pt\\
&\displaystyle\equiv I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3},\end{array}(2.24)

whereμ​(x;z)=i2​[(qx2​(qP​T)x−2​i​qx​x)​Ψ11−​(x;z)−qx​(qP​T)x​Ψ21−​(x;z)].\mu(x;z)=\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\left[\left(q_{x}^{2}(q^{PT})_{x}-2\mathrm{i}q_{xx}\right)\Psi_{11}^{-}(x;z)-q_{x}(q^{PT})_{x}\Psi_{21}^{-}(x;z)\right].

Sinceμ​(⋅;z)∈L1​(ℝ)\mu(\cdot;z)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}), we have|I1|≤e2​δ​Im​z​‖μ​(⋅;z)‖L1.|I_{1}|\leq\mathrm{e}^{2\delta\mathrm{Im}z}\|\mu(\cdot;z)\|_{L^{1}}.

Sinceμ​(⋅;z)∈C​(ℝ)\mu(\cdot;z)\in C(\mathbb{R}), we have|I3|≤12​|Im​z|​‖μ​(⋅;z)−μ​(x;z)‖L∞​(x−δ,x).|I_{3}|\leq\dfrac{1}{2|\mathrm{Im}z|}\|\mu(\cdot;z)-\mu(x;z)\|_{L^{\infty}(x-\delta,x)}.

On the other hand, we have the exact valueI2=12​i​z​(1−e−2​i​z​δ)​μ​(x;z).I_{2}=\dfrac{1}{2\mathrm{i}z}(1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z\delta})\mu(x;z).

Let us chooseδ:=[−Im​z]−1/2\delta:=[-\mathrm{Im}z]^{-1/2}such thatδ→0\delta\to 0as|Im​z|→∞|\mathrm{Im}z|\to\infty, then we obtainlim|z|→∞z​Ψ21−​(x;z)=−i2​lim|z|→∞μ​(x;z)=14​(−2​i​qx​x​(x)+qx2​(x)​(qP​T)x​(x))​ei2​m−​(x)=s2−​(x).\begin{split}\lim_{|z|\to\infty}z\Psi_{21}^{-}(x;z)&=-\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\lim_{|z|\to\infty}\mu(x;z)=\dfrac{1}{4}\left(-2\mathrm{i}q_{xx}(x)+q_{x}^{2}(x)(q^{PT})_{x}(x)\right)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{i}{2}m_{-}(x)}=s_{2}^{-}(x).\end{split}(2.25)

withs2−​(x)=−i2​∂x(qx​(x)​ei2​m−​(x)).s_{2}^{-}(x)=-\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\partial_{x}(q_{x}(x)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{i}{2}m_{-}(x)}).

On the other hand, the first equation (2.18) can be rewritten as the differential equation∂xΨ11−​(x;z)=i2​(qx​(qP​T)x)​(x)​Ψ11−​(x;z)−i2​(qP​T)x​(x)​Ψ21−​(x;z),\partial_{x}\Psi_{11}^{-}(x;z)=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(q_{x}(q^{PT})_{x})(x)\Psi_{11}^{-}(x;z)-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(q^{PT})_{x}(x)\Psi_{21}^{-}(x;z),

and we can use the integrating factore−i2​m−​(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{i}{2}m_{-}(x)}, then∂x(Ψ11−​(x;z)​e−i2​m−​(x))=−i2​(qP​T)x​(x)​Ψ21−​(x;z)​e−i2​m−​(x).\partial_{x}\left(\Psi_{11}^{-}(x;z)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}\right)=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(q^{PT})_{x}(x)\Psi_{21}^{-}(x;z)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}.

and we can get thatΨ11−​(x;z)−ei2​m−​(x)=ei2​m−​(x)​∫−∞x−i2​(qP​T)y​(y)​Ψ21−​(y;z)​e−i2​m−​(y)​d​y.\Psi_{11}^{-}(x;z)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}\int_{-\infty}^{x}-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(q^{PT})_{y}(y)\Psi_{21}^{-}(y;z)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(y)}\,dy.(2.26)

Also, taking the limit|z|→∞|z|\to\inftyofz​(Ψ11−​(x;z)−ei2​m−​(x))z(\Psi_{11}^{-}(x;z)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}), we havelim|z|→∞z​(Ψ11−​(x;z)−ei2​m−​(x))=−14​ei2​m−​(x)​∫−∞x((qP​T)y​(y)​qy​y​(y)+i2​((qP​T)y)2​(y)​qy2​(y))​𝑑y=s1−​(x).\begin{array}[]{rl}\displaystyle\lim_{|z|\to\infty}z(\Psi_{11}^{-}(x;z)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)})=&\displaystyle\!\!\!\!-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\left((q^{PT})_{y}(y)q_{yy}(y)+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}((q^{PT})_{y})^{2}(y)q_{y}^{2}(y)\right)dy\vskip 7.22743pt\\
=&\!\!\!\!s_{1}^{-}(x).\end{array}(2.27)

In the end, we get the limits ofΨ1−\Psi_{1}^{-}in (2.17). ∎

The next proposition plays a very important role in our subsequent estimates. Its proof is similar to the argument in[49], which we just modify the notation, so we will not elaborate on it here.

## Proposition 2.3.

Ifg∈L2​(ℝ)g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}), thensupx∈ℝ‖∫−∞xe−2​i​z​(x−y)​g​(y)​𝑑y‖Lz2​(ℝ)≤π​‖g‖L2.\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\int_{-\infty}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}g(y)\,dy\right\|_{L^{2}_{z}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\sqrt{\pi}\|g\|_{L^{2}}.(2.28)

Ifg∈H1​(ℝ)g\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}), thensupx∈ℝ‖g​(x)−2​i​z​∫−∞xe−2​i​z​(x−y)​g​(y)​𝑑y‖Lz2​(ℝ)≤π​‖gx‖L2.\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|g(x)-2\mathrm{i}z\int_{-\infty}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}g(y)\,dy\right\|_{L^{2}_{z}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\sqrt{\pi}\|g_{x}\|_{L^{2}}.(2.29)

Moreover, ifg∈L2,1​(ℝ)g\in L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), thensupx∈(−∞,x0)‖⟨x⟩​∫−∞xe−2​i​z​(x−y)​g​(y)​𝑑y‖Lz2​(ℝ)≤π​‖g‖L2,1​(−∞,x0)\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\left\|\langle x\rangle\int_{-\infty}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}g(y)\,dy\right\|_{L^{2}_{z}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\sqrt{\pi}\|g\|_{L^{2,1}(-\infty,x_{0})}(2.30)

for everyx0∈ℝ−x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{-}.

In fact, Proposition2.3can also allow us to analyze the smoothness of various remainder terms. It is worthy noting thatqP​T​(x)=q​(−x)q^{PT}(x)=q(-x), which means if we integrate over the entire real axis, the integral values of the two functions will actually be the same in the sense of the norm. The following proposition gives the smoothness of the Jost solutions

## Proposition 2.4.

Ifq∈H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), then for everyx∈ℝ±x\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm}, we haveΨ±​(x;⋅)−ei2​m±​(x)​σ3∈H1​(ℝ).\Psi^{\pm}(x;\cdot)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)\sigma_{3}}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}).(2.31)

Moreover, ifq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), then for everyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}, we havez​(Ψ1±​(x;z)−ei2​m±​(x)​e1)\displaystyle z(\Psi_{1}^{\pm}(x;z)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)}e_{1})−(s1±​(x)​e1+s2±​(x)​e2)∈Lz2​(ℝ),\displaystyle-(s_{1}^{\pm}(x)e_{1}+s_{2}^{\pm}(x)e_{2})\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}),(2.32a)z​(Ψ2±​(x;z)−e−i2​m±​(x)​e2)\displaystyle z(\Psi_{2}^{\pm}(x;z)-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)}e_{2})−(ν1±​(x)​e1+ν2±​(x)​e2)∈Lz2​(ℝ).\displaystyle-(\nu_{1}^{\pm}(x)e_{1}+\nu_{2}^{\pm}(x)e_{2})\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}).(2.32b)

## Proof.

Again, we prove the statement for the Jost solutionΨ1−\Psi_{1}^{-}. The proof for other Jost solutions is analogous. We rewrite equation (2.11) in the equivalent form(I−K)​(Ψ1−−ei2​m−​(x)​e1)=h​e2,(I-K)(\Psi_{1}^{-}-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}e_{1})=he_{2},(2.33)

whereh​(x;z)=∫−∞xe−2​i​z​(x−y)​w​(y)​𝑑y,w​(x)=∂x(qx​(x)​ei2​m−​(x)).h(x;z)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}w(y)\,dy,\quad w(x)=\partial_{x}\left(q_{x}(x)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}\right).(2.34)

Ifq∈H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), thenw∈L2,1​(ℝ)w\in L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}). By the bounds (2.28) and (2.30), we haveh​(x;z)∈Lx∞​(ℝ;Lz2​(ℝ))h(x;z)\in L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}))and for everyx0∈ℝ−x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{-}, the following bound is satisfied:supx∈(−∞,x0)‖⟨x⟩​h​(x;z)‖Lz2​(ℝ)≤e‖qx‖L222​π​(‖qx​x‖L2,1+12​‖qx2​(qP​T)x‖L2,1)≤C​(‖q‖H2,1+‖q‖H2,13),\begin{split}\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\|\langle x\rangle h(x;z)\|_{L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}&\leq\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\|q_{x}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{2}}\sqrt{\pi}\left(\|q_{xx}\|_{L^{2,1}}+\frac{1}{2}\|q_{x}^{2}(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{2,1}}\right)\\
&\leq C\left(\|q\|_{H^{2,1}}+\|q\|_{H^{2,1}}^{3}\right),\end{split}(2.35)

whereCCis a positive constant and we use the Sobolev embedding to get the final inequality.

By using estimates similar to those in the derivation of the bound (2.15) we find that for everyg​(x;z)∈Lx∞​(ℝ;Lz2​(ℝ))g(x;z)\in L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})), we have‖Kn​g‖Lx∞​Lz2≤1n!​‖Q^‖L1n​‖g​(x;z)‖Lx∞​Lz2.\|K^{n}g\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}L_{z}^{2}}\leq\dfrac{1}{n!}\|\widehat{Q}\|_{L^{1}}^{n}\|g(x;z)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}L_{z}^{2}}.(2.36)

Therefore, the operatorI−KI-Kis invertible on the spaceLx∞​(ℝ;Lz2​(ℝ))L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})), and a bound on the inverse operator is given by‖(I−K)−1‖Lx∞​Lz2​(ℝ)→Lx∞​Lz2​(ℝ)≤e‖Q^‖L1.\|(I-K)^{-1}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\to L_{x}^{\infty}L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\|\widehat{Q}\|_{L^{1}}}.(2.37)

Moreover, the same estimate (2.37) can be obtained in the normLx∞​((−∞,x0);Lz2​(ℝ))L_{x}^{\infty}((-\infty,x_{0});L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}))for everyx0∈ℝ−x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{-}. By using (2.33), (2.35) and (2.37), we have the estimatesupx∈(−∞,x0)‖⟨x⟩​(Ψ1−​(x;z)−ei2​m−​(x)​e1)‖Lz2≤C​e‖Q^‖L1​(‖q‖H2,1+‖q‖H2,13).\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\|\langle x\rangle\left(\Psi_{1}^{-}(x;z)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}e_{1}\right)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{\|\widehat{Q}\|_{L^{1}}}\left(\|q\|_{H^{2,1}}+\|q\|_{H^{2,1}}^{3}\right).(2.38)

To end the proof of (2.32), we need to show∂zΨ1−​(x;z)∈Lx∞​((−∞,x0);Lz2​(ℝ))\partial_{z}\Psi_{1}^{-}(x;z)\in L_{x}^{\infty}((-\infty,x_{0});L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}))for everyx0∈ℝx_{0}\in\mathbb{R}. We
differentiate the integral equation (2.11) inzzand introduce the vectorv​(x;z):=[∂zΨ11−​(x;z),∂zΨ21−​(x;z)+2​i​x​Ψ21−​(x;z)],v(x;z):=\left[\partial_{z}\Psi_{11}^{-}(x;z),\,\partial_{z}\Psi_{21}^{-}(x;z)+2ix\Psi_{21}^{-}(x;z)\right],

Thus, we obtain from (2.11) that(I−K)​v=μ1​e1+μ2​e2+μ3​e2,(I-K)v=\mu_{1}e_{1}+\mu_{2}e_{2}+\mu_{3}e_{2},(2.39)

whereμ1​(x;z)\displaystyle\mu_{1}(x;z)=−∫−∞xy​(qP​T)y​(y)​Ψ21−​(y;z)​𝑑y,\displaystyle=-\int_{-\infty}^{x}y(q^{PT})_{y}(y)\Psi_{21}^{-}(y;z)\,dy,μ2​(x;z)\displaystyle\mu_{2}(x;z)=∫−∞xy​e−2​i​z​(x−y)​(2​i​qy​y​(y)−qy2​(y)​(qP​T)y​(y))​(Ψ11−​(y;z)−ei2​m−​(y))​𝑑y,\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{x}y\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}\left(2\mathrm{i}q_{yy}(y)-q_{y}^{2}(y)(q^{PT})_{y}(y)\right)\left(\Psi_{11}^{-}(y;z)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(y)}\right)dy,μ3​(x;z)\displaystyle\mu_{3}(x;z)=∫−∞xy​e−2​i​z​(x−y)​(2​i​qy​y​(y)−qy2​(y)​(qP​T)y​(y))​ei2​m−​(y)​𝑑y.\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{x}y\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}\left(2\mathrm{i}q_{yy}(y)-q_{y}^{2}(y)(q^{PT})_{y}(y)\right)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(y)}\,dy.

For everyx0∈ℝ−x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{-}, applying the Hölder’s inequality toμ1\mu_{1}and using the estimate (2.28) forμ2\mu_{2}andμ3\mu_{3}, we obtain the following bounds:supx∈(−∞,x0)‖μ1​(x;⋅)‖Lz2​(ℝ)≤‖(qP​T)x‖L1​supx∈(−∞,x0)‖⟨x⟩​Ψ21−​(x;z)‖Lz2​(ℝ),supx∈(−∞,x0)‖μ2​(x;⋅)‖Lz2​(ℝ)≤(2​‖qx​x‖L1+‖qx‖L∞​‖qx‖L2​‖(qP​T)x‖L2)×supx∈(−∞,x0)∥⟨x⟩(Ψ11−(x;z)−ei2​m−​(x))∥Lz2​(ℝ),supx∈(−∞,x0)‖μ3​(x;⋅)‖Lz2​(ℝ)≤π​e12​‖qx‖L2​‖(qP​T)x‖L2​(2​‖qx​x‖L2,1+‖qx2​(qP​T)x‖L2,1).\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rl}\displaystyle\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\|\mu_{1}(x;\cdot)\|_{L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\!\!\!&\leq\displaystyle\|(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{1}}\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\|\langle x\rangle\Psi_{21}^{-}(x;z)\|_{L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})},\vskip 7.22743pt\\
\displaystyle\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\|\mu_{2}(x;\cdot)\|_{L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\!\!\!&\displaystyle\leq\left(2\|q_{xx}\|_{L^{1}}+\|q_{x}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|q_{x}\|_{L^{2}}\|(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\vskip 4.33601pt\\
\!\!\!&\qquad\displaystyle\times\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\left\|\langle x\rangle\left(\Psi_{11}^{-}(x;z)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}\right)\right\|_{L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})},\vskip 4.33601pt\\
\displaystyle\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\|\mu_{3}(x;\cdot)\|_{L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\!\!\!&\leq\displaystyle\sqrt{\pi}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\|q_{x}\|_{L^{2}}\|(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{2}}}\left(2\|q_{xx}\|_{L^{2,1}}+\|q_{x}^{2}(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{2,1}}\right).\end{array}

Because of the estimate (2.35), the first two bounds are finite. By the invertibility of(I−K)(I-K)in (2.37) via the uniformly convergent Neumann series, we summarize thatv​(x;z)∈Lx∞​((−∞,x0);Lz2​(ℝ))v(x;z)\in L_{x}^{\infty}((-\infty,x_{0});L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}))for everyx0∈ℝ−x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{-}. This implies∂zΨ1−​(x;⋅)∈Lz2​(ℝ)\partial_{z}\Psi_{1}^{-}(x;\cdot)\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}), yielding the proof of (2.31).

To prove (2.32), we note thatq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})guarantees thatw​(x)∈H1​(ℝ)w(x)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}). We can obtain that(I−K)​[z​(Ψ1−−ei2​m−​(x)​e1)−(s1−​e1+s2−​e2)]=μ~​e2,(I-K)\left[z\left(\Psi_{1}^{-}-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}e_{1}\right)-\left(s_{1}^{-}e_{1}+s_{2}^{-}e_{2}\right)\right]=\tilde{\mu}e_{2},(2.40)

withμ~​(x;z)=z​∫−∞xe−2​i​z​(x−y)​w​(y)​𝑑y−s2−​(x)+i2​∫−∞xe−2​i​z​(x−y)​[(qy2​(qP​T)y−2​i​qy​y)​s1−​(y)−qy​(qP​T)y​s2−​(y)]​𝑑y.\begin{array}[]{rl}\tilde{\mu}(x;z)\!\!\!&=\displaystyle z\int_{-\infty}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}w(y)\,dy-s_{2}^{-}(x)\vskip 7.22743pt\\
\!\!\!&\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}\left[\left(q_{y}^{2}(q^{PT})_{y}-2\mathrm{i}q_{yy}\right)s_{1}^{-}(y)-q_{y}(q^{PT})_{y}s_{2}^{-}(y)\right]dy.\end{array}

By using bounds (2.28) and (2.29), we haveμ~​(x;z)∈Lx∞​(ℝ;Lz2​(ℝ))\tilde{\mu}(x;z)\in L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}))ifw∈H1​(ℝ)w\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}). Inverting(I−K)(I-K)onLx∞​(ℝ;Lz2​(ℝ))L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})), we finally obtain (2.32) forΨ1−\Psi_{1}^{-}. ∎

To deduce the Lipschitz continuity of the scattering data, we need to analyze the Lipschitz continuity of the Jost solutions. The following result shows that the mapH2,1​(ℝ)∋q→(Ψ±​(x;z)−ei2​m±​(x)​σ3)∈Lx∞​(ℝ±;Hz1​(ℝ))H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\ni q\to\left(\Psi^{\pm}(x;z)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)\sigma_{3}}\right)\in L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\pm};H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}))(2.41)

is Lipschitz continuous. Moreover, the𝒪​(z−1)\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})term also has the continuity.

## Corollary 2.5.

Letq,q~∈H2,1​(ℝ)q,\tilde{q}\in H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})satisfy‖u‖H2,1,‖u~‖H2,1≤δ\|u\|_{H^{2,1}},\|\tilde{u}\|_{H^{2,1}}\leq\deltafor someδ>0\delta>0. Then, there is a positiveδ\delta-independent constantC​(δ)C(\delta)such that for everyx∈ℝ±x\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm}, we have‖Ψ1±​(x;⋅)−ei2​m±​(x)​e1−Ψ~1±​(x;⋅)+ei2​m~±​(x)​e1‖H1≤C​(δ)​‖q−q~‖H2,1.\|\Psi_{1}^{\pm}(x;\cdot)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)}e_{1}-\tilde{\Psi}_{1}^{\pm}(x;\cdot)+\mathrm{e}^{\frac{i}{2}\tilde{m}_{\pm}(x)}e_{1}\|_{H^{1}}\leq C(\delta)\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2,1}}.(2.42)

and‖Ψ2±​(x;⋅)−e−i2​m±​(x)​e2−Ψ~2±​(x;⋅)+e−i2​m~±​(x)​e2‖H1≤C​(δ)​‖q−q~‖H2,1.\|\Psi_{2}^{\pm}(x;\cdot)-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)}e_{2}-\tilde{\Psi}_{2}^{\pm}(x;\cdot)+\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{i}{2}\tilde{m}_{\pm}(x)}e_{2}\|_{H^{1}}\leq C(\delta)\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2,1}}.(2.43)

Furthermore, ifq,q~∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q,\tilde{q}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})satisfy‖q‖H3∩H2,1,‖q~‖H3∩H2,1≤δ\|q\|_{H^{3}\cap H^{2,1}},\|\tilde{q}\|_{H^{3}\cap H^{2,1}}\leq\delta, then for everyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}, there is a positive constant such that‖Ψ^1±​(x;⋅)−Ψ~^1±​(x;⋅)‖L2+‖Ψ^2±​(x;⋅)−Ψ~^2±​(x;⋅)‖L2≤C​(δ)​‖q−q~‖H3∩H2,1.\|\hat{\Psi}_{1}^{\pm}(x;\cdot)-\hat{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1}^{\pm}(x;\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{\pm}(x;\cdot)-\hat{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}^{\pm}(x;\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}\leq C(\delta)\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{3}\cap H^{2,1}}.(2.44)

whereΨ^1±​(x;z)\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{1}^{\pm}(x;z):=z​(Ψ1±​(x;z)−ei2​m±​(x)​e1)−(s1±​(x)​e1+s2±​(x)​e2),\displaystyle:=z(\Psi_{1}^{\pm}(x;z)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)}e_{1})-(s_{1}^{\pm}(x)e_{1}+s_{2}^{\pm}(x)e_{2}),\vskip 4.33601pt(2.45)Ψ^2±​(x;z)\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{\pm}(x;z):=z​(Ψ2±​(x;z)−e−i2​m±​(x)​e2)−(ν1±​(x)​e1+ν2±​(x)​e2).\displaystyle:=z(\Psi_{2}^{\pm}(x;z)-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)}e_{2})-(\nu_{1}^{\pm}(x)e_{1}+\nu_{2}^{\pm}(x)e_{2}).(2.46)

## Proof.

Again, we prove the statement for the Jost solutionΨ1−\Psi_{1}^{-}. The proof for other Jost solutions is analogous.

For everyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}, utilizing the nonlocal symmetryqP​T​(x)=q​(−x)q^{PT}(x)=q(-x)andq~P​T​(x)=q~​(−x)\tilde{q}^{PT}(x)=\tilde{q}(-x), we can estimate the difference of the exponential phase factors. Since‖(qP​T)x‖L2=‖qx‖L2\|(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{2}}=\|q_{x}\|_{L^{2}}, we have:|m−​(x)−m~−​(x)|≤∫−∞x|qy​(qP​T)y−q~y​(q~P​T)y|​𝑑y≤∫−∞x|qy​((qP​T)y−q~yP​T)|​𝑑y+∫−∞x|(qy−q~y)​q~yP​T|​𝑑y≤‖qx‖L2​‖(qP​T)x−(q~P​T)x‖L2+‖qx−q~x‖L2​‖(q~P​T)x‖L2≤2​δ​‖q−q~‖H2,1.\begin{split}|m_{-}(x)-\tilde{m}_{-}(x)|&\leq\int_{-\infty}^{x}|q_{y}(q^{PT})_{y}-\tilde{q}_{y}(\tilde{q}^{PT})_{y}|\,dy\\
&\leq\int_{-\infty}^{x}|q_{y}((q^{PT})_{y}-\tilde{q}^{PT}_{y})|dy+\int_{-\infty}^{x}|(q_{y}-\tilde{q}_{y})\tilde{q}^{PT}_{y}|\,dy\\
&\leq\|q_{x}\|_{L^{2}}\|(q^{PT})_{x}-(\tilde{q}^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{2}}+\|q_{x}-\tilde{q}_{x}\|_{L^{2}}\|(\tilde{q}^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{2}}\leq 2\delta\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2,1}}.\end{split}(2.47)

Using the basic inequality|eA−eB|≤|A−B|​max⁡(|eA|,|eB|)|\mathrm{e}^{A}-\mathrm{e}^{B}|\leq|A-B|\max(|\mathrm{e}^{A}|,|\mathrm{e}^{B}|)for complex exponents, we can bound the difference of the exponential terms byC1​(δ)​‖q−q~‖H2,1C_{1}(\delta)\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2,1}}.

Using the integral equation (2.33), we can express the difference of the Jost solutions via the resolvent identity:(Ψ1−−ei2​m−​(x)​e1)−(Ψ~1−−ei2​m~−​(x)​e1)=(I−K)−1​(μ​e2−μ~​e2)+(I−K)−1​(K−K~)​(I−K~)−1​μ~​e2,\begin{split}(\Psi_{1}^{-}-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}e_{1})-(\tilde{\Psi}_{1}^{-}-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\tilde{m}_{-}(x)}e_{1})&=(I-K)^{-1}(\mu e_{2}-\tilde{\mu}e_{2})+(I-K)^{-1}(K-\tilde{K})(I-\tilde{K})^{-1}\tilde{\mu}e_{2},\end{split}(2.48)

whereK~\tilde{K}andμ~\tilde{\mu}denote the same quantities asKKandμ\mubut withqqreplaced byq~\tilde{q}.

To estimate the first term on the right-hand side of (2.48), we analyze the differenceh​(x;z)−h~​(x;z)=∫−∞xe−2​i​z​(x−y)​(w​(y)−w~​(y))​𝑑yh(x;z)-\tilde{h}(x;z)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}(w(y)-\tilde{w}(y))dy. The termw​(y)−w~​(y)w(y)-\tilde{w}(y)is given by:w​(y)−w~​(y)=(qy​y+i2​qy2​(qP​T)y)​ei2​m−​(y)−(q~y​y+i2​q~y2​(q~P​T)y)​ei2​m~−​(y).w(y)-\tilde{w}(y)=\left(q_{yy}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}q_{y}^{2}(q^{PT})_{y}\right)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(y)}-\left(\tilde{q}_{yy}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\tilde{q}_{y}^{2}(\tilde{q}^{PT})_{y}\right)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\tilde{m}_{-}(y)}.(2.49)

Utilizing (2.47) and the Sobolev embedding, we can deduce that‖w−w~‖L2,1≤C2​(δ)​‖q−q~‖H2,1\|w-\tilde{w}\|_{L^{2,1}}\leq C_{2}(\delta)\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2,1}}. Then, applying the estimate (2.30), we obtain for everyx0∈ℝ−x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{-}:supx∈(−∞,x0)‖⟨x⟩​[μ​(x;⋅)−μ~​(x;⋅)]‖Lz2​(ℝ)≤π​C2​(δ)​‖q−q~‖H2,1.\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\|\langle x\rangle[\mu(x;\cdot)-\tilde{\mu}(x;\cdot)]\|_{L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\sqrt{\pi}\,C_{2}(\delta)\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2,1}}.(2.50)

For the second term in (2.48), we note thatKKis a Lipschitz continuous operator fromLx∞​(ℝ;Lz2​(ℝ))L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}))to itself. The difference of the integral kernels is bounded by‖Q~−Q~~‖L1≤C3​(δ)​‖q−q~‖H2,1\|\tilde{Q}-\tilde{\tilde{Q}}\|_{L^{1}}\leq C_{3}(\delta)\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2,1}}. Thus, for anyf∈Lx∞​(ℝ;Lz2​(ℝ))f\in L_{x}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})), we have:‖(K−K~)​f‖Lx∞​Lz2≤C3​(δ)​‖q−q~‖H2,1​‖f‖Lx∞​Lz2.\|(K-\tilde{K})f\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}L_{z}^{2}}\leq C_{3}(\delta)\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2,1}}\|f\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}L_{z}^{2}}.(2.51)

Combining these estimates with the uniform boundedness of the inverse operators(I−K)−1(I-K)^{-1}and(I−K~)−1(I-\tilde{K})^{-1}from (2.37), we derive that:supx∈(−∞,x0)‖⟨x⟩​(Ψ1−​(x;⋅)−ei2​m−​(x)​e1−Ψ~1−​(x;⋅)+ei2​m~−​(x)​e1)‖Lz2​(ℝ)≤C4​(δ)​‖q−q~‖H2,1.\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\|\langle x\rangle(\Psi_{1}^{-}(x;\cdot)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}e_{1}-\tilde{\Psi}_{1}^{-}(x;\cdot)+\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\tilde{m}_{-}(x)}e_{1})\|_{L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C_{4}(\delta)\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2,1}}.(2.52)

The bound for thezz-derivative inHz1​(ℝ)H^{1}_{z}(\mathbb{R})follows from applying the identical difference scheme to the differentiated integral equation (2.39). Combining these results yields the Lipschitz continuity in (2.42).

Finally, the proof for the bound (2.44) involving the asymptotic termsΨ^1±\hat{\Psi}_{1}^{\pm}andΨ^2±\hat{\Psi}_{2}^{\pm}is completed by recalling the same difference analysis on the integral equations to (2.39) and (2.40) previously. ∎

## 2.2Scattering coefficients on theλ\lambda-plane

In this subsection, we introduce the scattering data through the original Jost solutionsψ±​(x;λ)\psi^{\pm}(x;\lambda)associated with (2.4). This is the same point of
view as in the inverse scattering transform on theλ\lambda-plane. The transformation
(2.5) will only be used later as an auxiliary transformation to obtain a
Zakharov–Shabat type problem, but it is not used to define the scattering coefficients.

Throughout this subsection, the time variablettis fixed and suppressed. We denote the continuous spectra byΣλ:={λ∈ℂ:Im⁡λ2=0}=ℝ∪i​ℝ,\Sigma_{\lambda}:=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:\operatorname{Im}\lambda^{2}=0\}=\mathbb{R}\cup\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R},(2.53)

and set (see Fig.2(left))D±:={λ∈ℂ:±Im⁡λ2>0}.D_{\pm}:=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:\pm\operatorname{Im}\lambda^{2}>0\}.(2.54)

From the estimate of[15], we can get the following proposition, which we omit the proof:

## Proposition 2.6.

Letq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), denote the Jost functionψ±​(x;λ)=(ψ1±​(x;λ),ψ2±​(x;λ)),\psi^{\pm}(x;\lambda)=\bigl(\psi_{1}^{\pm}(x;\lambda),\psi_{2}^{\pm}(x;\lambda)\bigr),whereψj±​(x;λ)​(j=1,2)\psi_{j}^{\pm}(x;\lambda)\,(j=1,2)denotes the column vectors. Then the integral equation (2.4) admits the unique solutionsψ±​(x,t;λ)\psi^{\pm}(x,t;\lambda). Moreover,ψ1±​(x;λ),ψ2∓​(x;λ)\psi_{1}^{\pm}(x;\lambda),\,\psi_{2}^{\mp}(x;\lambda)are analytic inD±D_{\pm}.

Analytic properties of the Jost solutionsψ±\psi^{\pm}for everyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}are summarized in the following result. The result is a corollary of Proposition2.4.

## Corollary 2.7.

Ifq0​(x)∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q_{0}(x)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), then for everyx∈ℝ±x\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm}, the Jost solutionsψ±\psi^{\pm}have the following propertiesψ11±−ei2​m±​(x),2​λ​ψ21±−qx​ei2​m±​(x),λ−1​ψ21±∈Hλ1​(ℝ),\psi_{11}^{\pm}-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)},\ 2\lambda\psi_{21}^{\pm}-q_{x}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)},\ \lambda^{-1}\psi_{21}^{\pm}\in H_{\lambda}^{1}(\mathbb{R}),(2.55)

andψ22±−e−i2​m±​(x),λ−1​ψ12±∈Hλ1​(ℝ).\psi_{22}^{\pm}-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)},\ \lambda^{-1}\psi_{12}^{\pm}\in H_{\lambda}^{1}(\mathbb{R}).(2.56)

## Proof.

We still only prove the statement forψ1±\psi_{1}^{\pm}. The proof for other Jost solutions is analogous. From the first column of the transformation (2.5), we haveΨ11±​(x;z)\displaystyle\Psi_{11}^{\pm}(x;z)=ψ11±​(x;λ),\displaystyle=\psi_{11}^{\pm}(x;\lambda),(2.57)Ψ21±​(x;z)\displaystyle\Psi_{21}^{\pm}(x;z)=qx​ψ11±​(x;λ)−2​λ​ψ21±​(x;λ).\displaystyle=q_{x}\psi_{11}^{\pm}(x;\lambda)-2\lambda\psi_{21}^{\pm}(x;\lambda).

Recalling the result (2.31), we can obtainψ11±​(x;λ)−ei2​m±​(x)=Ψ11±​(x;z)−ei2​m±​(x)∈Hz1​(ℝ).\psi_{11}^{\pm}(x;\lambda)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)}=\Psi_{11}^{\pm}(x;z)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)}\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}).

Noting thatqx​(x)∈L∞​(ℝ)q_{x}(x)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})andΨ21±​(x;z)∈Hz1​(ℝ)\Psi_{21}^{\pm}(x;z)\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}), we find2​λ​ψ21±​(x;λ)−qx​ei2​m±​(x)=−Ψ21±​(x;z)+qx​(ψ11±​(x;λ)−ei2​m±​(x))∈Hz1​(ℝ).2\lambda\psi_{21}^{\pm}(x;\lambda)-q_{x}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)}=-\Psi_{21}^{\pm}(x;z)+q_{x}(\psi_{11}^{\pm}(x;\lambda)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)})\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}).

To proveλ−1​ψ21±∈Hz1​(ℝ)\lambda^{-1}\psi_{21}^{\pm}\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}), we write explicitly from the integral equation (2.4)λ−1​ψ21±​(x;λ)=i​∫±∞xe−2​i​z​(x−y)​qy​ei2​m±​(y)​𝑑y+∫±∞xe−2​i​z​(x−y)​qy​(ψ11±−ei2​m±​(y))​𝑑y.\begin{split}\lambda^{-1}\psi_{21}^{\pm}(x;\lambda)&=\mathrm{i}\int_{\pm\infty}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}q_{y}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(y)}\,dy+\int_{\pm\infty}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}q_{y}(\psi_{11}^{\pm}-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(y)})\,dy.\end{split}(2.58)

Due toq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})andψ11±​(x;λ)−ei2​m±​(x)∈Hz1​(ℝ)\psi_{11}^{\pm}(x;\lambda)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)}\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}), we can use Proposition2.3in the same way as it was used in the proof of Proposition2.4. Then, we can obtainλ−1​ψ21±​(x;λ)∈Hz1​(ℝ)\lambda^{-1}\psi_{21}^{\pm}(x;\lambda)\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})for everyx∈ℝ±x\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm}. The proof ofψ2​(x;λ)\psi_{2}(x;\lambda)is similar. ∎

Sinceψ±​(x;λ)​ei​λ2​x​σ3\psi^{\pm}(x;\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}x\sigma_{3}}are two fundamental matrix solutions of the original spectral problem, they are linearly dependent forλ∈Σλ\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda}. Hence there exists a scattering matrixS​(λ)S(\lambda)such thatψ−​(x;λ)=ψ+​(x;λ)​ei​λ2​x​σ^3​S​(λ),S​(λ)=(a​(λ)b˘​(λ)b​(λ)a˘​(λ)),λ∈Σλ.\psi^{-}(x;\lambda)=\psi^{+}(x;\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}x\,\hat{\sigma}_{3}}S(\lambda),\quad S(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}a(\lambda)&\breve{b}(\lambda)\vskip 4.33601pt\\
b(\lambda)&\breve{a}(\lambda)\end{pmatrix},\qquad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda}.(2.59)

Equivalently,S​(λ)=e−i​λ2​x​σ^3​(ψ+​(x;λ))−1​ψ−​(x;λ),S(\lambda)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}x\,\hat{\sigma}_{3}}\bigl(\psi^{+}(x;\lambda)\bigr)^{-1}\psi^{-}(x;\lambda),(2.60)

and the right-hand side is independent ofxx. In particular, by takingx=0x=0, we haveS​(λ)=(ψ+​(0;λ))−1​ψ−​(0;λ).S(\lambda)=\bigl(\psi^{+}(0;\lambda)\bigr)^{-1}\psi^{-}(0;\lambda).

Sincetr⁡(Qx)=0\operatorname{tr}(Q_{x})=0, it follows from (2.2) and the normalizationψ±​(x;λ)→I\psi^{\pm}(x;\lambda)\to Iasx→±∞x\to\pm\inftythatdetψ±​(x;λ)=1.\displaystyle\det\psi^{\pm}(x;\lambda)=1.(2.61)

ThereforedetS​(λ)=a​(λ)​a˘​(λ)−b​(λ)​b˘​(λ)=1.\det S(\lambda)=a(\lambda)\breve{a}(\lambda)-b(\lambda)\breve{b}(\lambda)=1.(2.62)

Using (2.59), the scattering coefficients can be written in terms
of Wronskians asa​(λ)\displaystyle a(\lambda)=Wr​(ψ1−​(x;λ),ψ2+​(x;λ))=Wr​(ψ1−​(0;λ),ψ2+​(0;λ)),\displaystyle=\mathrm{Wr}\bigl(\psi_{1}^{-}(x;\lambda),\psi_{2}^{+}(x;\lambda)\bigr)=\mathrm{Wr}\bigl(\psi_{1}^{-}(0;\lambda),\psi_{2}^{+}(0;\lambda)\bigr),(2.63)a˘​(λ)\displaystyle\breve{a}(\lambda)=Wr​(ψ1+​(x;λ),ψ2−​(x;λ))=Wr​(ψ1+​(0;λ),ψ2−​(0;λ)),\displaystyle=\mathrm{Wr}\bigl(\psi_{1}^{+}(x;\lambda),\psi_{2}^{-}(x;\lambda)\bigr)=\mathrm{Wr}\bigl(\psi_{1}^{+}(0;\lambda),\psi_{2}^{-}(0;\lambda)\bigr),(2.64)b​(λ)\displaystyle b(\lambda)=e2​i​λ2​x​Wr​(ψ1+​(x;λ),ψ1−​(x;λ))=Wr​(ψ1+​(0;λ),ψ1−​(0;λ)),\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}x}\mathrm{Wr}\bigl(\psi_{1}^{+}(x;\lambda),\psi_{1}^{-}(x;\lambda)\bigr)=\mathrm{Wr}\bigl(\psi_{1}^{+}(0;\lambda),\psi_{1}^{-}(0;\lambda)\bigr),(2.65)b˘​(λ)\displaystyle\breve{b}(\lambda)=e−2​i​λ2​x​Wr​(ψ2−​(x;λ),ψ2+​(x;λ))=Wr​(ψ2−​(0;λ),ψ2+​(0;λ)).\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}x}\mathrm{Wr}\bigl(\psi_{2}^{-}(x;\lambda),\psi_{2}^{+}(x;\lambda)\bigr)=\mathrm{Wr}\bigl(\psi_{2}^{-}(0;\lambda),\psi_{2}^{+}(0;\lambda)\bigr).(2.66)

We define the reflection coefficients byr​(λ):=b​(λ)a​(λ),r˘​(λ):=b˘​(λ)a˘​(λ),λ∈Σλ.r(\lambda):=\dfrac{b(\lambda)}{a(\lambda)},\quad\breve{r}(\lambda):=\dfrac{\breve{b}(\lambda)}{\breve{a}(\lambda)},\quad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda}.(2.67)

Now we note the symmetry on solutions to the first linear equation of (2.2). By using the boundary conditions for the normalized Jost solutions, we obtain the following resultsψ+​(x,t;λ)=σ1​ψ−​(−x,−t;λ)​σ1,ψ+​(x,t;λ)=σ3​ψ−​(x,t;−λ)​σ3.\psi^{+}(x,t;\lambda)=\sigma_{1}\psi^{-}(-x,-t;\lambda)\sigma_{1},\quad\psi^{+}(x,t;\lambda)=\sigma_{3}\psi^{-}(x,t;-\lambda)\sigma_{3}.(2.68)

whereσ1\sigma_{1}is Pauli matrix. As usual, the direct scattering transform is based ont=0t=0, the scattering coefficients have the resultsS​(λ;t)=σ3​S​(−λ;t)​σ3,S​(λ;t)=σ1​S−1​(λ;−t)​σ1,λ∈Σλ.S(\lambda;t)=\sigma_{3}S(-\lambda;t)\sigma_{3},\quad S(\lambda;t)=\sigma_{1}S^{-1}(\lambda;-t)\sigma_{1},\quad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda}.(2.69)

which means that whent=0t=0, we havea​(λ)\displaystyle a(\lambda)=a​(−λ),a˘​(λ)=a˘​(−λ),λ∈Σλ,\displaystyle=a(-\lambda),\qquad\breve{a}(\lambda)=\breve{a}(-\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda},(2.70)b​(λ)\displaystyle b(\lambda)=−b​(−λ),b˘​(λ)=−b˘​(−λ),b˘​(λ)=−b​(λ),λ∈Σλ,\displaystyle=-b(-\lambda),\quad\breve{b}(\lambda)=-\breve{b}(-\lambda),\quad\breve{b}(\lambda)=-b(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda},(2.71)r​(λ)\displaystyle r(\lambda)=−r​(−λ),r˘​(λ)=−r˘​(−λ),λ∈Σλ.\displaystyle=-r(-\lambda),\quad\breve{r}(\lambda)=-\breve{r}(-\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda}.(2.72)

We first consider the properties of them about|λ|→∞|\lambda|\to\inftyand we have the following proposition:

## Proposition 2.8.

Ifq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), then the functiona​(λ)a(\lambda)anda˘​(λ)\breve{a}(\lambda)are analytically inℂ−\mathbb{C}^{-}andℂ+\mathbb{C}^{+}with respect toz=λ2z=\lambda^{2}, respectively.
