witha∞=exp⁡(i2​∫ℝqy​(y)​(qP​T)y​(y)​𝑑y),a˘∞=exp⁡(−i2​∫ℝqy​(y)​(qP​T)y​(y)​𝑑y),\displaystyle a_{\infty}=\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}q_{y}(y)(q^{PT})_{y}(y)dy\right),\qquad\breve{a}_{\infty}=\exp\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}q_{y}(y)(q^{PT})_{y}(y)dy\right),(2.74)

andλ​b​(λ),λ−1​b​(λ),λ​b˘​(λ),λ−1​b˘​(λ)∈Lz2,1​(ℝ).\ \lambda b(\lambda),\ \lambda^{-1}b(\lambda),\ \lambda\breve{b}(\lambda),\ \lambda^{-1}\breve{b}(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}).(2.75)

## Proof.

Analytical proofs can be completed using the Wronskians determinant representation. And we only prove the results fora​(λ)a(\lambda)andb​(λ)b(\lambda). The proof of others is similar. In fact, the integration ofa​(λ)a(\lambda)is as follows:a​(λ)=1+i​λ​∫ℝ(qP​T)y​(y)​ψ21−​(y;λ)​𝑑y=1+i2​∫ℝqy​(y)​(qP​T)y​(y)​ψ11−​(y;λ)​𝑑y−i2​∫ℝ(qP​T)y​(y)​Ψ21−​(y;z)​𝑑y,\begin{split}a(\lambda)&=1+\mathrm{i}\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}}(q^{PT})_{y}(y)\psi_{21}^{-}(y;\lambda)\,dy\\
&=1+\dfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}q_{y}(y)(q^{PT})_{y}(y)\psi_{11}^{-}(y;\lambda)\,dy-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(q^{PT})_{y}(y)\Psi_{21}^{-}(y;z)\,dy,\end{split}(2.76)

which meanslim|λ|→∞a​(λ)=a∞=exp⁡(i2​∫ℝqy​(y)​(qP​T)y​(y)​𝑑y).\lim_{|\lambda|\to\infty}a(\lambda)=a_{\infty}=\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}q_{y}(y)(q^{PT})_{y}(y)dy\right).(2.77)

To prove thata​(λ)−a∞∈Hz1​(ℝ)a(\lambda)-a_{\infty}\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}), we add and subtract the asymptotic limits of the Jost solutions obtained in Corollary2.7. LetL11±=ei2​m±​(0)L_{11}^{\pm}=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(0)}andL22±=e−i2​m±​(0)L_{22}^{\pm}=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(0)}. We can factora​(λ)a(\lambda)as:a​(λ)−a∞=(ψ11−​(0;λ)−L11−)​(ψ22+​(0;λ)−L22+)+L11−​(ψ22+​(0;λ)−L22+)+L22+​(ψ11−​(0;λ)−L11−)+L11−​L22+−ψ21−​(0;λ)​ψ12+​(0;λ).\begin{split}a(\lambda)-a_{\infty}&=(\psi_{11}^{-}(0;\lambda)-L_{11}^{-})(\psi_{22}^{+}(0;\lambda)-L_{22}^{+})+L_{11}^{-}(\psi_{22}^{+}(0;\lambda)-L_{22}^{+})\vskip 4.33601pt\\
&\qquad+L_{22}^{+}(\psi_{11}^{-}(0;\lambda)-L_{11}^{-})+L_{11}^{-}L_{22}^{+}-\psi_{21}^{-}(0;\lambda)\psi_{12}^{+}(0;\lambda).\end{split}(2.78)

Note that the constant term evaluates exactly to the asymptotic limit:L11−​L22+=ei2​(m−​(0)−m+​(0))=a∞L_{11}^{-}L_{22}^{+}=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(m_{-}(0)-m_{+}(0))}=a_{\infty}. By Corollary2.7, functions likeψ11−−L11−\psi_{11}^{-}-L_{11}^{-},ψ22+−L22+\psi_{22}^{+}-L_{22}^{+}, andλ−1​ψ12+\lambda^{-1}\psi_{12}^{+}all belong toHz1​(ℝ)H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}). Since2​λ​ψ21−=(2​λ​ψ21−−qx​(0)​L11−)+qx​(0)​L11−∈Hz1​(ℝ)⊕ℂ2\lambda\psi_{21}^{-}=(2\lambda\psi_{21}^{-}-q_{x}(0)L_{11}^{-})+q_{x}(0)L_{11}^{-}\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathbb{C}, andHz1​(ℝ)H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})is a Banach algebra, their sums and products remain inHz1​(ℝ)H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}). Thus,a​(λ)−a∞∈Hz1​(ℝ)a(\lambda)-a_{\infty}\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}).

Similarly, we haveλ−1​b​(λ)=ψ11+​(0;λ)​(λ−1​ψ21−​(0;λ))−(λ−1​ψ21+​(0;λ))​ψ11−​(0;λ).\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)=\psi_{11}^{+}(0;\lambda)(\lambda^{-1}\psi_{21}^{-}(0;\lambda))-(\lambda^{-1}\psi_{21}^{+}(0;\lambda))\psi_{11}^{-}(0;\lambda).(2.79)

Sinceλ−1​ψ21±∈Hz1​(ℝ)\lambda^{-1}\psi_{21}^{\pm}\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})andψ11±\psi_{11}^{\pm}differ from constants by anHz1​(ℝ)H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})function, we immediately obtainλ−1​b​(λ)∈Hz1​(ℝ)\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}).

Similarly, to establish the relation for2​λ​b​(λ)2\lambda b(\lambda)in terms of the components ofΨ±​(0;z)\Psi^{\pm}(0;z), we evaluate the Wronskian (2.65) atx=0x=0:2​λ​b​(λ)=ψ11+​(0;λ)​(2​λ​ψ21−​(0;λ))−(2​λ​ψ21+​(0;λ))​ψ11−​(0;λ).2\lambda b(\lambda)=\psi_{11}^{+}(0;\lambda)(2\lambda\psi_{21}^{-}(0;\lambda))-(2\lambda\psi_{21}^{+}(0;\lambda))\psi_{11}^{-}(0;\lambda).(2.80)

Substituting the component relations from the transformation (2.5), namely2​λ​ψ21±​(0;λ)=qx​(0)​Ψ11±​(0;z)−Ψ21±​(0;z)2\lambda\psi_{21}^{\pm}(0;\lambda)=q_{x}(0)\Psi_{11}^{\pm}(0;z)-\Psi_{21}^{\pm}(0;z), we obtain:2​λ​b​(λ)=Ψ11+​(0;z)​[qx​(0)​Ψ11−​(0;z)−Ψ21−​(0;z)]−[qx​(0)​Ψ11+​(0;z)−Ψ21+​(0;z)]​Ψ11−​(0;z)=Ψ21+​(0;z)​Ψ11−​(0;z)−Ψ11+​(0;z)​Ψ21−​(0;z).\begin{split}2\lambda b(\lambda)&=\Psi_{11}^{+}(0;z)\left[q_{x}(0)\Psi_{11}^{-}(0;z)-\Psi_{21}^{-}(0;z)\right]-\left[q_{x}(0)\Psi_{11}^{+}(0;z)-\Psi_{21}^{+}(0;z)\right]\Psi_{11}^{-}(0;z)\vskip 4.33601pt\\
&=\Psi_{21}^{+}(0;z)\Psi_{11}^{-}(0;z)-\Psi_{11}^{+}(0;z)\Psi_{21}^{-}(0;z).\end{split}(2.81)

The remaining terms are combinations ofHz1​(ℝ)H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})functions, proving thatλ​b​(λ)∈Hz1​(ℝ)\lambda b(\lambda)\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}).

Sincez​λ−1​b​(λ)=λ​b​(λ)∈Hz1​(ℝ)z\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)=\lambda b(\lambda)\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}), we haveλ−1​b​(λ)∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}). In addition, to proveλ​b​(λ)∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)\lambda b(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), we multiply equation (2.81) byzzand write the resulting equation in the form2​λ​z​b​(λ)=−Ψ11+​(0;z)​(z​Ψ21−​(0;z)−s2−​(0))+Ψ11−​(0;z)​(z​Ψ21+​(0;z)−s2+​(0))−s2−​(0)​(Ψ11+​(0;z)−ei2​m+​(0))+s2+​(0)​(Ψ11−​(0;z)−ei2​m−​(0)),\begin{split}2\lambda zb(\lambda)&=-\Psi_{11}^{+}(0;z)\left(z\Psi_{21}^{-}(0;z)-s_{2}^{-}(0)\right)+\Psi_{11}^{-}(0;z)\left(z\Psi_{21}^{+}(0;z)-s_{2}^{+}(0)\right)\vskip 4.33601pt\\
&\qquad-s_{2}^{-}(0)\left(\Psi_{11}^{+}(0;z)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(0)}\right)+s_{2}^{+}(0)\left(\Psi_{11}^{-}(0;z)-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(0)}\right),\end{split}(2.82)

where we have used the identitys2−​(0)​ei2​m+​(0)−s2+​(0)​ei2​m−​(0)=0s_{2}^{-}(0)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(0)}-s_{2}^{+}(0)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(0)}=0, which is obtained from the limits in (2.17).
By (2.31) and (2.32), each term in the representation (2.82) is inLz2​(ℝ)L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}). Therefore, we deriveλ​b​(λ)∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)\lambda b(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}). ∎

Next we record the behavior of the scattering coefficients atλ=0\lambda=0. We seek the
expansion of the original Jost functions in the formψ±​(x;λ)=I+λ​U1​(x)+λ2​U2±​(x)+𝒪​(λ3),λ→0.\psi^{\pm}(x;\lambda)=I+\lambda U_{1}(x)+\lambda^{2}U_{2}^{\pm}(x)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3}),\qquad\lambda\to 0.(2.83)

Substituting (2.83) into thexx-part of
(2.2) and comparing coefficients of the same powers ofλ\lambda, we obtainU1,x=i​Qx,U2,x±=−Qx​Q.U_{1,x}=\mathrm{i}Q_{x},\qquad U_{2,x}^{\pm}=-Q_{x}Q.

Using the normalization ofψ±\psi^{\pm}at±∞\pm\infty, we getU1​(x)=i​Q​(x),U2±​(x)=−∫±∞xQy​(y)​Q​(y)​𝑑y.U_{1}(x)=\mathrm{i}Q(x),\qquad U_{2}^{\pm}(x)=-\int_{\pm\infty}^{x}Q_{y}(y)Q(y)\,dy.(2.84)

Thereforeψ±​(x;λ)=I+i​λ​Q​(x)−λ2​∫±∞xQy​(y)​Q​(y)​𝑑y+𝒪​(λ3),λ→0.\psi^{\pm}(x;\lambda)=I+\mathrm{i}\lambda Q(x)-\lambda^{2}\int_{\pm\infty}^{x}Q_{y}(y)Q(y)\,dy+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3}),\qquad\lambda\to 0.(2.85)

Takingx=0x=0in (2.60), we obtainS​(λ)=I−λ2​∫−∞+∞Qy​(y)​Q​(y)​𝑑y+𝒪​(λ3),λ→0.S(\lambda)=I-\lambda^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}Q_{y}(y)Q(y)\,dy+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3}),\qquad\lambda\to 0.(2.86)

SinceQy​Q=((qP​T)y​q00qy​qP​T),Q_{y}Q=\begin{pmatrix}(q^{PT})_{y}q&0\vskip 4.33601pt\\
0&q_{y}q^{PT}\end{pmatrix},

we havea​(λ)\displaystyle a(\lambda)=1−λ2​∫−∞+∞(qP​T)y​(y)​q​(y)​𝑑y+𝒪​(λ4),\displaystyle=1-\lambda^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}(q^{PT})_{y}(y)q(y)\,dy+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{4}),(2.87)a˘​(λ)\displaystyle\breve{a}(\lambda)=1−λ2​∫−∞+∞qy​(y)​qP​T​(y)​𝑑y+𝒪​(λ4),\displaystyle=1-\lambda^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}q_{y}(y)q^{PT}(y)\,dy+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{4}),(2.88)b​(λ)\displaystyle b(\lambda)=𝒪​(λ3),b˘​(λ)=𝒪​(λ3),λ→0.\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3}),\qquad\breve{b}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3}),\qquad\lambda\to 0.(2.89)

where we use (2.70), (2.72). In particular, the scattering coefficients are regular atλ=0\lambda=0.

We also need the behavior of the scattering coefficients as|λ|→∞|\lambda|\to\infty. LetM:=∫−∞+∞qy​(y)​(qP​T)y​(y)​𝑑y.M:=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}q_{y}(y)(q^{PT})_{y}(y)\,dy.(2.90)

From the large-λ\lambdaasymptotics of the original Jost functions,ψ±​(x;λ)=ei2​m±​(x)​σ3+𝒪​(λ−1),m±​(x):=∫±∞xqy​(y)​(qP​T)y​(y)​𝑑y,\psi^{\pm}(x;\lambda)=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}(x)\sigma_{3}}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-1}),\qquad m_{\pm}(x):=\int_{\pm\infty}^{x}q_{y}(y)(q^{PT})_{y}(y)\,dy,

we obtaina​(λ)\displaystyle a(\lambda)=ei2​M+𝒪​(λ−1),λ∈D−¯,|λ|→∞,\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}M}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-1}),\qquad\lambda\in\overline{D_{-}},\quad|\lambda|\to\infty,(2.91)a˘​(λ)\displaystyle\breve{a}(\lambda)=e−i2​M+𝒪​(λ−1),λ∈D+¯,|λ|→∞,\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}M}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-1}),\qquad\lambda\in\overline{D_{+}},\quad|\lambda|\to\infty,(2.92)b​(λ)\displaystyle b(\lambda)=𝒪​(λ−1),b˘​(λ)=𝒪​(λ−1),λ∈Σλ,|λ|→∞.\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-1}),\qquad\breve{b}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-1}),\qquad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda},\quad|\lambda|\to\infty.(2.93)

For the convenience of the subsequent analysis, we provide here a sufficient condition that has no solitons and no spectral singularities. And in the subsequent discussions, we always assume no isolated vertices and no spectral singularities.

## Proposition 2.9.

Letq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}). Thenz−1​λ−1​b​(λ)∈Hz1​(ℝ),z−1​λ−1​b˘​(λ)∈Hz1​(ℝ).z^{-1}\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}),\qquad z^{-1}\lambda^{-1}\breve{b}(\lambda)\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}).(2.94)

## Proof.

We only prove the statement forb​(λ)b(\lambda), since the proof forb˘​(λ)\breve{b}(\lambda)is analogous. From the Volterra integral equation
(2.4) and the scattering relation
(2.59), the scattering coefficientb​(λ)b(\lambda)admits the representationλ−1​b​(λ)=i​∫ℝqy​(y)​ψ11−​(y;λ)​e2​i​z​y​𝑑y.\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)=\mathrm{i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}q_{y}(y)\psi_{11}^{-}(y;\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zy}\,dy.(2.95)

Moreover, the first column ofψ−\psi^{-}satisfiesψ11−​(x;λ)\displaystyle\psi_{11}^{-}(x;\lambda)=1+i​λ​∫−∞x(qP​T)y​(y)​ψ21−​(y;λ)​𝑑y,\displaystyle=1+\mathrm{i}\lambda\int_{-\infty}^{x}(q^{PT})_{y}(y)\psi_{21}^{-}(y;\lambda)\,dy,(2.96)ψ21−​(x;λ)\displaystyle\psi_{21}^{-}(x;\lambda)=i​λ​∫−∞xe−2​i​z​(x−y)​qy​(y)​ψ11−​(y;λ)​𝑑y.\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\lambda\int_{-\infty}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(x-y)}q_{y}(y)\psi_{11}^{-}(y;\lambda)\,dy.(2.97)

Substituting (2.97) into
(2.96), we obtainψ11−​(x;λ)−1=−z​(𝒯​ψ11−)​(x;z),\psi_{11}^{-}(x;\lambda)-1=-z(\mathcal{T}\psi_{11}^{-})(x;z),(2.98)

where the integral operator𝒯\mathcal{T}is defined by(𝒯​f)​(x;z):=∫−∞x(qP​T)y​(y)​∫−∞ye−2​i​z​(y−s)​qs​(s)​f​(s;z)​𝑑s​𝑑y.(\mathcal{T}f)(x;z):=\int_{-\infty}^{x}(q^{PT})_{y}(y)\int_{-\infty}^{y}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}z(y-s)}q_{s}(s)f(s;z)\,ds\,dy.(2.99)

Sinceq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), we have(qP​T)x,qx∈L1​(ℝ)∩L2​(ℝ)(q^{PT})_{x},q_{x}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).
Hence‖𝒯‖L∞→L∞≤‖(qP​T)x‖L1​‖qx‖L1.\|\mathcal{T}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{\infty}}\leq\|(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{1}}\|q_{x}\|_{L^{1}}.(2.100)

Using (2.95) and
(2.98), we getλ−1​b​(λ)\displaystyle\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)=i​∫ℝqy​(y)​(ψ11−​(y;λ)−1)​e2​i​z​y​𝑑y+i​∫ℝqy​(y)​e2​i​z​y​𝑑y\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}q_{y}(y)\bigl(\psi_{11}^{-}(y;\lambda)-1\bigr)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zy}\,dy+\mathrm{i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}q_{y}(y)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zy}\,dy=−i​z​∫ℝqy​(y)​(𝒯​ψ11−)​(y;z)​e2​i​z​y​𝑑y+2​z​∫ℝq​(y)​e2​i​z​y​𝑑y.\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}z\int_{\mathbb{R}}q_{y}(y)(\mathcal{T}\psi_{11}^{-})(y;z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zy}\,dy+2z\int_{\mathbb{R}}q(y)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zy}\,dy.(2.101)

Therefore,z−1​λ−1​b​(λ)=−i​∫ℝqy​(y)​(𝒯​ψ11−)​(y;z)​e2​i​z​y​𝑑y+2​∫ℝq​(y)​e2​i​z​y​𝑑y.z^{-1}\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)=-\mathrm{i}\int_{\mathbb{R}}q_{y}(y)(\mathcal{T}\psi_{11}^{-})(y;z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zy}\,dy+2\int_{\mathbb{R}}q(y)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zy}\,dy.(2.102)

By the Plancherel’s theorem and the weighted Fourier estimate, together
with the uniform boundedness ofψ11−\psi_{11}^{-}, we have‖z−1​λ−1​b​(λ)‖Hz1\displaystyle\|z^{-1}\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)\|_{H_{z}^{1}}≤C​‖qx​𝒯​ψ11−‖L2,1+C​‖q‖L2,1\displaystyle\leq C\|q_{x}\mathcal{T}\psi_{11}^{-}\|_{L^{2,1}}+C\|q\|_{L^{2,1}}≤C​‖𝒯‖L∞→L∞​supx∈ℝ‖ψ11−​(⋅;λ)‖Lz∞​‖qx‖L2,1+C​‖q‖L2,1\displaystyle\leq C\|\mathcal{T}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{\infty}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\|\psi_{11}^{-}(\cdot;\lambda)\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\|q_{x}\|_{L^{2,1}}+C\|q\|_{L^{2,1}}≤C​(‖q‖H3∩H2,1),\displaystyle\leq C\bigl(\|q\|_{H^{3}\cap H^{2,1}}\bigr),(2.103)

which provesz−1​λ−1​b​(λ)∈Hz1​(ℝ)z^{-1}\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}).
The proof forb˘​(λ)\breve{b}(\lambda)follows in the same way from the
second column Volterra equation, or equivalently from the symmetryb˘​(λ)=−b​(λ)\breve{b}(\lambda)=-b(\lambda)att=0t=0. ∎

In conclusion, we can get the following corollary, which is the same as Corollary2.5.

## Corollary 2.10.

Ifq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), then the mapH2,1​(ℝ)∋q→[a​(λ)−a∞,a˘​(λ)−a˘∞,λ​b​(λ),λ​b˘​(λ),λ−1​b​(λ),λ−1​b˘​(λ)]∈Hz1​(ℝ)H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\ni q\to[a(\lambda)-a_{\infty},\ \breve{a}(\lambda)-\breve{a}_{\infty},\ \lambda b(\lambda),\ \lambda\breve{b}(\lambda),\ \lambda^{-1}b(\lambda),\ \lambda^{-1}\breve{b}(\lambda)]\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})(2.104)

is Lipschitz continuous. Moreover, the mapH3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)∋q\displaystyle H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\ni q→[λ​b​(λ),λ​b˘​(λ),λ−1​b​(λ),λ−1​b˘​(λ)]∈Lz2,1​(ℝ),\displaystyle\to[\lambda b(\lambda),\ \lambda\breve{b}(\lambda),\ \lambda^{-1}b(\lambda),\ \lambda^{-1}\breve{b}(\lambda)]\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}),(2.105)

is Lipschitz continuous.

The proof of this corollary is directly from Proposition2.8and Corollary2.5.

## 3Construction of the Riemann-Hilbert problems

## 3.1A basic RH problem on theλ\lambda-plane

From (2.67), (2.87)–(2.89) and (2.91)–(2.93), we can get thatr​(0)=r˘​(0)=0r(0)=\breve{r}(0)=0. Now we define the matrix functionΦ​(x;λ):=e−i2​m+​(x)​σ3​{(ψ1−​(x;λ)a​(λ),ψ2+​(x;λ)),λ∈D−,(ψ1+​(x;λ),ψ2−​(x;λ)a˘​(λ)),λ∈D+,\Phi(x;\lambda):=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(x)\sigma_{3}}\begin{cases}\left(\dfrac{\psi_{1}^{-}(x;\lambda)}{a(\lambda)},\,\psi_{2}^{+}(x;\lambda)\right),\quad&\lambda\in D_{-},\\[11.99998pt]
\left(\psi_{1}^{+}(x;\lambda),\,\dfrac{\psi_{2}^{-}(x;\lambda)}{\breve{a}(\lambda)}\right),\quad&\lambda\in D_{+},\end{cases}(3.1)

which satisfy the following RH problem:

## Riemann-Hilbert Problem 3.1.

Find a matrix functionΦ​(x;λ)\Phi(x;\lambda)with the following properties:
- 1.

Analyticity:Φ​(x;λ)\Phi(x;\lambda)is analytical inℂ∖Σλ\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma_{\lambda}.
- 2.

Jump condition:LetΦ+​(x;λ)\Phi_{+}(x;\lambda)andΦ−​(x;λ)\Phi_{-}(x;\lambda)denote the non-tangential boundary values ofΦ​(x;λ)\Phi(x;\lambda)fromD+D_{+}andD−D_{-}, respectively. ThenΦ+​(x;λ)=Φ−​(x;λ)​(I+V​(x;λ)),λ∈Σλ,\Phi_{+}(x;\lambda)=\Phi_{-}(x;\lambda)(I+V(x;\lambda)),\quad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda},(3.2)

where the jump matrix isV​(x;λ)=(0r˘​(λ)​e2​i​λ2​x−r​(λ)​e−2​i​λ2​x−r​(λ)​r˘​(λ)).V(x;\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}0&\breve{r}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}x}\\[3.99994pt]
-r(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}x}&-r(\lambda)\breve{r}(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}.(3.3)
- 3.

Normalization:Φ​(x;λ)=I+𝒪​(λ−1)\Phi(x;\lambda)=I+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-1})asλ→∞.\lambda\to\infty.

In the nonlocal caseqP​T​(x)=q​(−x)q^{PT}(x)=q(-x), the standard involution symmetry is
broken, and hence the reflection coefficientsr​(λ)r(\lambda)andr˘​(λ)\breve{r}(\lambda)are not necessarily complex conjugates of each other.
Consequently, the jump matrixV​(x;λ)V(x;\lambda)in (3.3) is in
general non-Hermitian. In order to apply the Zhou’s vanishing lemma, we impose
a small-norm condition on the effective Volterra potential.

## Proposition 3.2.

Suppose thatq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}),qP​T​(x)=q​(−x)q^{PT}(x)=q(-x), and satisfies the small-norm restrictionρ:=‖Q^‖L1​(ℝ)<ln⁡(32).\rho:=\|\widehat{Q}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}<\ln\left(\frac{3}{2}\right).(3.4)

Then the reflection coefficients satisfy the uniform bounds|r​(λ)|≤c0<1,|r˘​(λ)|≤c0<1,λ∈Σλ,|r(\lambda)|\leq c_{0}<1,\qquad|\breve{r}(\lambda)|\leq c_{0}<1,\qquad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda},(3.5)

for some positive constantc0c_{0}.

## Proof.

We first prove the estimate forr​(λ)r(\lambda). Let us consider the Zakharov-Shabat type spectral problem (2.7) and its associated Volterra integral equation (2.9).

Forz∈ℝz\in\mathbb{R}, the Jost solutionΨ−​(x;z)\Psi^{-}(x;z)satisfies:Ψ−​(x;z)=I+∫−∞xei​z​(x−y)​σ^3​Q^​(y)​Ψ−​(y;z)​𝑑y.\Psi^{-}(x;z)=I+\int_{-\infty}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(x-y)\hat{\sigma}_{3}}\widehat{Q}(y)\Psi^{-}(y;z)\,dy.(3.6)

According to the classical definition in the inverse scattering transform, the scattering matrixSΨ​(z)S_{\Psi}(z)is associated with the asymptotics of the Jost solution asx→+∞x\to+\infty:SΨ​(z)=limx→+∞e−i​z​x​σ3​Ψ−​(x;z)​ei​z​x​σ3.S_{\Psi}(z)=\lim_{x\to+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}zx\sigma_{3}}\Psi^{-}(x;z)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}zx\sigma_{3}}.(3.7)

Multiplying (3.6) bye−i​z​x​σ3\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}zx\sigma_{3}}from the left andei​z​x​σ3\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}zx\sigma_{3}}from the right, and taking the limitx→+∞x\to+\infty, we get the exact integral representation forSΨ​(z)−IS_{\Psi}(z)-I:SΨ​(z)−I=∫−∞+∞e−i​z​y​σ^3​(Q^​(y)​Ψ−​(y;z))​𝑑y.S_{\Psi}(z)-I=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}zy\hat{\sigma}_{3}}\left(\widehat{Q}(y)\Psi^{-}(y;z)\right)\,dy.(3.8)

To estimateΨ−​(x;z)\Psi^{-}(x;z)in (3.8), we employ the Volterra-Neumann expansion.
LetΨ−​(x;z)=∑n=0∞Ψn​(x;z)\Psi^{-}(x;z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Psi_{n}(x;z), whereΨ0​(x;z)=I\Psi_{0}(x;z)=IandΨn+1​(x;z)=∫−∞xei​z​(x−y)​σ^3​Q^​(y)​Ψn​(y;z)​𝑑y.\Psi_{n+1}(x;z)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(x-y)\hat{\sigma}_{3}}\widehat{Q}(y)\Psi_{n}(y;z)\,dy.(3.9)

Forz∈ℝz\in\mathbb{R}, the matrix norm (defined as the sum ofL1L^{1}norms of entries) is non-increasing under conjugation byei​z​(x−y)​σ3\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(x-y)\sigma_{3}}. Thus,‖ei​z​(x−y)​σ^3​Q^​(y)​Ψn​(y;z)‖≤‖Q^​(y)‖​‖Ψn​(y;z)‖\|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(x-y)\hat{\sigma}_{3}}\widehat{Q}(y)\Psi_{n}(y;z)\|\leq\|\widehat{Q}(y)\|\|\Psi_{n}(y;z)\|. Introducingρ​(x)=∫−∞x‖Q^​(y)‖​𝑑y\rho(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\|\widehat{Q}(y)\|\,dy, we can prove by induction that‖Ψn​(x;z)‖≤1n!​ρ​(x)n\|\Psi_{n}(x;z)\|\leq\frac{1}{n!}\rho(x)^{n}. Summing these norms yields the uniform bound:‖Ψ−​(x;z)‖≤∑n=0∞1n!​ρ​(x)n=eρ​(x).\|\Psi^{-}(x;z)\|\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\rho(x)^{n}=\mathrm{e}^{\rho(x)}.(3.10)

Taking the matrix norm of (3.8) and using (3.10), we obtain‖SΨ​(z)−I‖≤∫−∞+∞‖Q^​(y)‖​‖Ψ−​(y;z)‖​𝑑y≤∫−∞+∞‖Q^​(y)‖​eρ​(y)​𝑑y.\|S_{\Psi}(z)-I\|\leq\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\|\widehat{Q}(y)\|\|\Psi^{-}(y;z)\|\,dy\leq\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\|\widehat{Q}(y)\|\mathrm{e}^{\rho(y)}\,dy.(3.11)

Using the substitutiond​ρ​(y)=‖Q^​(y)‖​d​yd\rho(y)=\|\widehat{Q}(y)\|\,dy, we obtain the uniform global bound:‖SΨ​(z)−I‖≤∫0ρeu​𝑑u=eρ−1,z∈ℝ.\|S_{\Psi}(z)-I\|\leq\int_{0}^{\rho}\mathrm{e}^{u}\,du=\mathrm{e}^{\rho}-1,\qquad z\in\mathbb{R}.(3.12)

This strictly bounds the components ofSΨ​(z)S_{\Psi}(z):|aΨ​(z)−1|≤eρ−1,|a˘Ψ​(z)−1|≤eρ−1,|bΨ​(z)|≤eρ−1,|b˘Ψ​(z)|≤eρ−1.|a_{\Psi}(z)-1|\leq\mathrm{e}^{\rho}-1,\quad|\breve{a}_{\Psi}(z)-1|\leq\mathrm{e}^{\rho}-1,\quad|b_{\Psi}(z)|\leq\mathrm{e}^{\rho}-1,\quad|\breve{b}_{\Psi}(z)|\leq\mathrm{e}^{\rho}-1.(3.13)

From the gauge transformation (2.5), the original scattering coefficients are related to theΨ\Psi-system components bya​(λ)=aΨ​(z)a(\lambda)=a_{\Psi}(z),a˘​(λ)=a˘Ψ​(z)\breve{a}(\lambda)=\breve{a}_{\Psi}(z),b​(λ)=−(2​λ)−1​bΨ​(z)b(\lambda)=-(2\lambda)^{-1}b_{\Psi}(z), andb˘​(λ)=−2​λ​b˘Ψ​(z)\breve{b}(\lambda)=-2\lambda\breve{b}_{\Psi}(z).
Fora​(λ)a(\lambda), we immediately obtain the uniform lower bound:|a​(λ)|≥1−|aΨ​(z)−1|≥2−eρ.|a(\lambda)|\geq 1-|a_{\Psi}(z)-1|\geq 2-\mathrm{e}^{\rho}.(3.14)

To boundb​(λ)b(\lambda)globally and circumvent the spectral singularities atλ=0\lambda=0andλ=∞\lambda=\infty, we critically exploit the involution symmetry of the nonlocal Fokas-Lenells equation.
Underp​(x)=q​(−x)p(x)=q(-x), the scattering data satisfyb˘​(λ)=−b​(λ)\breve{b}(\lambda)=-b(\lambda)forλ∈Σλ\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda}.
This symmetry provides two simultaneous constraints for|b​(λ)||b(\lambda)|:|b​(λ)|\displaystyle|b(\lambda)|=|−12​λ​bΨ​(z)|≤12​|λ|​(eρ−1),\displaystyle=\left|-\frac{1}{2\lambda}b_{\Psi}(z)\right|\leq\frac{1}{2|\lambda|}(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}-1),(3.15)|b​(λ)|\displaystyle|b(\lambda)|=|b˘​(λ)|=|−2​λ​b˘Ψ​(z)|≤2​|λ|​(eρ−1).\displaystyle=|\breve{b}(\lambda)|=\left|-2\lambda\breve{b}_{\Psi}(z)\right|\leq 2|\lambda|(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}-1).(3.16)

Therefore, for allλ∈Σλ\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda},|b​(λ)||b(\lambda)|must be bounded by their minimum:|b​(λ)|≤(eρ−1)​min⁡(2​|λ|,12​|λ|)≤eρ−1,|b(\lambda)|\leq(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}-1)\min\left(2|\lambda|,\frac{1}{2|\lambda|}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{\rho}-1,(3.17)

where we used the elementary fact thatmin⁡(x,x−1)≤1\min(x,x^{-1})\leq 1for allx>0x>0.
To guarantee|r​(λ)|=|b​(λ)/a​(λ)|≤c0<1|r(\lambda)|=|b(\lambda)/a(\lambda)|\leq c_{0}<1, it is sufficient to require|b​(λ)|<|a​(λ)||b(\lambda)|<|a(\lambda)|.
Substituting (3.14) and (3.17), this is satisfied if:eρ−1<2−eρ⇔2​eρ<3⇔ρ<ln⁡(32).\mathrm{e}^{\rho}-1<2-\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\quad\iff\quad 2\mathrm{e}^{\rho}<3\quad\iff\quad\rho<\ln\left(\frac{3}{2}\right).(3.18)

Since our small norm restriction explicitly demandsρ<ln⁡(3/2)\rho<\ln(3/2), there exists a constantc0=eρ−12−eρ<1c_{0}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\rho}-1}{2-\mathrm{e}^{\rho}}<1such that|r​(λ)|≤c0|r(\lambda)|\leq c_{0}.

The proof for|r˘​(λ)|≤c0<1|\breve{r}(\lambda)|\leq c_{0}<1follows analogously.
 ∎

In fact, in the above estimation, although we setz∈ℝz\in\mathbb{R}, if we discussa​(λ)a(\lambda)anda˘​(λ)\breve{a}(\lambda), they can be extended to the corresponding analytic region, which ensures the absence of solitons and spectral singularities. This also means that under (3.4), the propositions discussed earlier are valid.

## Remark 3.3.

The small norm inequality (3.4) leads to the absence of both discrete spectrum and spectral singularities. Therefore, this situation cannot be avoided when characterizing nonlocal regions.

It is worthy noting that under (3.4), the Hermitian part of the jump matrixV​(x;λ)V(x;\lambda)is strictly positive definite. More precisely, we have the
following proposition.

## Proposition 3.4.

Suppose that (3.4) holds. Then there exist
two positive constantsη−\eta_{-}andη+\eta_{+}such that, for anyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R},λ∈Σλ\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda}, and any column vectorf∈ℂ2f\in\mathbb{C}^{2}, one hasRe​[f†​(I+V​(x;λ))​f]≥η−​f†​f,\mathrm{Re}\left[f^{\dagger}(I+V(x;\lambda))f\right]\geq\eta_{-}f^{\dagger}f,(3.19)

and‖(I+V​(x;λ))​f‖≤η+​‖f‖.\|(I+V(x;\lambda))f\|\leq\eta_{+}\|f\|.(3.20)

In particular, the Hermitian partI+VH​(x;λ):=I+12​(V​(x;λ)+V†​(x;λ))I+V_{H}(x;\lambda):=I+\frac{1}{2}\left(V(x;\lambda)+V^{\dagger}(x;\lambda)\right)(3.21)

is uniformly positive definite onℝ×Σλ\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma_{\lambda}.

## Proof.

By Proposition3.2, there existsc0<1c_{0}<1such that|r​(λ)|≤c0,|r˘​(λ)|≤c0,λ∈Σλ.\displaystyle|r(\lambda)|\leq c_{0},\qquad|\breve{r}(\lambda)|\leq c_{0},\qquad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda}.(3.22)

From the explicit expression ofV​(x;λ)V(x;\lambda)in (3.3),
we computeI+VH​(x;λ)=(112​(r˘​(λ)−r​(λ)¯)​e2​i​λ2​x12​(r˘​(λ)¯−r​(λ))​e−2​i​λ2​x1−Re​[r​(λ)​r˘​(λ)]).I+V_{H}(x;\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}1&\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\breve{r}(\lambda)-\overline{r(\lambda)}\right)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}x}\\[8.00003pt]
\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\overline{\breve{r}(\lambda)}-r(\lambda)\right)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}x}&1-\mathrm{Re}\left[r(\lambda)\breve{r}(\lambda)\right]\end{pmatrix}.(3.23)

For anyf∈ℂ2f\in\mathbb{C}^{2},Re​[f†​(I+V​(x;λ))​f]=f†​(I+VH​(x;λ))​f.\mathrm{Re}\left[f^{\dagger}(I+V(x;\lambda))f\right]=f^{\dagger}(I+V_{H}(x;\lambda))f.(3.24)

Thus the quadratic form in (3.24) is real-valued.

We next show thatVH​(x;λ)V_{H}(x;\lambda)is positive definite. Since the
upper-left entry ofVHV_{H}is11, it suffices to prove thatdet(I+VH​(x;λ))>0\det(I+V_{H}(x;\lambda))>0. A direct calculation givesdet(I+VH​(x;λ))\displaystyle\det(I+V_{H}(x;\lambda))=1−Re​[r​(λ)​r˘​(λ)]−14​|r˘​(λ)−r​(λ)¯|2\displaystyle=1-\mathrm{Re}\left[r(\lambda)\breve{r}(\lambda)\right]-\frac{1}{4}\left|\breve{r}(\lambda)-\overline{r(\lambda)}\right|^{2}=1−14​|r​(λ)+r˘​(λ)¯|2.\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{4}\left|r(\lambda)+\overline{\breve{r}(\lambda)}\right|^{2}.(3.25)

Using (3.5), we obtaindet(I+VH​(x;λ))≥1−14​(|r​(λ)|+|r˘​(λ)|)2≥1−c02>0.\det(I+V_{H}(x;\lambda))\geq 1-\frac{1}{4}\left(|r(\lambda)|+|\breve{r}(\lambda)|\right)^{2}\geq 1-c_{0}^{2}>0.(3.26)

ThereforeVH​(x;λ)V_{H}(x;\lambda)is strictly positive definite for allx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}andλ∈Σλ\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda}.

Moreover, the entries ofVHV_{H}are uniformly bounded. Hence its smallest
eigenvalue has a strictly positive uniform lower bound. Equivalently,
there exists a constantη−>0\eta_{-}>0, depending only onc0c_{0}, such thatf†​(I+VH​(x;λ))​f≥η−​f†​f.f^{\dagger}(I+V_{H}(x;\lambda))f\geq\eta_{-}f^{\dagger}f.(3.27)

Combining this estimate with (3.24), we obtain
(3.19).

It remains to prove (3.20). Letf=(f1,f2)Tf=(f_{1},f_{2})^{T}. By
(3.3),(I+V​(x;λ))​f=(f1+r˘​(λ)​e2​i​λ2​x​f2−r​(λ)​e−2​i​λ2​x​f1+(1−r​(λ)​r˘​(λ))​f2).(I+V(x;\lambda))f=\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}+\breve{r}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}x}f_{2}\\[3.99994pt]
-r(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}x}f_{1}+\left(1-r(\lambda)\breve{r}(\lambda)\right)f_{2}\end{pmatrix}.(3.28)

Using again|r​(λ)|≤c0|r(\lambda)|\leq c_{0}and|r˘​(λ)|≤c0|\breve{r}(\lambda)|\leq c_{0}, we get‖(I+V​(x;λ))​f‖2\displaystyle\|(I+V(x;\lambda))f\|^{2}≤(|f1|+c0​|f2|)2+(c0​|f1|+(1+c02)​|f2|)2\displaystyle\leq\left(|f_{1}|+c_{0}|f_{2}|\right)^{2}+\left(c_{0}|f_{1}|+(1+c_{0}^{2})|f_{2}|\right)^{2}≤η+2​(|f1|2+|f2|2)=η+2​‖f‖2,\displaystyle\leq\eta_{+}^{2}\left(|f_{1}|^{2}+|f_{2}|^{2}\right)=\eta_{+}^{2}\|f\|^{2},(3.29)

for some positive constantη+\eta_{+}depending only onc0c_{0}, which proves
(3.20). ∎

## 3.2A new RH problem on thezz-plane

In order to use the theorems on classical Cauchy integral and its projection on the real axis,
we change the original RH Problem3.1with jump contour onΣλ\Sigma_{\lambda}in theλ\lambda-plane into a RH problem with jump contour onℝ\mathbb{R}in thezz-plane.

We define a matrix function asN​(x;z):=e−i2​m+​(x)​σ3​{(Ψ1−​(x;z)a​(λ),Ψ2+​(x;z)),z∈ℂ−(Ψ1+​(x;z),Ψ2−​(x;z)a˘​(λ)),z∈ℂ+N(x;z):=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(x)\sigma_{3}}\begin{cases}\left(\dfrac{\Psi_{1}^{-}(x;z)}{a(\lambda)},\,\Psi_{2}^{+}(x;z)\right),\quad&z\in\mathbb{C}^{-}\\[11.99998pt]
\left(\Psi_{1}^{+}(x;z),\,\dfrac{\Psi_{2}^{-}(x;z)}{\breve{a}(\lambda)}\right),\quad&z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}\end{cases}(3.30)

and it satisfies the following RH problem on thezz-plane.

## Riemann-Hilbert Problem 3.5.

Find a matrix functionN​(x;z)N(x;z)with the following properties:
- 1.

Analyticity:N​(x;z)N(x;z)is analytic inℂ∖ℝ\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}.
- 2.

Jump condition:N​(x;z)N(x;z)satisfies the jump conditionN+​(x;z)=N−​(x;z)​(I+R​(x;z)),z∈ℝ,N_{+}(x;z)=N_{-}(x;z)(I+R(x;z)),\quad z\in\mathbb{R},(3.31)

where the jump matrix is defined byR​(x;z):=(0r1​(z)​e2​i​z​xr2​(z)​e−2​i​z​xr1​(z)​r2​(z)),R(x;z):=\begin{pmatrix}0&r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\vskip 4.33601pt\\
r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}&r_{1}(z)r_{2}(z)\end{pmatrix},(3.32)

withr1​(z):=−b˘​(λ)2​λ​a˘​(λ),r2​(z):=2​λ​b​(λ)a​(λ),z=λ2.r_{1}(z):=-\frac{\breve{b}(\lambda)}{2\lambda\breve{a}(\lambda)},\qquad r_{2}(z):=\frac{2\lambda b(\lambda)}{a(\lambda)},\qquad z=\lambda^{2}.(3.33)
- 3.

Normalization:N​(x;z)=I+𝒪​(z−1)N(x;z)=I+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}), asz→∞.z\to\infty.

Now we try to analyze some properties of the reflection coefficient.

## Proposition 3.6.

By the small norm assumption (3.4), we can get that|rj​(z)|≤c0<1,z∈ℝ,j=1,2.|r_{j}(z)|\leq c_{0}<1,\quad z\in\mathbb{R},\ j=1,2.(3.34)

## Proof.

One can find that the definition ofr1,2​(z)r_{1,2}(z)in (3.33) can be represented thatr1​(z)=b˘Ψ​(z)a˘Ψ​(z),r2​(z)=−bΨ​(z)aΨ​(z),r_{1}(z)=\dfrac{\breve{b}_{\Psi}(z)}{\breve{a}_{\Psi}(z)},\quad r_{2}(z)=\dfrac{-b_{\Psi}(z)}{a_{\Psi}(z)},(3.35)

which the notations are used in Proposition3.2. With the analysis in Proposition3.2, we can find that|b˘Ψ​(z)a˘Ψ​(z)|,|bΨ​(z)aΨ​(z)|≤eρ−12−eρ=c0<1,\left|\dfrac{\breve{b}_{\Psi}(z)}{\breve{a}_{\Psi}(z)}\right|,\left|\dfrac{b_{\Psi}(z)}{a_{\Psi}(z)}\right|\leq\dfrac{\mathrm{e}^{\rho}-1}{2-\mathrm{e}^{\rho}}=c_{0}<1,(3.36)

which complete the proof.
 ∎

## Proposition 3.7.

LetΩ⊂ℝ\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}. Iff∈Lp1​(Ω)∩L∞​(Ω),1≤p1<∞f\in L^{p_{1}}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega),\,1\leq p_{1}<\infty, thenf∈Lp2​(Ω)∩L∞​(Ω)f\in L^{p_{2}}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)for arbitrary1≤p1≤p2<∞1\leq p_{1}\leq p_{2}<\infty, and‖f‖Lp2​(Ω)≤C​‖f‖Lp1​(Ω)p1/p2,\|f\|_{L^{p_{2}}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{p_{1}}(\Omega)}^{p_{1}/p_{2}},(3.37)

where the constantCCdepends onp1,2p_{1,2}and‖f‖L∞​(Ω)\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}. Conversely, iff∈Lp2​(Ω)∩L∞​(Ω),1<p2<∞f\in L^{p_{2}}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega),\,1<p_{2}<\infty, then‖f‖Lp1​(Ω)\|f\|_{L^{p_{1}}(\Omega)}must not exist for arbitry1≤p1<p2<∞1\leq p_{1}<p_{2}<\infty.

## Proof.

Sincef∈L∞​(Ω)f\in L^{\infty}(\Omega), for1≤p1≤p2<∞1\leq p_{1}\leq p_{2}<\inftywe have|f|p2−p1∈L∞​(Ω)|f|^{p_{2}-p_{1}}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega). By the Hölder’s inequality,‖f‖Lp2​(Ω)p2=∫Ω|f|p2​𝑑x=∫Ω|f|p1​|f|p2−p1​𝑑x≤‖f‖L∞​(Ω)p2−p1​‖f‖Lp1​(Ω)p1.\|f\|_{L^{p_{2}}(\Omega)}^{p_{2}}=\int_{\Omega}|f|^{p_{2}}\,dx=\int_{\Omega}|f|^{p_{1}}|f|^{p_{2}-p_{1}}\,dx\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{p_{2}-p_{1}}\|f\|_{L^{p_{1}}(\Omega)}^{p_{1}}.(3.38)

Taking thep1p_{1}-th root on both sides of (3.38) gives rise to (3.37).

Conversely, for example, we takefα​(x)=x−α,x∈[1,∞]f_{\alpha}(x)=x^{-\alpha},\,x\in[1,\infty]with1/p2<α≤1/p11/p_{2}<\alpha\leq 1/p_{1}and1≤p1<p2<∞1\leq p_{1}<p_{2}<\infty(i.e.,αp2>1,0<αp1≤1)\alpha p_{2}>1,\,0<\alpha p_{1}\leq 1). Thus we have∫1∞|fα​(x)|p2​𝑑x=∫1∞1xα​p2​𝑑x=1α​p2−1<∞.\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}|f_{\alpha}(x)|^{p_{2}}dx=\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{x^{\alpha p_{2}}}dx=\frac{1}{\alpha p_{2}-1}<\infty.(3.39)

that is,fα​(x)∈Lp2​(Ω)∩L∞​(Ω)f_{\alpha}(x)\in L^{p_{2}}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega). However∫1∞|fα​(x)|p1​𝑑x=∫1∞1xα​p1​𝑑x≥∫1∞1x​𝑑x​(non−integrable),\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}|f_{\alpha}(x)|^{p_{1}}dx=\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{x^{\alpha p_{1}}}dx\geq\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{x}dx\,({\rm non-integrable}),(3.40)

which means thatfα​(x)f_{\alpha}(x)is not beLp1L^{p_{1}}-integrable inx∈[1,∞)x\in[1,\infty).
∎

## Proposition 3.8.

Suppose thatq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})andqP​T​(x)=q​(−x)q^{PT}(x)=q(-x), the scattering coefficientb​(λ)b(\lambda)admits the higher-order asymptotic behavior at the origin:b​(λ)=𝒪​(λ5),b˘​(λ)=𝒪​(λ5),λ→0.b(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{5}),\quad\breve{b}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{5}),\quad\lambda\to 0.(3.41)

## Proof.

By the symmetryb​(−λ)=−b​(λ)b(-\lambda)=-b(\lambda), the Maclaurin expansion ofb​(λ)b(\lambda)asλ→0\lambda\to 0contains only odd powers:b​(λ)=b3​λ3+b5​λ5+𝒪​(λ7),λ→0.b(\lambda)=b_{3}\lambda^{3}+b_{5}\lambda^{5}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{7}),\quad\lambda\to 0.(3.42)

To determineb3b_{3}, we extend the perturbation expansion of the Jost solutions (2.83) to𝒪​(λ3)\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3}):ψ±​(x;λ)=I+λ​U1​(x)+λ2​U2±​(x)+λ3​U3±​(x)+𝒪​(λ4).\psi^{\pm}(x;\lambda)=I+\lambda U_{1}(x)+\lambda^{2}U_{2}^{\pm}(x)+\lambda^{3}U_{3}^{\pm}(x)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{4}).(3.43)

Substituting this into thexx-part of (2.2) and matching the𝒪​(λ3)\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3})terms, we haveU3,x±=i​[σ3,U1±]+i​Qx​U2±U_{3,x}^{\pm}=\mathrm{i}[\sigma_{3},U_{1}^{\pm}]+\mathrm{i}Q_{x}U_{2}^{\pm}.
Recall from (2.84) thatU1=i​QU_{1}=\mathrm{i}QandU2,11±​(x)=−∫±∞x(qP​T)y​(y)​q​(y)​𝑑yU_{2,11}^{\pm}(x)=-\int_{\pm\infty}^{x}(q^{PT})_{y}(y)q(y)\,dy. For the(2,1)(2,1)-entry ofU3±U_{3}^{\pm}, direct computation yields the differential equation:U3,21,x±=2​q​(x)−i​qx​(x)​∫±∞x(qP​T)y​(y)​q​(y)​𝑑y.U_{3,21,x}^{\pm}=2q(x)-\mathrm{i}q_{x}(x)\int_{\pm\infty}^{x}(q^{PT})_{y}(y)q(y)\,dy.(3.44)

Integrating this equation with the boundary conditionsψ±​(x;λ)→I\psi^{\pm}(x;\lambda)\to Iasx→±∞x\to\pm\inftygives:U3,21±​(x)=∫±∞x(2​q​(y)−i​qy​(y)​∫±∞yqsP​T​(s)​q​(s)​𝑑s)​𝑑y.U_{3,21}^{\pm}(x)=\int_{\pm\infty}^{x}\left(2q(y)-\mathrm{i}q_{y}(y)\int_{\pm\infty}^{y}q_{s}^{PT}(s)q(s)\,ds\right)dy.(3.45)

Using the Wronskian representation (2.65) evaluated atx=0x=0,b​(λ)=ψ11+​(0;λ)​ψ21−​(0;λ)−ψ21+​(0;λ)​ψ11−​(0;λ).b(\lambda)=\psi_{11}^{+}(0;\lambda)\psi_{21}^{-}(0;\lambda)-\psi_{21}^{+}(0;\lambda)\psi_{11}^{-}(0;\lambda).

Extracting the coefficient ofλ3\lambda^{3}, we find:b3=U3,21−​(0)−U3,21+​(0)+i​q​(0)​(U2,11+​(0)−U2,11−​(0)).b_{3}=U_{3,21}^{-}(0)-U_{3,21}^{+}(0)+\mathrm{i}q(0)\left(U_{2,11}^{+}(0)-U_{2,11}^{-}(0)\right).(3.46)

The diagonal contribution is simply evaluated as:U2,11+​(0)−U2,11−​(0)=∫−∞+∞(qP​T)x​(x)​q​(x)​𝑑x.U_{2,11}^{+}(0)-U_{2,11}^{-}(0)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}(q^{PT})_{x}(x)q(x)\,dx.(3.47)

For the off-diagonal terms, (3.45) leads to:U3,21−​(0)−U3,21+​(0)=∫−∞+∞2​q​(x)​𝑑x−i​(∫−∞0+∫0+∞)​[qx​(∫−∞x(qP​T)y​q​𝑑y)]​d​x.\begin{array}[]{rl}U_{3,21}^{-}(0)-U_{3,21}^{+}(0)=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}2q(x)\,dx-\mathrm{i}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{0}+\int_{0}^{+\infty}\right)\left[q_{x}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{x}(q^{PT})_{y}q\,dy\right)\right]dx.\end{array}(3.48)

Applying integration by parts to the double integrals in the bracket yields:∫−∞0qx​(x)​(∫−∞x(qP​T)y​q​𝑑y)​𝑑x\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}q_{x}(x)\left(\int_{-\infty}^{x}(q^{PT})_{y}q\,dy\right)dx=q​(0)​∫−∞0(qP​T)x​q​𝑑x−∫−∞0(qP​T)x​(x)​q2​(x)​𝑑x,\displaystyle=q(0)\int_{-\infty}^{0}(q^{PT})_{x}q\,dx-\int_{-\infty}^{0}(q^{PT})_{x}(x)q^{2}(x)\,dx,(3.49)∫0+∞qx​(x)​(∫+∞x(qP​T)y​q​𝑑y)​𝑑x\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}q_{x}(x)\left(\int_{+\infty}^{x}(q^{PT})_{y}q\,dy\right)dx=−q​(0)​∫0+∞(qP​T)x​q​𝑑x−∫0+∞(qP​T)x​(x)​q2​(x)​𝑑x.\displaystyle=-q(0)\int_{0}^{+\infty}(q^{PT})_{x}q\,dx-\int_{0}^{+\infty}(q^{PT})_{x}(x)q^{2}(x)\,dx.(3.50)

Substituting these back into (3.48) and collecting terms, the boundary valuesq​(0)q(0)cancel out perfectly with the contribution from (3.47). This simplifiesb3b_{3}to a remarkable global integral:b3=∫−∞+∞(2​q​(x)+i​(qP​T)x​(x)​q2​(x))​𝑑x.b_{3}=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\left(2q(x)+\mathrm{i}(q^{PT})_{x}(x)q^{2}(x)\right)dx.(3.51)

Finally, applying integration by parts to the second term in (3.51) under the assumption of vanishing boundaries at infinity, we havei​∫−∞+∞(qP​T)x​q2​𝑑x=−2​i​∫−∞+∞qP​T​q​qx​𝑑x\mathrm{i}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}(q^{PT})_{x}q^{2}\,dx=-2\mathrm{i}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}q^{PT}qq_{x}\,dx. Consequently,b3=2​∫−∞+∞(q​(x)−i​q​(x)​qP​T​(x)​qx​(x))​𝑑x.b_{3}=2\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\left(q(x)-\mathrm{i}q(x)q^{PT}(x)q_{x}(x)\right)dx.(3.52)

Crucially, recalling the nFL equation (1.7), we have the inherent dynamical identityq−i​q​qP​T​qx=qx​tq-\mathrm{i}qq^{PT}q_{x}=q_{xt}. Substituting this into (3.52) directly yields:b3=2​∫−∞+∞qx​t​𝑑x=2​∂∂t​∫−∞+∞qx​𝑑x=0.b_{3}=2\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}q_{xt}\,dx=2\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}q_{x}\,dx=0.(3.53)

This confirms thatb3≡0b_{3}\equiv 0identically as a consequence of the equation’s intrinsic dynamics, establishingb​(λ)=𝒪​(λ5)b(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{5})asλ→0\lambda\to 0. Forb˘​(λ)\breve{b}(\lambda), we can use the symmetryb˘​(λ)=−b​(λ)\breve{b}(\lambda)=-b(\lambda), which can prove the result.

## Proposition 3.9.

Suppose thatq​(x)∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q(x)\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}).
Letδ>0\delta>0be fixed and sufficiently small. Thenz−2​λ−1​b​(λ)∈Lz2​(−δ,δ),z−2​λ−1​b˘​(λ)∈Lz2​(−δ,δ).z^{-2}\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2}(-\delta,\delta),\qquad z^{-2}\lambda^{-1}\breve{b}(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2}(-\delta,\delta).(3.54)

Moreover, under (3.4) and we have|a​(λ)|≥a0>0,|a˘​(λ)|≥a˘0>0,λ∈Σλ.|a(\lambda)|\geq a_{0}>0,\qquad|\breve{a}(\lambda)|\geq\breve{a}_{0}>0,\qquad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda}.(3.55)

Thenz−2​r1​(z)∈L2​(ℝ),z−2​r2​(z)∈L2​(ℝ).z^{-2}r_{1}(z)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\qquad z^{-2}r_{2}(z)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).(3.56)

## Proof.

By Proposition2.9, we havez−1​λ−1​b​(λ)∈Hz1​(ℝ)⊂Lz2​(ℝ)∩Lz∞​(ℝ),z^{-1}\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\subset L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{z}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}),(3.57)

and similarlyz−1​λ−1​b˘​(λ)∈Lz2​(ℝ)∩Lz∞​(ℝ).z^{-1}\lambda^{-1}\breve{b}(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{z}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}).

Hence, by Proposition3.7,z−1​λ−1​b​(λ)∈Lzp​(ℝ),z−1​λ−1​b˘​(λ)∈Lzp​(ℝ),2≤p<∞.z^{-1}\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{p}(\mathbb{R}),\qquad z^{-1}\lambda^{-1}\breve{b}(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{p}(\mathbb{R}),\qquad 2\leq p<\infty.(3.58)

From Proposition3.8, we can find thatz−2​λ−1​b​(λ)=𝒪​(1),λ→0.z^{-2}\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}(1),\quad\lambda\to 0.(3.59)

which means that for fixed sufficiently smallδ>0\delta>0,z−2​λ−1​b​(λ)z^{-2}\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)is bounded on(−δ,δ)(-\delta,\delta).
Thusz−2​λ−1​b​(λ)∈Lz2​(−δ,δ)z^{-2}\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2}(-\delta,\delta).
The same argument givesz−2​λ−1​b˘​(λ)∈Lz2​(−δ,δ)z^{-2}\lambda^{-1}\breve{b}(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2}(-\delta,\delta).

Now letΩ:=ℝ∖(−δ,δ),\Omega:=\mathbb{R}\setminus(-\delta,\delta),and denote byχΩ\chi_{\Omega}the characteristic function ofΩ\Omega.
Sincer1,r2∈L∞​(ℝ)r_{1},r_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})andz−2∈L2​(Ω)z^{-2}\in L^{2}(\Omega), we have‖z−2​r1​(z)‖L2​(ℝ)\displaystyle\|z^{-2}r_{1}(z)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}≤‖χΩ​z−2​r1​(z)‖L2​(ℝ)+‖(1−χΩ)​z−2​r1​(z)‖L2​(ℝ)\displaystyle\leq\|\chi_{\Omega}z^{-2}r_{1}(z)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\|(1-\chi_{\Omega})z^{-2}r_{1}(z)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}(3.60)≤2​‖r1‖L∞​‖z−2‖L2​(δ,∞)+1a˘0​‖z−2​λ−1​b˘​(λ)‖L2​(0,δ).\displaystyle\leq 2\|r_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|z^{-2}\|_{L^{2}(\delta,\infty)}+\frac{1}{\breve{a}_{0}}\|z^{-2}\lambda^{-1}\breve{b}(\lambda)\|_{L^{2}(0,\delta)}.(3.61)

Similarly, sinceλ​b​(λ)=z​λ−1​b​(λ),\lambda b(\lambda)=z\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda),

we obtain‖z−2​r2​(z)‖L2​(ℝ)≤2​‖r2‖L∞​‖z−2‖L2​(δ,∞)+4a0​‖z−1​λ−1​b​(λ)‖L2​(0,δ).\displaystyle\|z^{-2}r_{2}(z)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq 2\|r_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|z^{-2}\|_{L^{2}(\delta,\infty)}+\frac{4}{a_{0}}\|z^{-1}\lambda^{-1}b(\lambda)\|_{L^{2}(0,\delta)}.(3.62)

Therefore,z−2​r1,z−2​r2∈L2​(ℝ)z^{-2}r_{1},z^{-2}r_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

From the above analysis, we can draw the following conclusion

## Proposition 3.10.

Suppose thatq​(x)q(x)satisfies the small norm restriction (3.4). The relations between the potentialq​(x)q(x)and the reflection coefficientsr1​(z),r2​(z)r_{1}(z),r_{2}(z)are given as follows:
- (i)

Ifq∈H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), thenr1,r2∈Hz1​(ℝ)r_{1},r_{2}\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}).
- (ii)

Ifq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), thenr1,r2∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)r_{1},r_{2}\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})andz−2​r1,z−2​r2∈Lz2​(ℝ)z^{-2}r_{1},z^{-2}r_{2}\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Furthermore, the mappingH3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)∋q↦(r1,r2)∈𝒲​(ℝ)H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\ni q\mapsto(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})is Lipschitz continuous.

## Proof.

According to the definition (3.33), the reflection coefficients can be written as:r1​(z)=−λ−1​b˘​(λ)2​a˘​(λ),r2​(z)=2​λ​b​(λ)a​(λ).r_{1}(z)=-\frac{\lambda^{-1}\breve{b}(\lambda)}{2\breve{a}(\lambda)},\quad r_{2}(z)=\frac{2\lambda b(\lambda)}{a(\lambda)}.(3.63)

By Proposition3.9, under the small norm condition (3.4), the denominators are uniformly bounded away from zero on the continuous spectrum:|a​(λ)|≥a0>0,|a˘​(λ)|≥a˘0>0,∀λ∈Σλ.|a(\lambda)|\geq a_{0}>0,\quad|\breve{a}(\lambda)|\geq\breve{a}_{0}>0,\quad\forall\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda}.(3.64)

From Corollary2.10, we know that ifq∈H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), thenλ−1​b˘​(λ),λ​b​(λ)∈Hz1​(ℝ)\lambda^{-1}\breve{b}(\lambda),\lambda b(\lambda)\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}), anda​(λ)−a∞,a˘​(λ)−a˘∞∈Hz1​(ℝ)a(\lambda)-a_{\infty},\breve{a}(\lambda)-\breve{a}_{\infty}\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}). SinceHz1​(ℝ)H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})is a Banach algebra, and the reciprocal of a non-vanishing function of the form constant +Hz1​(ℝ)H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})remains in the same class, the quotientsr1​(z)r_{1}(z)andr2​(z)r_{2}(z)also belong toHz1​(ℝ)H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}).

Similarly, ifq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), Corollary2.10ensures thatλ−1​b˘​(λ),λ​b​(λ)∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)\lambda^{-1}\breve{b}(\lambda),\lambda b(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}). Since multiplication by anLz∞L_{z}^{\infty}bounded function preserves theLz2,1​(ℝ)L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})property, we haver1,r2∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)r_{1},r_{2}\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}). The additional propertyz−2​r1,z−2​r2∈Lz2​(ℝ)z^{-2}r_{1},z^{-2}r_{2}\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})has already been strictly established in Proposition3.9.

Finally, the Lipschitz continuity of the mapping from the potentialqqto(r1,r2)(r_{1},r_{2})follows directly from the Lipschitz continuity of the mappings fromqqto the basic scattering dataλ−1​b˘​(λ),λ​b​(λ),a​(λ)\lambda^{-1}\breve{b}(\lambda),\lambda b(\lambda),a(\lambda), anda˘​(λ)\breve{a}(\lambda), as stated in Corollary2.10. Because the denominator termsa​(λ)a(\lambda)anda˘​(λ)\breve{a}(\lambda)are globally strictly separated from zero, the quotient map inherits and preserves the Lipschitz continuity.
 ∎

Before we formally estimate the solution, we also need to analyze some analytical properties of the original reflection coefficient.

## Proposition 3.11.

Ifr1​(z),r2​(z)∈Hz1​(ℝ)∩Lz2,1​(ℝ)r_{1}(z),r_{2}(z)\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})andqqsatisfies (3.4), thenr​(λ),r˘​(λ)∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)∩Lz∞​(ℝ)r(\lambda),\breve{r}(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{z}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}).

## Proof.

First, we prove the boundedness inLz∞​(ℝ)L_{z}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}). From3.2, we have|r​(λ)|≤c0,|r˘​(λ)|≤c0,∀λ∈Σλ.|r(\lambda)|\leq c_{0},\quad|\breve{r}(\lambda)|\leq c_{0},\quad\forall\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda}.

This strictly bounds them by a constant, thus yieldingr​(λ),r˘​(λ)∈Lz∞​(ℝ)r(\lambda),\breve{r}(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}).

Next, we prove the property inLz2,1​(ℝ)L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}). According to (3.33), we haver1​(z)=−b˘​(λ)2​λ​a˘​(λ)=−r˘​(λ)2​λ,r2​(z)=2​λ​b​(λ)a​(λ)=2​λ​r​(λ).r_{1}(z)=-\frac{\breve{b}(\lambda)}{2\lambda\breve{a}(\lambda)}=-\frac{\breve{r}(\lambda)}{2\lambda},\quad r_{2}(z)=\frac{2\lambda b(\lambda)}{a(\lambda)}=2\lambda r(\lambda).(3.65)

To avoid singularities atλ=0\lambda=0and the scaling issue as|λ|→∞|\lambda|\to\infty, we writer​(λ)r(\lambda)in a piecewise form:r​(λ)={b​(λ)a​(λ),|λ|≤1,r2​(z)2​λ,|λ|>1.r(\lambda)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{b(\lambda)}{a(\lambda)},&|\lambda|\leq 1,\\[8.00003pt]
\dfrac{r_{2}(z)}{2\lambda},&|\lambda|>1.\end{cases}(3.66)

For|λ|≤1|\lambda|\leq 1, sinceb​(λ)=𝒪​(λ5)b(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{5})asλ→0\lambda\to 0anda​(λ)a(\lambda)has no zeros,r​(λ)r(\lambda)is continuous and bounded on this compact set. For|λ|>1|\lambda|>1, since(2​λ)−1(2\lambda)^{-1}is bounded andr2​(z)∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)r_{2}(z)\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), this part belongs toLz2,1​(ℝ)L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}).

Similarly, forr˘​(λ)\breve{r}(\lambda), we write:r˘​(λ)={−2​λ​r1​(z),|λ|≤1,b˘​(λ)a˘​(λ),|λ|>1.\breve{r}(\lambda)=\begin{cases}-2\lambda r_{1}(z),&|\lambda|\leq 1,\\[8.00003pt]
\dfrac{\breve{b}(\lambda)}{\breve{a}(\lambda)},&|\lambda|>1.\end{cases}(3.67)

For|λ|≤1|\lambda|\leq 1,2​λ2\lambdais bounded andr1​(z)∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)r_{1}(z)\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), so the product is inLz2,1​(ℝ)L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}).
For|λ|>1|\lambda|>1, we use the propertyλ​b˘​(λ)∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)\lambda\breve{b}(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})established in Corollary2.10. We can write the numerator asb˘​(λ)=λ−1​(λ​b˘​(λ))\breve{b}(\lambda)=\lambda^{-1}(\lambda\breve{b}(\lambda)). Since|λ−1|<1|\lambda^{-1}|<1in this region, the multiplication by a bounded function ensuresb˘​(λ)∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)\breve{b}(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}). Becausea˘​(λ)\breve{a}(\lambda)is uniformly bounded away from zero, we haver˘​(λ)∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)\breve{r}(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}).
Combining these results, we conclude thatr​(λ),r˘​(λ)∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)r(\lambda),\breve{r}(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}).

Combining these results, we conclude thatr​(λ),r˘​(λ)∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)r(\lambda),\breve{r}(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}).
 ∎

## Proposition 3.12.

Ifr1​(z),r2​(z)∈Hz1​(ℝ)∩Lz2,1​(ℝ)r_{1}(z),r_{2}(z)\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), then‖λ​r1​(z)‖Lz∞≤‖r1‖H1∩L2,1,‖λ​r2​(z)‖Lz∞≤‖r2‖H1∩L2,1.\|\lambda r_{1}(z)\|_{L_{z}^{\infty}}\leq\|r_{1}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}},\quad\|\lambda r_{2}(z)\|_{L_{z}^{\infty}}\leq\|r_{2}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}}.(3.68)

## Proof.

Forrj​(z)∈Hz1​(ℝ)∩Lz2,1​(ℝ)r_{j}(z)\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})(j=1,2j=1,2), usingz=λ2z=\lambda^{2}and the fundamental theorem of calculus, we have:|λ​rj​(z)|2=|z​rj2​(z)|=|∫0zdd​s​(s​rj2​(s))​𝑑s|.|\lambda r_{j}(z)|^{2}=|zr_{j}^{2}(z)|=\left|\int_{0}^{z}\frac{d}{ds}\left(sr_{j}^{2}(s)\right)ds\right|.(3.69)

Applying the product rule to the integrand gives:|λ​rj​(z)|2=|∫0z(rj​(s)2+2​s​rj​(s)​rj′​(s))​𝑑s|.|\lambda r_{j}(z)|^{2}=\left|\int_{0}^{z}\left(r_{j}(s)^{2}+2sr_{j}(s)r_{j}^{\prime}(s)\right)ds\right|.(3.70)

By extending the integration interval to the whole real lineℝ\mathbb{R}and using the triangle inequality, we obtain:|λ​rj​(z)|2≤∫ℝ|rj​(s)|2​𝑑s+2​∫ℝ|s​rj​(s)|​|rj′​(s)|​𝑑s.|\lambda r_{j}(z)|^{2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}|r_{j}(s)|^{2}\,ds+2\int_{\mathbb{R}}|sr_{j}(s)||r_{j}^{\prime}(s)|\,ds.(3.71)

Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to the second term yields:|λ​rj​(z)|2≤‖rj‖L22+2​‖rj′‖L2​‖z​rj‖L2.|\lambda r_{j}(z)|^{2}\leq\|r_{j}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+2\|r_{j}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}}\|zr_{j}\|_{L^{2}}.(3.72)

Sincerj∈Hz1​(ℝ)∩Lz2,1​(ℝ)r_{j}\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), the norms‖rj‖L2\|r_{j}\|_{L^{2}},‖rj′‖L2\|r_{j}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}}, and‖z​rj‖L2\|zr_{j}\|_{L^{2}}are all bounded. We can bound the entire right-hand side by the mixed norm:|λ​rj​(z)|2≤‖rj‖H1∩L2,12.|\lambda r_{j}(z)|^{2}\leq\|r_{j}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}}^{2}.(3.73)

Taking the square root on both sides, we arrive at the desired estimate:‖λ​rj​(z)‖Lz∞≤‖rj‖H1∩L2,1,j=1,2.\|\lambda r_{j}(z)\|_{L_{z}^{\infty}}\leq\|r_{j}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}},\quad j=1,2.(3.74)

∎

## 4Inverse Scattering Transform

## 4.1Transformations of RH problem

For a given functionh​(z)∈Lp​(ℝ)h(z)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})with1≤p<∞1\leq p<\infty, the Cauchy operator is defined by𝒞​(h)​(z):=12​π​i​∫ℝh​(s)s−z​𝑑s,z∈ℂ∖ℝ.\mathcal{C}(h)(z):=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{h(s)}{s-z}\,ds,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}.(4.1)

Whenz±i​ϵz\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilonapproaches to a point at the real axisz∈ℝz\in\mathbb{R}transversely from the upper and the lower half planes, the Cauchy operator𝒞\mathcal{C}becomes the Plemelj projection operators defined respectively by𝒫±​(h)​(z):=limϵ↓012​π​i​∫ℝh​(s)s−(z±i​ϵ)​𝑑s,z∈ℝ.\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(h)(z):=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{h(s)}{s-(z\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}\,ds,\quad z\in\mathbb{R}.(4.2)

We list the basic properties of the Cauchy and the Plemelj projection operators in the following proposition[24,49,69].

## Proposition 4.1.

For everyh∈Lp​(ℝ)h\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}),1≤p<∞1\leq p<\inftythe Cauchy operator𝒞​(h)\mathcal{C}(h)and the projection operator𝒫±​(h)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(h)has the following properties:
- 1.

𝒞​(h)\mathcal{C}(h)is analytic inℂ±\mathbb{C}^{\pm}and goes to zero as|z|→∞|z|\to\infty.
- 2.

Ifh∈L1​(ℝ)h\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}), then inℂ+\mathbb{C}^{+}orℂ−\mathbb{C}^{-}, asλ\lambdaalongs the contour which is not tangential toℝ\mathbb{R}going to∞\infty, the Cauchy operator admits the following asymptoticlim|z|→∞z​𝒞​(h)​(z)=−12​π​i​∫ℝh​(s)​𝑑s.\lim_{|z|\to\infty}z\mathcal{C}(h)(z)=-\dfrac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(s)\,ds.(4.3)

And the projection operator𝒫±​(h)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(h)has the following properties:
- 3.

𝒞​(h)\mathcal{C}(h)approaches to𝒫±​(h)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(h)almost everywhere, when a pointz∈ℂ±z\in\mathbb{C}^{\pm}approaches to a pointz0∈ℝz_{0}\in\mathbb{R}by any non-tangential contour fromℂ±\mathbb{C}^{\pm}.
- 4.

For1<p<∞1<p<\infty, there exists a positive constantccsuch that‖𝒫±​(h)‖Lp​(ℝ)≤c​‖h‖Lp​(ℝ).\|\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(h)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leq c\|h\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}.(4.4)

The RH Problem3.5admits the Beals-Coifman solutionN±​(x;z)=I+𝒫±​(N−​(x;⋅)​R​(x;⋅))​(z),z∈ℝ,N_{\pm}(x;z)=I+\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(N_{-}(x;\cdot)R(x;\cdot))(z),\quad z\in\mathbb{R},(4.5)

which can be used to estimate the columns ofN±​(x;z)−IN_{\pm}(x;z)-I.
Next, we introduce the2×22\times 2matrixT​(x;z)=(N+,1​(x;z)−e1,N−,2​(x;z)−e2).T(x;z)=(N_{+,1}(x;z)-e_{1},N_{-,2}(x;z)-e_{2}).(4.6)

From the jump condition (3.31), we can deduce thatT​(x;z)T(x;z)satisfies the following integral equationT−𝒫+​(T​R−)−𝒫−​(T​R+)=FT-\mathcal{P}^{+}(TR_{-})-\mathcal{P}^{-}(TR_{+})=F(4.7)

whereR+​(x;z)=(0r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x00),R−​(x;z)=(00r2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x0),R_{+}(x;z)=\begin{pmatrix}0&r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad R_{-}(x;z)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}&0\end{pmatrix},(4.8)

andF​(x;z)=(𝒫+​(r2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x)​e2,𝒫−​(r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x)​e1)F(x;z)=\left(\mathcal{P}^{+}(r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx})e_{2},\,\mathcal{P}^{-}(r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})e_{1}\right)(4.9)

An estimate onFFrelies on the mapping properties of the projection operators. Our main task is to make a further estimate on the Cauchy integral projection in the equation above. To analyze the derivatives ofN+,1​(x;z)N_{+,1}(x;z)andN−,2​(x;z)N_{-,2}(x;z), we take the derivative of the inhomogeneous equation inxx, which gives∂xT−𝒫+​(∂xT​R−)−𝒫−​(∂xT​R+)=F~,\partial_{x}T-\mathcal{P}^{+}(\partial_{x}TR_{-})-\mathcal{P}^{-}(\partial_{x}TR_{+})=\tilde{F},(4.10)

whereF~​(x;z)=2​i​(e2​𝒫+​(−z​r2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x),e1​𝒫−​(z​r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x))+2​i​(𝒫+​(−z​r2​(z)​N−,12​(x;z)​e−2​i​z​x)𝒫−​(z​r1​(z)​(N+,11​(x;z)−1)​e2​i​z​x)𝒫+​(−z​r2​(z)​(N−,22​(x;z)−1)​e−2​i​z​x)𝒫−​(z​r1​(z)​N+,21​(x;z)​e2​i​z​x)).\begin{split}\tilde{F}(x;z)=&\,2\mathrm{i}\left(e_{2}\mathcal{P}^{+}(-zr_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}),\,e_{1}\mathcal{P}^{-}(zr_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})\right)\\
&+2\mathrm{i}\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{P}^{+}(-zr_{2}(z)N_{-,12}(x;z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx})&\mathcal{P}^{-}(zr_{1}(z)(N_{+,11}(x;z)-1)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})\vskip 4.33601pt\\
\mathcal{P}^{+}(-zr_{2}(z)(N_{-,22}(x;z)-1)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx})&\mathcal{P}^{-}(zr_{1}(z)N_{+,21}(x;z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}(4.11)

Proposition3.12inspires us to start withλ​r1,2\lambda r_{1,2}to solve the problem. Therefore, for everyλ∈ℂ∖{0}\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}, we introduce two new matricesτ1​(λ):=(100−2​λ),τ2​(λ):=((−2​λ)−1001),\tau_{1}(\lambda):=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-2\lambda\end{pmatrix},\quad\tau_{2}(\lambda):=\begin{pmatrix}(-2\lambda)^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},(4.12)

to give the original problem the form we want. With the definition ofV​(x;λ)V(x;\lambda)in (3.3),τ1,2\tau_{1,2}admitsτ1−1​(λ)​R​(x;z)​τ1​(λ)=τ2−1​(λ)​R​(x;z)​τ2​(λ)=V​(x;λ),z∈ℝ,λ∈Σλ.\tau_{1}^{-1}(\lambda)R(x;z)\tau_{1}(\lambda)=\tau_{2}^{-1}(\lambda)R(x;z)\tau_{2}(\lambda)=V(x;\lambda),\quad z\in\mathbb{R},\ \lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda}.(4.13)

It can be seen from the following analysis that the transformation ofN​(x;z)N(x;z)using these two matrices could not only complete the estimation ofN​(x;z)N(x;z)itself and its derivative, but also make full use of the good properties of the jump matrixV​(x;λ)V(x;\lambda).

In the following RH problem, the properties of the matrix elements are characterized in thezz-plane: Under (3.4), ifr1,2∈Hz1​(ℝ)∩Lz2,1​(ℝ)r_{1,2}\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), then Proposition3.11implies thatV∈Lz1​(ℝ)∩Lz∞​(ℝ)V\in L_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{z}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})andF​(x;z)∈Lz2​(ℝ)F(x;z)\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})for everyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}. We consider the class of solutions to the RH Problem3.5for everyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}. Therefore, we equivalently reduce the RH Problem3.5in thezz-plane to a new RH problem related to the matrixV​(x;λ)V(x;\lambda)instead of the matrixR​(x;z)R(x;z).

## Riemann-Hilbert Problem 4.2.

Find a matrix functionGj​(x;λ)G_{j}(x;\lambda)(j=1,2j=1,2) with the following properties:
- 1.

Analyticity:Gj​(x;λ)G_{j}(x;\lambda)are analytic functions ofzzinℂ±\mathbb{C}^{\pm}.
- 2.

Jump condition:Gj​(x;λ)G_{j}(x;\lambda)satisfies the jump conditionGj,+​(x;λ)=Gj,−​(x;λ)​(I+V​(x;λ))+Dj​(x;λ),λ∈Σλ,G_{j,+}(x;\lambda)=G_{j,-}(x;\lambda)(I+V(x;\lambda))+D_{j}(x;\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda},(4.14)

whereGj,±​(x;λ):=N±​(x;z)​τj​(λ)−τj​(λ),Dj​(x;λ):=τj​(λ)​V​(x;λ).G_{j,\pm}(x;\lambda):=N_{\pm}(x;z)\tau_{j}(\lambda)-\tau_{j}(\lambda),\quad D_{j}(x;\lambda):=\tau_{j}(\lambda)V(x;\lambda).(4.15)
- 3.

Parity:the columns ofGj,±​(x;λ)G_{j,\pm}(x;\lambda),Gj,−​(x;λ)​VG_{j,-}(x;\lambda)V, andDjD_{j}have the same parity inλ\lambda.
- 4.

Normalization:Gj,±​(x;λ)→0as|λ|→∞.G_{j,\pm}(x;\lambda)\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad|\lambda|\to\infty.

Based on the positive definiteness ofI+VH​(x;λ)I+V_{H}(x;\lambda)established in Proposition3.4, we can express the solution of RH Problem4.2with the Cauchy integralsGj,±​(x;λ)=𝒞​(Gj,−​(x;⋅)​V+Dj)​(z),z∈ℂ±.G_{j,\pm}(x;\lambda)=\mathcal{C}(G_{j,-}(x;\cdot)V+D_{j})(z),\quad z\in\mathbb{C}^{\pm}.(4.16)

There is a solutionGj,−​(x;λ)∈Lz2​(ℝ)G_{j,-}(x;\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})to the Fredholm integral equation:Gj,−​(x;λ)=𝒫−​(Gj,−​(x;⋅)​V​(x;⋅)+Dj​(x;⋅))​(z),z∈ℝ.G_{j,-}(x;\lambda)=\mathcal{P}^{-}(G_{j,-}(x;\cdot)V(x;\cdot)+D_{j}(x;\cdot))(z),\quad z\in\mathbb{R}.(4.17)

OnceGj,−​(x;λ)∈Lz2​(ℝ)G_{j,-}(x;\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})is found,Gj,+​(x;λ)∈Lz2​(ℝ)G_{j,+}(x;\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})is obtained from the projection formulaGj,+​(x;λ)=𝒫+​(Gj,−​(x;⋅)​V​(x;⋅)+Dj​(x;⋅))​(z),z∈ℝ.G_{j,+}(x;\lambda)=\mathcal{P}^{+}(G_{j,-}(x;\cdot)V(x;\cdot)+D_{j}(x;\cdot))(z),\quad z\in\mathbb{R}.(4.18)

In order to show the RH problem4.2exists a unique solution, we first need to explain the following proposition:

## Proposition 4.3.

Under (3.4), for everyr​(λ),r˘​(λ)∈Lz2∩Lz∞r(\lambda),\breve{r}(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2}\cap L_{z}^{\infty}andD​(λ)∈Lz2​(ℝ)D(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}), there exists a unique solutionG​(λ)∈Lz2​(ℝ)G(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})of the linear inhomogeneous equation(I−𝒫V−)​G​(λ)=D​(λ),λ∈Σλ,(I-\mathcal{P}_{V}^{-})G(\lambda)=D(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda},(4.19)

with𝒫V−​G:=𝒫−​(G​V)\mathcal{P}_{V}^{-}G:=\mathcal{P}^{-}(GV).

## Proof.

By the classical theory of singular integral operators[8,9,68], and the Proposition3.4, which establishes that the Hermitian partI+VH​(x;λ)I+V_{H}(x;\lambda)is strictly positive definite for allλ∈Σλ\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda}, it is known thatI−𝒫V−I-\mathcal{P}_{V}^{-}is a Fredholm operator with index zero.

By Fredholm’s alternative, the unique solvability of the inhomogeneous equation (4.19) inLz2​(ℝ)L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})is equivalent to showing that the corresponding homogeneous equation has only the trivial solution. Suppose there exists a nonzero row vectorg∈Lz2​(ℝ)g\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})such that(I−𝒫V−)​g=0(I-\mathcal{P}_{V}^{-})g=0. We can define two analytic functions inℂ∖ℝ\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}withV​(λ)∈Lz2​(ℝ)∩Lz∞​(ℝ)V(\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{z}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})byg1​(z):=𝒞​(g​V)​(z)andg2​(z):=(𝒞​(g​V)​(z¯))†.\displaystyle g_{1}(z):=\mathcal{C}(gV)(z)\quad\text{and}\quad g_{2}(z):=(\mathcal{C}(gV)(\bar{z}))^{\dagger}.(4.20)

We integrate the product ofg1g_{1}andg2g_{2}inℂ±\mathbb{C}^{\pm}along a semicircle with radius R and center 0.0=∮g1​(z)​g2​(z)​𝑑z\displaystyle 0=\oint g_{1}(z)\,g_{2}(z)\,dz(4.21)

holds becauseg1g_{1}andg2g_{2}are analytic functions inℂ+\mathbb{C}^{+}.
Under the conditiong∈Lz2​(ℝ)g\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})andV​(x;λ)∈Lz2​(ℝ)∩Lz∞​(ℝ)V(x;\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{z}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}), we deriveg​V∈Lz1​(ℝ)gV\in L_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}).
Finally, Proposition4.1impliesg1,2​(z)=𝒪​(z−1)g_{1,2}(z)=\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})as|z|→∞|z|\to\infty.
Therefore, the integral on the arc goes to zero asR→∞R\to\infty, and we obtain0=∫ℝg1​(z)​g2​(z)​𝑑z=∫ℝ𝒫+​(g​V)​(z)​[𝒫−​(g​V)​(z¯)]†​𝑑z=∫ℝ[𝒫−​(g​V)+g​V]​[𝒫−​(g​V)]†​𝑑z,\begin{split}0&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{1}(z)g_{2}(z)\,dz\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{P}^{+}(gV)(z)[\mathcal{P}^{-}(gV)(\bar{z})]^{\dagger}\,dz\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}[\mathcal{P}^{-}(gV)+gV][\mathcal{P}^{-}(gV)]^{\dagger}\,dz,\end{split}(4.22)

where we used the Plemelj formula𝒫+−𝒫−=I\mathcal{P}^{+}-\mathcal{P}^{-}=I.
Also, utilizing the homogeneous assumption𝒫−​(g​V)=g\mathcal{P}^{-}(gV)=g, we deduce0=∫ℝg​(I+V​(x;λ))​g†​𝑑z.0=\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(I+V(x;\lambda))g^{\dagger}\,dz.(4.23)

For everyz∈ℝz\in\mathbb{R}, the real part of the quadratic form associated with the matrixI+V​(x;λ)I+V(x;\lambda)is strictly positive definite by Proposition3.4.
Finally, we conclude thatg=0g=0is the only solution to the homogeneous equation(I−𝒫V−)​g=0(I-\mathcal{P}_{V}^{-})g=0inLz2​(ℝ)L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}).
 ∎

For further estimates, we modify the method of Proposition4.3and prove that the operator(I−𝒫V−)−1(I-\mathcal{P}_{V}^{-})^{-1}in the integral Fredholm equation (4.19) is invertible with a bounded inverse in spaceLz2​(ℝ)L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

## Proposition 4.4.

Suppose that the small norm restriction (3.4) holds. Then the inverse operator(I−𝒫V−)−1(I-\mathcal{P}_{V}^{-})^{-1}is a bounded operator fromLz2​(ℝ)L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})toLz2​(ℝ)L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}).
In particular, there is a positive constantCCsuch that for every row vectord∈Lz2​(ℝ)d\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}), we have‖(I−𝒫V−)−1​d‖Lz2≤C​‖d‖Lz2.\|(I-\mathcal{P}_{V}^{-})^{-1}d\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\leq C\|d\|_{L_{z}^{2}}.(4.24)

## Proof.

We consider the linear inhomogeneous equation (4.19) withD∈Lz2​(ℝ)D\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}). Recalling the Plemelj formula𝒫+−𝒫−=I\mathcal{P}^{+}-\mathcal{P}^{-}=I, we can decompose the solution asG=G+−G−G=G_{+}-G_{-}, whereG+G_{+}andG−G_{-}satisfy the inhomogeneous equationsG−−𝒫−​(G−​V)=𝒫−​(D),G+−𝒫−​(G+​V)=𝒫+​(D).G_{-}-\mathcal{P}^{-}(G_{-}V)=\mathcal{P}^{-}(D),\quad G_{+}-\mathcal{P}^{-}(G_{+}V)=\mathcal{P}^{+}(D).(4.25)

By Proposition4.3, since𝒫±​(D)∈Lz2​(ℝ)\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(D)\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}), there are unique solutions to the inhomogeneous equations (4.25), meaning the decompositionG=G+−G−G=G_{+}-G_{-}is unique. We now estimateG+G_{+}andG−G_{-}inLz2​(ℝ)L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

To deal withG−G_{-}, we define two analytic functions inℂ∖ℝ\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}byg1​(z):=𝒞​(G−​V)​(z)andg2​(z):=(𝒞​(G−​V+D)​(z¯))†,g_{1}(z):=\mathcal{C}(G_{-}V)(z)\quad\text{and}\quad g_{2}(z):=(\mathcal{C}(G_{-}V+D)(\bar{z}))^{\dagger},(4.26)

similarly to the proof of Proposition4.3. By Proposition4.1, sinceD,G−∈Lz2​(ℝ)D,G_{-}\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})andV​(x;λ)∈Lz2​(ℝ)∩Lz∞​(ℝ)V(x;\lambda)\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{z}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}), we haveg1​(z)=𝒪​(z−1)g_{1}(z)=\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})andg2​(z)=𝒪​(z−1)g_{2}(z)=\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})as|z|→∞|z|\to\infty. Therefore, the integral on the semi-circle of radiusR>0R>0in the upper half-plane goes to zero asR→∞R\to\infty. Performing the same contour integration, we obtain0=∮g1​(z)​g2​(z)​𝑑z=∫ℝ𝒫+​(G−​V)​[𝒫−​(G−​V+D)]†​𝑑z=∫ℝ[𝒫−​(G−​V)+G−​V]​[𝒫−​(G−​V+D)]†​𝑑z=∫ℝ[G−−𝒫−​(D)+G−​V]​G−†​𝑑z,\begin{split}0&=\oint g_{1}(z)g_{2}(z)\,dz\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{P}^{+}(G_{-}V)[\mathcal{P}^{-}(G_{-}V+D)]^{\dagger}\,dz\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}[\mathcal{P}^{-}(G_{-}V)+G_{-}V][\mathcal{P}^{-}(G_{-}V+D)]^{\dagger}\,dz\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}[G_{-}-\mathcal{P}^{-}(D)+G_{-}V]G_{-}^{\dagger}\,dz,\end{split}(4.27)

where we have used the first inhomogeneous equation in (4.25). By the coercivity bound (3.19) established in Proposition3.4, there is a positive constantη−\eta_{-}such thatη−​‖G−‖Lz22≤Re​∫ℝG−​(I+V)​G−†​𝑑z=Re​∫ℝ𝒫−​(D)​G−†​𝑑z≤‖𝒫−​(D)‖Lz2​‖G−‖Lz2,\eta_{-}\|G_{-}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}^{2}\leq\mathrm{Re}\int_{\mathbb{R}}G_{-}(I+V)G_{-}^{\dagger}\,dz=\mathrm{Re}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{P}^{-}(D)G_{-}^{\dagger}\,dz\leq\|\mathcal{P}^{-}(D)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\|G_{-}\|_{L_{z}^{2}},(4.28)

where we applied the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. SinceG−=(I−𝒫V−)−1​𝒫−​(D)G_{-}=(I-\mathcal{P}_{V}^{-})^{-1}\mathcal{P}^{-}(D), for every row-vectord∈Lz2​(ℝ)d\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}), the above inequality yields‖(I−𝒫V−)−1​𝒫−​d‖Lz2≤η−−1​‖𝒫−​(d)‖Lz2=η−−1​‖d‖Lz2,\|(I-\mathcal{P}_{V}^{-})^{-1}\mathcal{P}^{-}d\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\leq\eta_{-}^{-1}\|\mathcal{P}^{-}(d)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}=\eta_{-}^{-1}\|d\|_{L_{z}^{2}},(4.29)

using theL2L^{2}-boundedness of the Plemelj projection𝒫−\mathcal{P}^{-}.

To deal withG+G_{+}, we use𝒫+−𝒫−=I\mathcal{P}^{+}-\mathcal{P}^{-}=Iand rewrite the second inhomogeneous equation in (4.25) as:G+​(I+V)−𝒫+​(G+​V)=𝒫+​(D).G_{+}(I+V)-\mathcal{P}^{+}(G_{+}V)=\mathcal{P}^{+}(D).(4.30)

We now define two analytic functions inℂ∖ℝ\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}byh1​(z):=𝒞​(G+​V)​(z)andh2​(z):=𝒞​(G+​V+D)†.h_{1}(z):=\mathcal{C}(G_{+}V)(z)\quad\text{and}\quad h_{2}(z):=\mathcal{C}(G_{+}V+D)^{\dagger}.(4.31)

Integrating the product ofh1h_{1}andh2h_{2}on the semi-circle of radiusR>0R>0in the lower half-plane, and lettingR→∞R\to\infty, we obtain0=∮h1​(z)​h2​(z)​𝑑z=∫ℝ𝒫−​(G+​V)​[𝒫+​(G+​V+D)]†​𝑑z=∫ℝ[G+−𝒫+​(D)]​[G+​(I+V)]†​𝑑z,\begin{split}0&=\oint h_{1}(z)h_{2}(z)\,dz\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{P}^{-}(G_{+}V)[\mathcal{P}^{+}(G_{+}V+D)]^{\dagger}\,dz\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}[G_{+}-\mathcal{P}^{+}(D)][G_{+}(I+V)]^{\dagger}\,dz,\end{split}(4.32)

where we have used equation (4.30). Taking the real part and using bounds (3.19) and (3.20) in Proposition3.4, there are positive constantsη−\eta_{-}andη+\eta_{+}such thatη−​‖G+‖Lz22≤Re​∫ℝG+​(I+V)†​G+†​𝑑z=Re​∫ℝ𝒫+​(D)​(I+V)†​G+†​𝑑z≤η+​‖𝒫+​(D)‖Lz2​‖G+‖Lz2.\begin{split}\eta_{-}\|G_{+}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}^{2}&\leq\mathrm{Re}\int_{\mathbb{R}}G_{+}(I+V)^{\dagger}G_{+}^{\dagger}\,dz\\
&=\mathrm{Re}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{P}^{+}(D)(I+V)^{\dagger}G_{+}^{\dagger}\,dz\leq\eta_{+}\|\mathcal{P}^{+}(D)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\|G_{+}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}.\end{split}(4.33)

SinceG+=(I−𝒫V−)−1​𝒫+​(D)G_{+}=(I-\mathcal{P}_{V}^{-})^{-1}\mathcal{P}^{+}(D), for every row-vectord∈Lz2​(ℝ)d\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}), we get‖(I−𝒫V−)−1​𝒫+​d‖Lz2≤η−−1​η+​‖d‖Lz2.\|(I-\mathcal{P}_{V}^{-})^{-1}\mathcal{P}^{+}d\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\leq\eta_{-}^{-1}\eta_{+}\|d\|_{L_{z}^{2}}.(4.34)

The assertion of the proposition is finally proved by combining bounds (4.29), (4.34), and the triangle inequality.
 ∎
