Then the expression ofGj,±​(x;λ)G_{j,\pm}(x;\lambda)is given byG1,±​(x;λ)=N±​(x;z)​τ1​(λ)−τ1​(λ)=((N±,1​(x;z)−e1),−2​λ​(N±,2​(x;z)−e2)),\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{1,\pm}(x;\lambda)&=N_{\pm}(x;z)\tau_{1}(\lambda)-\tau_{1}(\lambda)\\
&=\left(\left(N_{\pm,1}(x;z)-e_{1}\right),\ -2\lambda\left(N_{\pm,2}(x;z)-e_{2}\right)\right),\end{split}G2,±​(x;λ)=N±​(x;z)​τ2​(λ)−τ2​(λ)=((−2​λ)−1​(N±,1​(x;z)−e1),(N±,2​(x;z)−e2)),\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{2,\pm}(x;\lambda)&=N_{\pm}(x;z)\tau_{2}(\lambda)-\tau_{2}(\lambda)\\
&=\left((-2\lambda)^{-1}\left(N_{\pm,1}(x;z)-e_{1}\right),\ \left(N_{\pm,2}(x;z)-e_{2}\right)\right),\end{split}

andDj​(x;λ):=τj​(λ)​V=R​(x;z)​τj,j=1,2.D_{j}(x;\lambda):=\tau_{j}(\lambda)V=R(x;z)\tau_{j},\ j=1,2.(4.36)

We use the relation(Gj,−​V+Dj)i​1=(N−​τj​V)i​1=(N−​R​τj)i​1,i=1,2,(G_{j,-}V+D_{j})_{i1}=(N_{-}\tau_{j}V)_{i1}=(N_{-}R\tau_{j})_{i1},\quad i=1,2,(4.37)

to obtain the explicit expressions for the first column ofGj,±G_{j,\pm}:N±,1​(x;z)−e1\displaystyle N_{\pm,1}(x;z)-e_{1}=𝒫±​(N−​(x;⋅)​R​(x;⋅))11,z∈ℝ,\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(N_{-}(x;\cdot)R(x;\cdot))_{11},\quad z\in\mathbb{R},(4.38)(−2​λ)−1​(N±,1​(x;z)−e1)\displaystyle(-2\lambda)^{-1}(N_{\pm,1}(x;z)-e_{1})=𝒫±​((−2​λ)−1​N−​(x;⋅)​R​(x;⋅))11,z∈ℝ,\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{\pm}((-2\lambda)^{-1}N_{-}(x;\cdot)R(x;\cdot))_{11},\quad z\in\mathbb{R},(4.39)

and for the second column ofGj,±G_{j,\pm}:N±,2​(x;z)−e2\displaystyle N_{\pm,2}(x;z)-e_{2}=𝒫±​(N−​(x;⋅)​R​(x;⋅))21,z∈ℝ,\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(N_{-}(x;\cdot)R(x;\cdot))_{21},\quad z\in\mathbb{R},(4.40)−2​λ​(N±,2​(x;z)−e2)\displaystyle-2\lambda(N_{\pm,2}(x;z)-e_{2})=𝒫±​(−2​λ​N−​(x;⋅)​R​(x;⋅))21,z∈ℝ.\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(-2\lambda N_{-}(x;\cdot)R(x;\cdot))_{21},\quad z\in\mathbb{R}.(4.41)

## Remark 4.5.

From the results above, (4.38) and (4.39) may seem redundant, as well as (4.40) and (4.41). However, by using operations similar to[49,16], we can see that both are redundant, which we omit the proof here.

The solution to the RH Problem3.5on the real line is given by the integral equationN±​(x;z)=I+𝒫±​(N−​(x;⋅)​R​(x;⋅))​(z),z∈ℝ.N_{\pm}(x;z)=I+\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(N_{-}(x;\cdot)R(x;\cdot))(z),\quad z\in\mathbb{R}.(4.42)

The analytical continuation of functionsN±​(x;⋅)N_{\pm}(x;\cdot)inℂ±\mathbb{C}^{\pm}is given by the Cauchy operatorsN​(x;z)=I+𝒞​(N−​(x;⋅)​R​(x;⋅))​(z),z∈ℂ±.N(x;z)=I+\mathcal{C}(N_{-}(x;\cdot)R(x;\cdot))(z),\quad z\in\mathbb{C}^{\pm}.(4.43)

The corresponding result on solvability of the integral equations (4.42) is given by the following proposition.

## Proposition 4.6.

Supposer1,2​(z)∈H1​(ℝ)∩L2,1​(ℝ)r_{1,2}(z)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})such that the small norm inequality (3.4) is satisfied. Then for everyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R},N±​(x;z)N_{\pm}(x;z)admits the estimate:‖N±​(x;⋅)−I‖L2​(ℝ)≤C​(‖r1‖L2+‖r2‖L2+‖r‖L2+‖r˘‖L2),\|N_{\pm}(x;\cdot)-I\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C(\|r_{1}\|_{L^{2}}+\|r_{2}\|_{L^{2}}+\|r\|_{L^{2}}+\|\breve{r}\|_{L^{2}}),(4.44)

whereCCis a positive constant depending only on‖r1,2‖L∞​(ℝ)\|r_{1,2}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}.

## Proof.

Under the conditionr1,2​(z)∈H1​(ℝ)∩L2,1​(ℝ)r_{1,2}(z)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}),R​(x;z)​τj​(λ)R(x;z)\tau_{j}(\lambda)belongs toLz2​(ℝ)L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})for everyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}according to the explicit expressions ofDjD_{j}. There exists a positive constantCCdependent only on‖r1,2‖L∞\|r_{1,2}\|_{L^{\infty}}such that‖R​(x;⋅)​τj​(⋅)‖Lz2≤C​(‖r1‖L2+‖r2‖L2+‖r‖L2+‖r˘‖L2),j=1,2\|R(x;\cdot)\tau_{j}(\cdot)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\leq C(\|r_{1}\|_{L^{2}}+\|r_{2}\|_{L^{2}}+\|r\|_{L^{2}}+\|\breve{r}\|_{L^{2}}),\quad j=1,2(4.45)

According to𝒫−​(Dj)=𝒫−​(R​(x;⋅)​τj​(⋅))\mathcal{P}^{-}(D_{j})=\mathcal{P}^{-}(R(x;\cdot)\tau_{j}(\cdot)), the equation for the projection operator𝒫−\mathcal{P}^{-}is derived from Proposition4.4. Each element ofN−​(x;z)N_{-}(x;z)satisfies the bound for the corresponding row vectors of𝒫−​(Dj)\mathcal{P}^{-}(D_{j}). Combining the operator bound (4.24) and (4.45), we obtain (4.44).
 ∎

Next, we derive accurate estimates on the solution to the integral equations by employing Fourier theory to evaluate the projection operators on oscillatory terms.
For a given functionf​(z)∈L2​(ℝ)f(z)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}), we define its Fourier transform and inverse transform byf^​(ξ)=12​π​∫ℝf​(z)​e−i​z​ξ​𝑑z,f​(z)=∫ℝf^​(ξ)​ei​z​ξ​𝑑ξ.\hat{f}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(z)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}z\xi}\,dz,\quad f(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{f}(\xi)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z\xi}\,d\xi.(4.46)

## Proposition 4.7.

Iff​(z)∈L2​(ℝ)f(z)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}), we have the following Fourier representations for the Cauchy projections:𝒫−​(f​(z)​e2​i​z​x)\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{-}(f(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})=−∫−∞−2​xf^​(ξ)​ei​z​(ξ+2​x)​𝑑ξ,\displaystyle=-\int_{-\infty}^{-2x}\hat{f}(\xi)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(\xi+2x)}\,d\xi,(4.47)𝒫+​(f​(z)​e−2​i​z​x)\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{+}(f(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx})=∫2​x+∞f^​(ξ)​ei​z​(ξ−2​x)​𝑑ξ.\displaystyle=\int_{2x}^{+\infty}\hat{f}(\xi)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(\xi-2x)}\,d\xi.(4.48)

## Proof.

By using the definition of the projection operator and the Fourier inverse transform, we can expand𝒫+\mathcal{P}^{+}as follows:𝒫+​(f​(z)​e−2​i​z​x)=12​π​i​limϵ↓0∫ℝf​(s)​e−2​i​s​xs−(z+i​ϵ)​𝑑s=12​π​i​∫ℝf^​(ξ)​(limϵ↓0∫ℝei​s​(ξ−2​x)s−(z+i​ϵ)​𝑑s)​𝑑ξ.\begin{split}\mathcal{P}^{+}(f(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx})&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{f(s)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}sx}}{s-(z+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}\,ds\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{f}(\xi)\left(\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s(\xi-2x)}}{s-(z+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}\,ds\right)d\xi.\end{split}(4.49)

Applying the residue theorem to the inner complex integral, the integrand has a simple pole ats=z+i​ϵs=z+\mathrm{i}\epsilonin the upper half-plane. To ensure the integral converges at infinity, the choice of the closed contour depends on the sign ofξ−2​x\xi-2x:limϵ↓012​π​i​∫ℝei​s​(ξ−2​x)s−(z+i​ϵ)​𝑑s=limϵ↓0{ei​(z+i​ϵ)​(ξ−2​x),if​ξ−2​x>00,if​ξ−2​x<0=χ​(ξ−2​x)​ei​z​(ξ−2​x),\begin{array}[]{rl}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\dfrac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}s(\xi-2x)}}{s-(z+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}\,ds&\!\!\!=\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\begin{cases}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(z+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)(\xi-2x)},&\text{if }\xi-2x>0\\
0,&\text{if }\xi-2x<0\end{cases}\vskip 4.33601pt\\
&\!\!\!=\displaystyle\chi(\xi-2x)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(\xi-2x)},\end{array}(4.50)

whereχ​(⋅)\chi(\cdot)is the characteristic function. Substituting (4.50) into (4.49), the characteristic function truncates the lower limit of the integration overξ\xi:𝒫+​(f​(z)​e−2​i​z​x)=∫2​x+∞f^​(ξ)​ei​z​(ξ−2​x)​𝑑ξ.\mathcal{P}^{+}(f(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx})=\int_{2x}^{+\infty}\hat{f}(\xi)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(\xi-2x)}\,d\xi.(4.51)

To evaluate𝒫−\mathcal{P}^{-}, we start with its definition:𝒫−​(f​(z)​e2​i​z​x)=12​π​i​limϵ↓0∫ℝf​(s)​e2​i​s​xs−(z−i​ϵ)​𝑑s.\mathcal{P}^{-}(f(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{f(s)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}sx}}{s-(z-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}\,ds.(4.52)

Taking the complex conjugation on both sides (noting thatx,z,s∈ℝx,z,s\in\mathbb{R}), we find its relation to the𝒫+\mathcal{P}^{+}operator:𝒫−​(f​(z)​e2​i​z​x)¯=−12​π​i​limϵ↓0∫ℝf¯​(s)​e−2​i​s​xs−(z+i​ϵ)​𝑑s=−𝒫+​(f¯​(z)​e−2​i​z​x).\overline{\mathcal{P}^{-}(f(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})}=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\bar{f}(s)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}sx}}{s-(z+\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}\,ds=-\mathcal{P}^{+}(\bar{f}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}).(4.53)

Applying the previously derived formula (4.51) to−𝒫+​(f¯​(z)​e−2​i​z​x)-\mathcal{P}^{+}(\bar{f}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}), and utilizing the Fourier conjugate propertyf¯^​(ξ)=f^​(−ξ)¯\hat{\bar{f}}(\xi)=\overline{\hat{f}(-\xi)}, we obtain:−𝒫+​(f¯​(z)​e−2​i​z​x)=−∫2​x+∞f¯^​(ξ)​ei​z​(ξ−2​x)​𝑑ξ.-\mathcal{P}^{+}(\bar{f}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx})=-\int_{2x}^{+\infty}\hat{\bar{f}}(\xi)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(\xi-2x)}\,d\xi.(4.54)

which means𝒫−​(f​(z)​e2​i​z​x)=−∫2​x+∞f^​(−ξ)​e−i​z​(ξ−2​x)​𝑑ξ=−∫−∞−2​xf^​(ξ)​ei​z​(ξ+2​x)​𝑑ξ.\mathcal{P}^{-}(f(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})=-\int_{2x}^{+\infty}\hat{f}(-\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}z(\xi-2x)}\,d\xi=-\int_{-\infty}^{-2x}\hat{f}(\xi)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(\xi+2x)}\,d\xi.

∎

## Proposition 4.8.

For everyx0∈ℝ+x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}and everyr1,2​(z)∈𝒲​(ℝ)r_{1,2}(z)\in\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R}), the reflection coefficients satisfy the following spatial weighted estimates forℓ=0,1\ell=0,1:supx∈(x0,∞)‖⟨x⟩​𝒫−​(z−ℓ​r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x)‖Lz2​(ℝ)\displaystyle\sup_{x\in(x_{0},\infty)}\left\|\langle x\rangle\mathcal{P}^{-}(z^{-\ell}r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})\right\|_{L^{2}_{z}(\mathbb{R})}≤‖z−ℓ​r1​(z)‖Hz1​(ℝ),\displaystyle\leq\|z^{-\ell}r_{1}(z)\|_{H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})},(4.55)supx∈(x0,∞)‖⟨x⟩​𝒫+​(r2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x)‖Lz2​(ℝ)\displaystyle\sup_{x\in(x_{0},\infty)}\left\|\langle x\rangle\mathcal{P}^{+}(r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx})\right\|_{L^{2}_{z}(\mathbb{R})}≤‖r2​(z)‖Hz1​(ℝ).\displaystyle\leq\|r_{2}(z)\|_{H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})}.(4.56)

In addition, we have the followingL∞L^{\infty}bounds on the entire real line:supx∈ℝ‖𝒫−​(z−ℓ​r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x)‖Lz∞\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{P}^{-}(z^{-\ell}r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}≤12​‖z−ℓ​r1​(z)‖Hz1​(ℝ),\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\|z^{-\ell}r_{1}(z)\|_{H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})},(4.57)supx∈ℝ‖𝒫+​(r2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x)‖Lz∞\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{P}^{+}(r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx})\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}≤12​‖r2​(z)‖Hz1​(ℝ).\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\|r_{2}(z)\|_{H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})}.(4.58)

Furthermore, ifr1,2​(z)∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)r_{1,2}(z)\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), then the following estimates hold:supx∈ℝ‖𝒫−​(z​r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x)‖Lz2​(ℝ)\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{P}^{-}(zr_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})\right\|_{L^{2}_{z}(\mathbb{R})}≤‖r1​(z)‖Lz2,1​(ℝ),\displaystyle\leq\|r_{1}(z)\|_{L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})},(4.59)supx∈ℝ‖𝒫+​(z​r2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x)‖Lz2​(ℝ)\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{P}^{+}(zr_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx})\right\|_{L^{2}_{z}(\mathbb{R})}≤‖r2​(z)‖Lz2,1​(ℝ).\displaystyle\leq\|r_{2}(z)\|_{L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})}.(4.60)

## Proof.

The proof follows from the argument of[16]. We employ the Fourier representations of the Plemelj projections derived in the previous proposition. For a given reflection coefficientr​(z)∈L2​(ℝ)r(z)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}), its Fourier transform satisfiesr^​(ξ)∈L2​(ℝ)\hat{r}(\xi)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}). According to the classical Fourier theory,r​(z)∈H1​(ℝ)r(z)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})if and only ifr^​(ξ)∈L2,1​(ℝ)\hat{r}(\xi)\in L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), and similarly,r​(z)∈L2,1​(ℝ)r(z)\in L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})if and only ifr^​(ξ)∈H1​(ℝ)\hat{r}(\xi)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}).

To establish the weighted estimate (4.55), we use the explicit Fourier representation for𝒫−\mathcal{P}^{-}:𝒫−​(z−ℓ​r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x)=−∫−∞−2​x(z−ℓ​r1)^​(ξ)​ei​z​(ξ+2​x)​𝑑ξ.\mathcal{P}^{-}(z^{-\ell}r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})=-\int_{-\infty}^{-2x}\widehat{(z^{-\ell}r_{1})}(\xi)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(\xi+2x)}\,d\xi.(4.61)

By applying the integral bound (2.30) from Proposition2.3to the right-hand side of (4.61), the spatial weight⟨x⟩\langle x\rangleon the positive half-linex∈(x0,∞)x\in(x_{0},\infty)is directly bounded by theL2,1L^{2,1}-norm of the Fourier transform:supx∈(x0,∞)‖⟨x⟩​∫−∞−2​x(z−ℓ​r1)^​(ξ)​ei​z​(ξ+2​x)​𝑑ξ‖Lz2​(ℝ)≤2​π​‖(z−ℓ​r1)^​(ξ)‖L2,1​(ℝ).\sup_{x\in(x_{0},\infty)}\left\|\langle x\rangle\int_{-\infty}^{-2x}\widehat{(z^{-\ell}r_{1})}(\xi)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z(\xi+2x)}\,d\xi\right\|_{L^{2}_{z}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\sqrt{2\pi}\left\|\widehat{(z^{-\ell}r_{1})}(\xi)\right\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})}.(4.62)

Using the Plancherel identity, theL2,1L^{2,1}-norm of(z−ℓ​r1)^​(ξ)\widehat{(z^{-\ell}r_{1})}(\xi)is equivalent to theH1H^{1}-norm ofz−ℓ​r1​(z)z^{-\ell}r_{1}(z)in the spectral domain, which yields the desired bound (4.55).

The estimate (4.56) for𝒫+\mathcal{P}^{+}follows via an identical routine by deploying the Fourier integration up to the lower limit2​x2xon the positive half-linex∈(x0,∞)x\in(x_{0},\infty).

To prove theL∞L^{\infty}bound (4.57), we take the absolute value of the Fourier representation (4.61) and apply the Hölder’s inequality:|𝒫−​(z−ℓ​r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x)|≤∫−∞−2​x|(z−ℓ​r1)^​(ξ)|​𝑑ξ≤‖(z−ℓ​r1)^​(ξ)‖L1​(ℝ).\left|\mathcal{P}^{-}(z^{-\ell}r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})\right|\leq\int_{-\infty}^{-2x}\left|\widehat{(z^{-\ell}r_{1})}(\xi)\right|\,d\xi\leq\left\|\widehat{(z^{-\ell}r_{1})}(\xi)\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}.(4.63)

By using the standard Sobolev embedding inequality‖f^‖L1≤12​‖f‖H1\|\hat{f}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\|f\|_{H^{1}}, we immediately obtain:supx∈ℝ‖𝒫−​(z−ℓ​r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x)‖Lz∞≤12​‖z−ℓ​r1​(z)‖Hz1​(ℝ),\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{P}^{-}(z^{-\ell}r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{z}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\|z^{-\ell}r_{1}(z)\|_{H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})},(4.64)

which proves (4.57). The companion bound (4.58) is proved in the same manner.

Finally, the assertions (4.59) and (4.60) characterize the dual mapping properties when the reflection coefficients belong to the weighted spaceL2,1​(ℝ)L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}). By swapping the roles of the spaces under the Plancherel transform, theLz2L^{2}_{z}-norm of the projected derivative terms𝒫−​(z​r1​e2​i​z​x)\mathcal{P}^{-}(zr_{1}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})and𝒫+​(z​r2​e−2​i​z​x)\mathcal{P}^{+}(zr_{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx})is uniformly bounded by theH1H^{1}-norm of their Fourier counterparts, which corresponds exactly to the initialL2,1L^{2,1}-norm ofr1,2​(z)r_{1,2}(z).
 ∎

Our ultimate goal is to estimate the solutionsN​(x;z)N(x;z)to the RH Problem3.5. By Proposition4.6, these solutions on the real line can be written in the integral Fredholm form (4.42). One remaining task is to estimate the column vectorsN+,1−e1N_{+,1}-e_{1}andN−,2−e2N_{-,2}-e_{2}. From the equation (4.38), we obtainN+,1​(x;z)−e1=𝒫+​(r2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​N−,2​(x;z)),z∈ℝ.N_{+,1}(x;z)-e_{1}=\mathcal{P}^{+}\left(r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{-,2}(x;z)\right),\quad z\in\mathbb{R}.(4.65)

Also, starting from (4.40), we knowN−,2​(x;z)−e2=𝒫−​(r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x​N+,1​(x;z)),z∈ℝ.N_{-,2}(x;z)-e_{2}=\mathcal{P}^{-}\left(r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{+,1}(x;z)\right),\quad z\in\mathbb{R}.(4.66)

Both calculations take advantage of the explicit expressions for the columns ofN−​RN_{-}R:(N−​R)1=r2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​N−,2,(N−​R)2=r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x​N+,1.(N_{-}R)_{1}=r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{-,2},\quad(N_{-}R)_{2}=r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{+,1}.(4.67)

From the explicit expression (4.9) forF​(x;z)F(x;z), we note that the first row vector ofF​(x;z)F(x;z)is equal to the first row ofF​(x;z)​τ2​(λ)F(x;z)\tau_{2}(\lambda)and the second row vector ofF​(x;z)F(x;z)is equal to the second row ofF​(x;z)​τ1​(λ)F(x;z)\tau_{1}(\lambda), which have the following representations:F1​(x;z)\displaystyle F_{1}(x;z)=(F​(x;z)​τ2)1=(0,𝒫−​(r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x)),\displaystyle=(F(x;z)\tau_{2})_{1}=\left(0,\mathcal{P}^{-}(r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})\right),(4.68)F2​(x;z)\displaystyle F_{2}(x;z)=(F​(x;z)​τ1)2=(𝒫+​(r2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x),0).\displaystyle=(F(x;z)\tau_{1})_{2}=\left(\mathcal{P}^{+}(r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}),0\right).(4.69)

## Proposition 4.9.

For everyx0∈ℝ+x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}and everyr1,2∈H1​(ℝ)r_{1,2}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}), the unique solution satisfies the estimatessupx∈(x0,∞)‖⟨x⟩​N+,21​(x;⋅)‖L2​(ℝ)\displaystyle\sup_{x\in(x_{0},\infty)}\|\langle x\rangle N_{+,21}(x;\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}≤C​‖r2‖H1​(ℝ),\displaystyle\leq C\|r_{2}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})},(4.70)supx∈(x0,∞)‖⟨x⟩​N−,12​(x;⋅)‖L2​(ℝ)\displaystyle\sup_{x\in(x_{0},\infty)}\|\langle x\rangle N_{-,12}(x;\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}≤C​‖r1‖H1​(ℝ),\displaystyle\leq C\|r_{1}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})},(4.71)

whereCCis a positive constant depending on‖r1,2‖L∞\|r_{1,2}\|_{L^{\infty}}.
Moreover, ifr1,2∈H1​(ℝ)∩L2,1​(ℝ)r_{1,2}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), we have the derivative bounds:supx∈ℝ‖∂xN+,21​(x;⋅)‖L2​(ℝ)\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\|\partial_{x}N_{+,21}(x;\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}≤C​(‖r1‖H1∩L2,1+‖r2‖H1∩L2,1),\displaystyle\leq C(\|r_{1}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}}+\|r_{2}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}}),(4.72)supx∈ℝ‖∂xN−,12​(x;⋅)‖L2​(ℝ)\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\|\partial_{x}N_{-,12}(x;\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}≤C​(‖r1‖H1∩L2,1+‖r2‖H1∩L2,1).\displaystyle\leq C(\|r_{1}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}}+\|r_{2}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}}).(4.73)

## Proof.

From the explicit expression (4.6), the first row vector ofT​(x;z)​τ2​(λ)T(x;z)\tau_{2}(\lambda)is given by((−2​λ)−1​(N+,11​(x;z)−1),N−,12​(x;z)),\left((-2\lambda)^{-1}(N_{+,11}(x;z)-1),\ N_{-,12}(x;z)\right),(4.74)

and the second row vector ofT​(x;z)​τ1​(λ)T(x;z)\tau_{1}(\lambda)is given by(N+,21​(x;z),−2​λ​(N−,22​(x;z)−1)).\left(N_{+,21}(x;z),\ -2\lambda(N_{-,22}(x;z)-1)\right).(4.75)

Using the operator bound (4.24) along with the expressions ofF1​(x;z)F_{1}(x;z)andF2​(x;z)F_{2}(x;z)in (4.68) and (4.69), we map the bounds of the projection operators in Proposition4.8directly onto the vector components.
Specifically, from the explicit component equations, we have for everyx∈ℝx\in\mathbb{R}:‖N+,21​(x;⋅)‖Lz2\displaystyle\|N_{+,21}(x;\cdot)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}≤C​‖𝒫+​(r2​e−2​i​z​x)‖Lz2,\displaystyle\leq C\|\mathcal{P}^{+}(r_{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx})\|_{L_{z}^{2}},(4.76)‖(−2​λ)−1​(N+,11​(x;⋅)−1)‖Lz2\displaystyle\|(-2\lambda)^{-1}(N_{+,11}(x;\cdot)-1)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}≤C​‖𝒫−​(r1​e2​i​z​x)‖Lz2,\displaystyle\leq C\|\mathcal{P}^{-}(r_{1}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})\|_{L_{z}^{2}},(4.77)‖−2​λ​(N−,22​(x;⋅)−1)‖Lz2\displaystyle\|-2\lambda(N_{-,22}(x;\cdot)-1)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}≤C​‖𝒫+​(r2​e−2​i​z​x)‖Lz2,\displaystyle\leq C\|\mathcal{P}^{+}(r_{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx})\|_{L_{z}^{2}},(4.78)‖N−,12​(x;⋅)‖Lz2\displaystyle\|N_{-,12}(x;\cdot)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}≤C​‖𝒫−​(r1​e2​i​z​x)‖Lz2.\displaystyle\leq C\|\mathcal{P}^{-}(r_{1}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx})\|_{L_{z}^{2}}.(4.79)

where the positive constantCCstill has the only dependence on‖r1,2‖L∞\|r_{1,2}\|_{L^{\infty}}.
Applying the spatial weighted estimates (4.56) and (4.55) onto the positive half-linex∈(x0,∞)x\in(x_{0},\infty)directly yields the weighted bounds (4.70) and (4.71).

For estimating the spatial derivatives∂xN±\partial_{x}N_{\pm}, we refer to the differentiated inhomogeneous equation (4.10).
In order to exploit the uniform bounds from the resolvent operator(I−𝒫V−)−1(I-\mathcal{P}_{V}^{-})^{-1}, we multiplyF~​(x;z)\tilde{F}(x;z)by the scaling matricesτ1​(λ)\tau_{1}(\lambda)andτ2​(λ)\tau_{2}(\lambda)from the right.
Specifically, we extract the second row ofF~​(x;z)​τ1​(λ)\tilde{F}(x;z)\tau_{1}(\lambda)and the first row ofF~​(x;z)​τ2​(λ)\tilde{F}(x;z)\tau_{2}(\lambda)to show they securely belong toLz2​(ℝ)L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}).
Let us examine the second row ofF~​(x;z)​τ1​(λ)\tilde{F}(x;z)\tau_{1}(\lambda):(F~​τ1)2=(F~21​(x;z),−2​λ​F~22​(x;z)).(\tilde{F}\tau_{1})_{2}=\left(\tilde{F}_{21}(x;z),\ -2\lambda\tilde{F}_{22}(x;z)\right).(4.80)

By (4.11), we haveF~21=2​i​𝒫+​(−z​r2​e−2​i​z​x)+2​i​𝒫+​(−z​r2​(N−,22−1)​e−2​i​z​x)\tilde{F}_{21}=2\mathrm{i}\mathcal{P}^{+}(-zr_{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx})+2\mathrm{i}\mathcal{P}^{+}(-zr_{2}(N_{-,22}-1)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}).
The boundedness of𝒫+\mathcal{P}^{+}onLz2​(ℝ)L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})gives:‖F~21‖Lz2≤C​(‖z​r2‖Lz2+‖z​r2​(N−,22−1)‖Lz2).\|\tilde{F}_{21}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\leq C\left(\|zr_{2}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}+\|zr_{2}(N_{-,22}-1)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\right).(4.81)

Sincer2∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)r_{2}\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}), the first term is bounded by‖r2‖L2,1\|r_{2}\|_{L^{2,1}}.
For the second term, we strategically rewritez​r2​(N−,22−1)=−12​(λ​r2)​[−2​λ​(N−,22−1)]zr_{2}(N_{-,22}-1)=-\frac{1}{2}(\lambda r_{2})[-2\lambda(N_{-,22}-1)].
By Proposition3.12,‖λ​r2‖L∞≤‖r2‖H1∩L2,1\|\lambda r_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|r_{2}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}}. Coupled with theLz2L_{z}^{2}estimate in (4.78), this yields:‖z​r2​(N−,22−1)‖Lz2≤12​‖λ​r2‖L∞​‖−2​λ​(N−,22−1)‖Lz2≤C​‖r2‖H1∩L2,12.\|zr_{2}(N_{-,22}-1)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\lambda r_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|-2\lambda(N_{-,22}-1)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\leq C\|r_{2}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}}^{2}.(4.82)

Thus,F~21∈Lz2​(ℝ)\tilde{F}_{21}\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

For the second component−2​λ​F~22-2\lambda\tilde{F}_{22}, we analyze the equivalent formulation via the jump matrixV​(x;λ)V(x;\lambda)andD1=τ1​VD_{1}=\tau_{1}V.

Therefore, the combined norms of these terms are rigorously controlled by:‖(F~​τ1)2‖Lz2≤C​(‖r1‖H1∩L2,1+‖r2‖H1∩L2,1).\|(\tilde{F}\tau_{1})_{2}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\leq C(\|r_{1}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}}+\|r_{2}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}}).(4.83)

A completely analogous expansion and bounding procedure applied to the first row ofF~​(x;z)​τ2​(λ)\tilde{F}(x;z)\tau_{2}(\lambda)confirms that it also belongs toLz2​(ℝ)L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})and is bounded by identical norms.

## 4.3Reconstruction formulas

Recalling the asymptotic expansions of the Jost solutions in Proposition2.2, we can extract the potentialq​(x)q(x)from the large-zzbehavior of the RH problem solutions. Specifically, using the definition ofN​(x;z)N(x;z)in (3.30) and the limits (2.17), we have forz∈ℂ−z\in\mathbb{C}^{-}:lim|z|→∞z​N−,21​(x;z)=lim|z|→∞z​ei2​m+​(x)​Ψ21−​(x;z)a​(λ)=ei2​m+​(x)a∞​s2−​(x),\lim_{|z|\to\infty}zN_{-,21}(x;z)=\lim_{|z|\to\infty}z\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(x)}\frac{\Psi_{21}^{-}(x;z)}{a(\lambda)}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(x)}}{a_{\infty}}s_{2}^{-}(x),(4.84)

wherea∞=ei2​Ma_{\infty}=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}M}andM=m−​(x)−m+​(x)=∫−∞+∞qy​(qP​T)y​𝑑yM=m_{-}(x)-m_{+}(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}q_{y}(q^{PT})_{y}\,dy. Thus,ei2​m+​(x)/a∞=e−i2​m−​(x)+i​m+​(x)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(x)}/a_{\infty}=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)+\mathrm{i}m_{+}(x)}.
Substituting the explicit expression fors2−​(x)=−i2​∂x(qx​(x)​ei2​m−​(x))s_{2}^{-}(x)=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\partial_{x}(q_{x}(x)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}), we obtain the reconstruction formula:ei​m+​(x)−i2​m−​(x)​∂x(qx​(x)​ei2​m−​(x))=2​i​lim|z|→∞z​N−,21​(x;z).\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}m_{+}(x)-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}\partial_{x}\left(q_{x}(x)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}\right)=2\mathrm{i}\lim_{|z|\to\infty}zN_{-,21}(x;z).(4.85)

At the same time, from (2.84), we find thatqP​T​(x)=limλ→0((i​λ)−1​ψ​(x;λ))12q^{PT}(x)=\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left((\mathrm{i}\lambda)^{-1}\psi(x;\lambda)\right)_{12}(4.86)

From the construction of RH problem3.5and the transformation (2.5), we can getqP​T​(x)​e−i2​m+​(x)=2​i​limz→0N±,12​(x;z).q^{PT}(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(x)}=2\mathrm{i}\lim_{z\to 0}N_{\pm,12}(x;z).(4.87)

In fact, we cannot get any more information from the RH problems3.5,4.2, and the reconstruction formulas (4.85) and (4.87) are not sufficient to recover the potentialq​(x)q(x)on the whole lineℝ\mathbb{R}. So we will first introduce the scalar RH problemδ​(z)\delta(z)to transform the jump matrixR​(x;z)R(x;z)of the RH problem3.5to obtain more information.

## Riemann-Hilbert Problem 4.10.

Find a scalar functionδ​(z)\delta(z)that satisfies the following conditions:
- 1.

Analyticity:δ​(z)\delta(z)is analytic inℂ∖ℝ\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}.
- 2.

Jump condition:δ​(z)\delta(z)has continuous boundary valuesδ±​(z)\delta_{\pm}(z)onℝ\mathbb{R}and satisfiesδ+​(z)=δ−​(z)​(1+r1​(z)​r2​(z))−1,z∈ℝ.\delta_{+}(z)=\delta_{-}(z)(1+r_{1}(z)r_{2}(z))^{-1},\quad z\in\mathbb{R}.(4.88)
- 3.

Asymptotic condition:δ​(z)→1as|z|→∞.\delta(z)\to 1\quad\text{as}\quad|z|\to\infty.

Under the small norm restriction (3.4), Proposition3.2establishes that|r1​(z)​r2​(z)|≤c02<1|r_{1}(z)r_{2}(z)|\leq c_{0}^{2}<1. Thus,1+r1​(z)​r2​(z)1+r_{1}(z)r_{2}(z)has a strictly positive real part and does not vanish onℝ\mathbb{R}. This allows us to define a single-valued branch forln⁡(1+r1​(z)​r2​(z))\ln(1+r_{1}(z)r_{2}(z)). By utilizing the Cauchy projection operators, the unique solution to RH Problem4.10is given by:δ​(z)=e−𝒞​(ln⁡(1+r1​r2))​(z),z∈ℂ∖ℝ.\delta(z)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathcal{C}(\ln(1+r_{1}r_{2}))(z)},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}.(4.89)

The boundary limits on the real axis are:δ±​(z)=e−𝒫±​(ln⁡(1+r1​r2))​(z),z∈ℝ.\delta_{\pm}(z)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathcal{P}^{\pm}(\ln(1+r_{1}r_{2}))(z)},\quad z\in\mathbb{R}.(4.90)

By an analysis similar to the Cauchy operator properties discussed previously, sinceln⁡(1+r1​r2)∈Hz1​(ℝ)\ln(1+r_{1}r_{2})\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}), we can establish thatδ±​(z)∈Lz∞​(ℝ)\delta_{\pm}(z)\in L_{z}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}).

Next, we useδ​(z)\delta(z)to define the transformed matrix function:Nδ​(x;z):=N​(x;z)​δ−σ3​(z)=N​(x;z)​(δ−1​(z)00δ​(z)).N_{\delta}(x;z):=N(x;z)\delta^{-\sigma_{3}}(z)=N(x;z)\begin{pmatrix}\delta^{-1}(z)&0\\
0&\delta(z)\end{pmatrix}.(4.91)

This transformation yields a new RH problem.

## Riemann-Hilbert Problem 4.11.

Find a matrix-valued functionNδ​(x;z)N_{\delta}(x;z)that satisfies the following conditions:
- 1.

Analyticity:Nδ​(x;z)N_{\delta}(x;z)is analytic inℂ∖ℝ\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}.
- 2.

Jump condition:Nδ​(x;z)N_{\delta}(x;z)has continuous boundary valuesNδ,±​(x;z)N_{\delta,\pm}(x;z)onℝ\mathbb{R}andNδ,+​(x;z)=Nδ,−​(x;z)​(I+Rδ​(x;z)),z∈ℝ,N_{\delta,+}(x;z)=N_{\delta,-}(x;z)(I+R_{\delta}(x;z)),\quad z\in\mathbb{R},(4.92)

where the modified jump matrix is derived asI+Rδ​(x;z)=δ−σ3​(z)​(I+R​(x;z))​δ+−σ3​(z)I+R_{\delta}(x;z)=\delta_{-}^{\sigma_{3}}(z)(I+R(x;z))\delta_{+}^{-\sigma_{3}}(z). Substitutingδ+=δ−​(1+r1​r2)−1\delta_{+}=\delta_{-}(1+r_{1}r_{2})^{-1}, we obtain:Rδ​(x;z)=(rδ,1​(z)​rδ,2​(z)rδ,1​(z)​e2​i​z​xrδ,2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x0).R_{\delta}(x;z)=\begin{pmatrix}r_{\delta,1}(z)r_{\delta,2}(z)&r_{\delta,1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\vskip 4.33601pt\\
r_{\delta,2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}&0\end{pmatrix}.(4.93)
- 3.

Asymptotic condition:Nδ​(x;z)→Ias|z|→∞.N_{\delta}(x;z)\to I\quad\text{as}\quad|z|\to\infty.

Here, the new effective reflection coefficients are defined as:rδ,1​(z):=δ+​(z)​δ−​(z)​r1​(z),rδ,2​(z):=δ+−1​(z)​δ−−1​(z)​r2​(z).r_{\delta,1}(z):=\delta_{+}(z)\delta_{-}(z)r_{1}(z),\quad r_{\delta,2}(z):=\delta_{+}^{-1}(z)\delta_{-}^{-1}(z)r_{2}(z).(4.94)

Then we can get the proposition that

Substituting (4.100)–(4.101)
into the Beals–Coifman representation givesNδ,−,1​(x;z)−e1\displaystyle N_{\delta,-,1}(x;z)-e_{1}=𝒫−​(rδ,2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​Nδ,+,2​(x;z)),\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{-}\left(r_{\delta,2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{\delta,+,2}(x;z)\right),(4.102)Nδ,+,2​(x;z)−e2\displaystyle N_{\delta,+,2}(x;z)-e_{2}=𝒫+​(rδ,1​(z)​e2​i​z​x​Nδ,−,1​(x;z)).\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{+}\left(r_{\delta,1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{\delta,-,1}(x;z)\right).(4.103)

## Proposition 4.14.

Suppose that the small norm condition (3.4) holds andrδ,1,rδ,2∈Hz1​(ℝ)∩Lz2,1​(ℝ).r_{\delta,1},r_{\delta,2}\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}).Then, for everyx0∈ℝ−x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{-},supx∈(−∞,x0)‖⟨x⟩​Nδ,−,21​(x;⋅)‖Lz2\displaystyle\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\left\|\langle x\rangle N_{\delta,-,21}(x;\cdot)\right\|_{L_{z}^{2}}≤C​‖rδ,2‖Hz1,\displaystyle\leq C\|r_{\delta,2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}},(4.110)supx∈(−∞,x0)‖⟨x⟩​Nδ,+,12​(x;⋅)‖Lz2\displaystyle\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\left\|\langle x\rangle N_{\delta,+,12}(x;\cdot)\right\|_{L_{z}^{2}}≤C​‖rδ,1‖Hz1.\displaystyle\leq C\|r_{\delta,1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}.(4.111)

Moreover,supx∈ℝ‖∂xNδ,−,21​(x;⋅)‖Lz2+supx∈ℝ‖∂xNδ,+,12​(x;⋅)‖Lz2≤C​(‖rδ,1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖rδ,2‖Hz1∩Lz2,1).\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\partial_{x}N_{\delta,-,21}(x;\cdot)\right\|_{L_{z}^{2}}+\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\partial_{x}N_{\delta,+,12}(x;\cdot)\right\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\leq C\left(\|r_{\delta,1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|r_{\delta,2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}\right).(4.112)

## Proof.

The proof is parallel to that of Proposition4.9.
The only change is the choice of columns. In the original RH problem,
the useful pair is(N+,1−e1,N−,2−e2).(N_{+,1}-e_{1},\;N_{-,2}-e_{2}).For the transformed RH problem, the useful pair is(Nδ,−,1−e1,Nδ,+,2−e2).(N_{\delta,-,1}-e_{1},\;N_{\delta,+,2}-e_{2}).Equations (4.102) and
(4.103) reduce the desired estimates to the
projection estimates
(4.104)–(4.107).
The boundedness of the corresponding Beals–Coifman resolvent follows
from the same coercivity argument as before, sincerδ,1​(z)​rδ,2​(z)=r1​(z)​r2​(z).r_{\delta,1}(z)r_{\delta,2}(z)=r_{1}(z)r_{2}(z).This gives (4.110) and
(4.111). The proofs for the remaining parts are similar with Proposition4.9.
 ∎

We now derive the reconstruction formulae associated with the transformed

the large-zzreconstruction formula is unchanged at leading order.
Using (4.100) and the asymptotic formula for the Cauchy
operator, we obtainei​m+​(x)−i2​m−​(x)​∂x(qx​(x)​ei2​m−​(x))=−1π​∫ℝrδ,2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​Nδ,+,22​(x;z)​𝑑z.\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}m_{+}(x)-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}\partial_{x}\left(q_{x}(x)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}\right)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{\delta,2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{\delta,+,22}(x;z)\,dz.(4.114)

Nearz=0z=0, however, the factorδ\deltacontributes to the
reconstruction formula. Indeed, fromNδ=N​δ−σ3,N_{\delta}=N\delta^{-\sigma_{3}},

we haveN12​(x;z)=δ−1​(z)​Nδ,12​(x;z).N_{12}(x;z)=\delta^{-1}(z)N_{\delta,12}(x;z).

Therefore, using the++-boundary value and
(4.103), we getqP​T​(x)​e−i2​m+​(x)=δ+−1​(0)π​∫ℝz−1​rδ,1​(z)​e2​i​z​x​Nδ,−,11​(x;z)​𝑑z.q^{PT}(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(x)}=\frac{\delta_{+}^{-1}(0)}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}z^{-1}r_{\delta,1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{\delta,-,11}(x;z)\,dz.(4.115)

We can easily see thatδ+−1​(0)\delta_{+}^{-1}(0)can be controlled by theH1H_{1}norm ofr1,2r_{1,2}. And one can find that under (3.4), the exponential factor can be controlled and is a fixed bounded value. Now, we can give estimates on the wholeℝ\mathbb{R}.

## Proposition 4.15.

Assume that the small norm condition (3.4) holds andr1,r2∈𝒲​(ℝ).r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R}).Then the potential reconstructed from the RH problem satisfiesq∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ).q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}).Moreover,‖q‖H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)≤C​(‖r1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖r2‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖z−1​r1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1)≤C​(‖r1‖𝒲​(ℝ)+‖r2‖𝒲​(ℝ)),\displaystyle\|q\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C\left(\|r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|r_{2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|z^{-1}r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}\right)\leq C\left(\|r_{1}\|_{\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})}+\|r_{2}\|_{\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})}\right),(4.116)

whereCCdepends only on the small norm bound and on the size ofr1,r2r_{1},r_{2}inHz1∩Lz2,1H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}.

## Proof.

We first record a simple consequence of the small norm condition
(3.4), which will be used repeatedly below. Sincem±​(x)=∫±∞xqy​(y)​(qP​T)y​(y),d​ym_{\pm}(x)=\int_{\pm\infty}^{x}q_{y}(y)(q^{PT})_{y}(y),dy

and the entry(qx​(qP​T)x)(q_{x}(q^{PT})_{x})is contained in the effective Volterra potentialQ~\tilde{Q}, we have‖e±i2​m±‖Lx∞+‖e±i​m±‖Lx∞≤Cρ,ρ=|Q~|L1<ln⁡32.\|\mathrm{e}^{\pm\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{\pm}}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}+\|\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}m_{\pm}}\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}\leq C_{\rho},\qquad\rho=|\tilde{Q}|_{L^{1}}<\ln\frac{3}{2}.(4.117)

In particular, all exponential factors appearing in the reconstruction
formulae may be absorbed into the constants.

Next, by Proposition4.12and the proof of the scalar
RH problem forδ\delta, we also have‖δ±±1‖Lz∞+|δ+−1​(0)|≤Cρ.\|\delta_{\pm}^{\pm 1}\|_{L_{z}^{\infty}}+|\delta_{+}^{-1}(0)|\leq C_{\rho}.(4.118)

Indeed, ifg​(z):=ln⁡(1+r1​(z)​r2​(z)),g(z):=\ln(1+r_{1}(z)r_{2}(z)),

then the small norm condition gives|r1​r2|≤c02<1|r_{1}r_{2}|\leq c_{0}^{2}<1, and henceg∈Hz1​(ℝ)g\in H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R}). Sinceδ±​(z)=e−𝒫±​g​(z),\displaystyle\delta_{\pm}(z)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathcal{P}^{\pm}g(z)},(4.119)

the Sobolev embeddingHz1​(ℝ)↪Lz∞​(ℝ)H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow L_{z}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})and the boundedness of the Plemelj projections give‖δ±±1‖Lz∞≤Cρ.\displaystyle\|\delta_{\pm}^{\pm 1}\|_{L_{z}^{\infty}}\leq C_{\rho}.(4.120)

Consequently,‖rδ,1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖rδ,2‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖z−1​rδ,1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1≤Cρ​(‖r1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖r2‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖z−1​r1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1).\|r_{\delta,1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|r_{\delta,2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|z^{-1}r_{\delta,1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}\leq C_{\rho}\left(\|r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|r_{2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|z^{-1}r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}\right).(4.121)

We first estimateqP​Tq^{PT}on the positive half-line. By the
Beals–Coifman representation (4.5), the reconstruction
formula (4.87), and the Cauchy asymptotic formula
(4.3), we obtainqP​T​(x)​e−i2​m+​(x)\displaystyle q^{PT}(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(x)}=1π​∫ℝz−1​(N−​R)12​(x;z)​𝑑z\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}z^{-1}(N_{-}R)_{12}(x;z)\,dz=1π​∫ℝz−1​r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x​N+,11​(x;z)​𝑑z\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}z^{-1}r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{+,11}(x;z)\,dz=:I1(x)+I2(x),\displaystyle=:I_{1}(x)+I_{2}(x),(4.122)

whereI1​(x)\displaystyle I_{1}(x):=1π​∫ℝz−1​r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x​𝑑z,\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}z^{-1}r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\,dz,(4.123)I2​(x)\displaystyle I_{2}(x):=1π​∫ℝz−1​r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x​(N+,11​(x;z)−1)​𝑑z.\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}z^{-1}r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\left(N_{+,11}(x;z)-1\right)\,dz.(4.124)

By Plancherel’s theorem,‖I1‖L2,1​(ℝ+)≤C​‖z−1​r1‖Hz1.\|I_{1}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|z^{-1}r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}.(4.125)

ForI2I_{2}, from (4.5) and (4.67),N+,11​(x;z)−1=𝒫+​(r2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​N−,12​(x;z)).N_{+,11}(x;z)-1=\mathcal{P}^{+}\left(r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{-,12}(x;z)\right).

Using the adjoint relation of the Plemelj projections, we rewriteI2​(x)=−1π​∫ℝr2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​N−,12​(x;z)​𝒫−​(z−1​r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x)​(z)​𝑑z.I_{2}(x)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{-,12}(x;z)\mathcal{P}^{-}\left(z^{-1}r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\right)(z)\,dz.(4.126)

Therefore, by Cauchy’s inequality, Propositions4.9and4.8, for everyx0∈ℝ+x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+},supx∈(x0,∞)|⟨x⟩2​I2​(x)|\displaystyle\sup_{x\in(x_{0},\infty)}\left|\langle x\rangle^{2}I_{2}(x)\right|≤C​‖r2‖Lz∞​supx∈(x0,∞)‖⟨x⟩​N−,12​(x;⋅)‖Lz2​supx∈(x0,∞)‖⟨x⟩​𝒫−​(z−1​r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x)‖Lz2\displaystyle\leq C\|r_{2}\|_{L_{z}^{\infty}}\sup_{x\in(x_{0},\infty)}\|\langle x\rangle N_{-,12}(x;\cdot)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\sup_{x\in(x_{0},\infty)}\left\|\langle x\rangle\mathcal{P}^{-}\left(z^{-1}r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\right)\right\|_{L_{z}^{2}}≤C​‖r1‖Hz1​‖z−1​r1‖Hz1\displaystyle\leq C\|r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}\|z^{-1}r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}(4.127)

which means‖qP​T​(x)‖L2,1​(ℝ+)≤C​(‖z−1​r1‖Hz1+‖r1‖Hz1​‖z−1​r1‖Hz1).\|q^{PT}(x)\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left(\|z^{-1}r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}+\|r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}\|z^{-1}r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}\right).(4.128)

ThusI2∈L2,1​(ℝ+)I_{2}\in L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}), and hence
(4.128) gives the required weighted estimate forqP​Tq^{PT}on the positive half-line.

To estimate(qP​T)x(q^{PT})_{x}, instead of differentiatingI2I_{2}, we use
the large-zzcoefficientν1+\nu_{1}^{+}in Proposition2.2. By (3.30) and (2.17),lim|z|→∞z​N−,12​(x;z)=e−i2​m+​(x)​ν1+​(x)=14​(qP​T)x​(x)​e−i​m+​(x).\lim_{|z|\to\infty}zN_{-,12}(x;z)=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(x)}\nu_{1}^{+}(x)=\frac{1}{4}(q^{PT})_{x}(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}m_{+}(x)}.(4.129)

On the other hand, the Beals–Coifman representation
(4.5), the identity (4.67), and the
Cauchy asymptotic formula (4.3) yield(qP​T)x​(x)​e−i​m+​(x)\displaystyle(q^{PT})_{x}(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}m_{+}(x)}=2​iπ​∫ℝr1​(z)​e2​i​z​x​N+,11​(x;z)​𝑑z\displaystyle=\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{+,11}(x;z)\,dz=:J1(x)+J2(x),\displaystyle=:J_{1}(x)+J_{2}(x),(4.130)

whereJ1​(x)\displaystyle J_{1}(x):=2​iπ​∫ℝr1​(z)​e2​i​z​x​𝑑z,\displaystyle:=\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\,dz,(4.131)J2​(x)\displaystyle J_{2}(x):=2​iπ​∫ℝr1​(z)​e2​i​z​x​(N+,11​(x;z)−1)​𝑑z.\displaystyle:=\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\left(N_{+,11}(x;z)-1\right)\,dz.(4.132)

By Plancherel’s theorem,‖J1‖L2,1​(ℝ+)≤C​‖r1‖Hz1.\|J_{1}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}.(4.133)

UsingN+,11​(x;z)−1=𝒫+​(r2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​N−,12​(x;z))N_{+,11}(x;z)-1=\mathcal{P}^{+}\left(r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{-,12}(x;z)\right)

and the adjoint relation of the Plemelj projections, we rewriteJ2​(x)=−2​iπ​∫ℝr2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​N−,12​(x;z)​𝒫−​(r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x)​(z)​𝑑z.J_{2}(x)=-\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{-,12}(x;z)\mathcal{P}^{-}\left(r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\right)(z)\,dz.(4.134)

Therefore, by (4.71) and
(4.55), for everyx0∈ℝ+x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+},supx∈(x0,∞)|⟨x⟩2​J2​(x)|\displaystyle\sup_{x\in(x_{0},\infty)}\left|\langle x\rangle^{2}J_{2}(x)\right|≤C​‖r2‖Lz∞​supx∈(x0,∞)‖⟨x⟩​N−,12​(x;⋅)‖Lz2​supx∈(x0,∞)‖⟨x⟩​𝒫−​(r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x)‖Lz2\displaystyle\leq C\|r_{2}\|_{L_{z}^{\infty}}\sup_{x\in(x_{0},\infty)}\|\langle x\rangle N_{-,12}(x;\cdot)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\sup_{x\in(x_{0},\infty)}\left\|\langle x\rangle\mathcal{P}^{-}\left(r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\right)\right\|_{L_{z}^{2}}≤C​‖r1‖Hz12.\displaystyle\leq C\|r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}^{2}.(4.135)

Since⟨x⟩−1∈L2​(ℝ+)\langle x\rangle^{-1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}), it follows that‖J2‖L2,1​(ℝ+)≤C​‖r1‖Hz12.\|J_{2}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}^{2}.

Combining this estimate with (4.133) and
the phase bound (4.117), we obtain‖(qP​T)x‖L2,1​(ℝ+)≤C​(‖r1‖Hz1+‖r1‖Hz12).\|(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left(\|r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}+\|r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}^{2}\right).(4.136)

We next estimate the spatial derivative by using the large-zzreconstruction formula (4.85). Combining
(4.85) with the Beals–Coifman representation givesei​m+​(x)−i2​m−​(x)​∂x(qx​(x)​ei2​m−​(x))\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}m_{+}(x)-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}\partial_{x}\left(q_{x}(x)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}\right)=−1π​∫ℝr2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​N+,22​(x;z)​𝑑z\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{+,22}(x;z)\,dz=:I3(x)+I4(x),\displaystyle=:I_{3}(x)+I_{4}(x),(4.137)

whereI3​(x)\displaystyle I_{3}(x):=−1π​∫ℝr2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​𝑑z,\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}\,dz,(4.138)I4​(x)\displaystyle I_{4}(x):=−1π​∫ℝr2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​(N+,22​(x;z)−1)​𝑑z.\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}\left(N_{+,22}(x;z)-1\right)\,dz.(4.139)

By Plancherel’s theorem,‖I3‖L2,1​(ℝ+)+‖∂xI3‖L2​(ℝ+)≤C​‖r2‖Hz1∩Lz2,1.\|I_{3}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|\partial_{x}I_{3}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\|r_{2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}.(4.140)

Indeed,∂xI3​(x)=2​iπ​∫ℝz​r2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​𝑑z,\partial_{x}I_{3}(x)=\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}zr_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}\,dz,

andz​r2∈Lz2​(ℝ)zr_{2}\in L_{z}^{2}(\mathbb{R})follows fromr2∈Lz2,1​(ℝ)r_{2}\in L_{z}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}).

For the remainderI4I_{4}, unlikeI2I_{2}, here one should not simply repeat the previous
adjoint-projection estimate. Instead, using the second-row estimate of the Beals–Coifman system, namely the estimate for the diagonal component
generated byN+,22−1N_{+,22}-1, we first obtain‖I4‖L2,1​(ℝ+)≤Cρ​‖r2‖Hz1​(ℝ)2.\|I_{4}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C_{\rho}\|r_{2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.(4.141)

We next estimate the derivative ofI4I_{4}separately. Differentiating
(4.139) gives∂xI4​(x)\displaystyle\partial_{x}I_{4}(x)=2​iπ​∫ℝz​r2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​(N+,22​(x;z)−1)​𝑑z−1π​∫ℝr2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​∂xN+,22​(x;z)​d​z.\displaystyle=\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}zr_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}\left(N_{+,22}(x;z)-1\right)\,dz-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}\partial_{x}N_{+,22}(x;z)\,dz.(4.142)

The two terms on the right-hand side are estimated by the differentiated
Beals–Coifman equation. More precisely, using
(4.56), (4.58),
(4.60), and the derivative bounds
(4.72)–(4.73), we get, for everyx0∈ℝ+x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+},supx∈(x0,∞)|⟨x⟩​∂xI4​(x)|\displaystyle\sup_{x\in(x_{0},\infty)}\left|\langle x\rangle\partial_{x}I_{4}(x)\right|≤Cρ​‖r2‖Hz1∩Lz2,1​‖r1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1​(‖z−1​r1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖r2‖Hz1∩Lz2,1).\displaystyle\leq C_{\rho}\|r_{2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}\|r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}\left(\|z^{-1}r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|r_{2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}\right).(4.143)

Since the norms ofr1r_{1}andr2r_{2}are controlled by the small norm
assumption, the product factor in

Consequently,‖∂xI4‖L2​(ℝ+)≤C​(‖z−1​r1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖r2‖Hz1∩Lz2,1).\|\partial_{x}I_{4}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left(\|z^{-1}r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|r_{2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}\right).(4.144)

Combining (4.141) and
(4.144), we obtain the refined bound‖I4‖L2,1​(ℝ+)+‖∂xI4‖L2​(ℝ+)≤C​(‖z−1​r1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖r2‖Hz1∩Lz2,1).\|I_{4}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}+\|\partial_{x}I_{4}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left(\|z^{-1}r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|r_{2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}\right).(4.145)

From (4.140)– (4.145), we can obtain that‖qx​x​x​(x)‖L2​(ℝ+)≤C​(‖z−1​r1‖H1∩L2,1+‖r2‖H1∩L2,1),\|q_{xxx}(x)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+})}\leq C\left(\|z^{-1}r_{1}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}}+\|r_{2}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}}\right),(4.146)

and‖∂x(qx​(x)​ei2​m+​(x))‖L2,1​(R+)≤𝒞​(‖r2‖H1+‖r2‖H12).\|\partial_{x}\left(q_{x}(x)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(x)}\right)\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathrm{R}^{+})}\leq\mathcal{C}(\|r_{2}\|_{H^{1}}+\|r_{2}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}).(4.147)

We now turn to the negative half-line. By the transformed RH problem and
the reconstruction formula (4.115),qP​T​(x)​e−i2​m+​(x)\displaystyle q^{PT}(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(x)}=δ+−1​(0)π​∫ℝz−1​rδ,1​(z)​e2​i​z​x​Nδ,−,11​(x;z)​𝑑z\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{+}^{-1}(0)}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}z^{-1}r_{\delta,1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{\delta,-,11}(x;z)\,dz=:I~1(x)+I~2(x).\displaystyle=:\widetilde{I}_{1}(x)+\widetilde{I}_{2}(x).(4.148)

The leading term satisfies, by Plancherel’s theorem and
(4.118),‖I~1‖L2,1​(ℝ−)+‖∂xI~1‖L2,1​(ℝ−)≤Cρ​‖z−1​rδ,1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1.\|\widetilde{I}_{1}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{-})}+\|\partial_{x}\widetilde{I}_{1}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{-})}\leq C_{\rho}\|z^{-1}r_{\delta,1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}.(4.149)

For the remainder, we use the column equation forNδ,−,11−1N_{\delta,-,11}-1, the adjoint relation of the Plemelj projections,
Proposition4.13, and
Proposition4.14. This gives, for everyx0∈ℝ−x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{-},supx∈(−∞,x0)|⟨x⟩2​I~2​(x)|≤Cρ​‖rδ,2‖Hz1​‖z−1​rδ,1‖Hz1.\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\left|\langle x\rangle^{2}\widetilde{I}_{2}(x)\right|\leq C_{\rho}\|r_{\delta,2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}\|z^{-1}r_{\delta,1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}.(4.150)

Combining (4.149) and
(4.150), and using
(4.117), we obtain‖qP​T‖L2,1​(ℝ−)≤C​(‖z−1​rδ,1‖Hz1+‖rδ,2‖Hz1​‖z−1​rδ,1‖Hz1).\|q^{PT}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{-})}\leq C\left(\|z^{-1}r_{\delta,1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}+\|r_{\delta,2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}\|z^{-1}r_{\delta,1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}\right).(4.151)

We next estimate(qP​T)x(q^{PT})_{x}on the negative half-line through the
same large-zzcoefficientν1\nu_{1}. SinceNδ=N​δ−σ3N_{\delta}=N\delta^{-\sigma_{3}}andδ​(z)→1\delta(z)\to 1as|z|→∞|z|\to\infty, the leading off-diagonal coefficient is unchanged:lim|z|→∞z​Nδ,±,12​(x;z)=14​(qP​T)x​(x)​e−i​m+​(x).\lim_{|z|\to\infty}zN_{\delta,\pm,12}(x;z)=\frac{1}{4}(q^{PT})_{x}(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}m_{+}(x)}.(4.152)

Using (4.95),
(4.101), and
(4.3), we obtain(qP​T)x​(x)​e−i​m+​(x)\displaystyle(q^{PT})_{x}(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}m_{+}(x)}=2​iπ​∫ℝrδ,1​(z)​e2​i​z​x​Nδ,−,11​(x;z)​𝑑z\displaystyle=\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{\delta,1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{\delta,-,11}(x;z)\,dz=:J~1(x)+J~2(x),\displaystyle=:\widetilde{J}_{1}(x)+\widetilde{J}_{2}(x),(4.153)

whereJ~1​(x)\displaystyle\widetilde{J}_{1}(x):=2​iπ​∫ℝrδ,1​(z)​e2​i​z​x​𝑑z,\displaystyle:=\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{\delta,1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\,dz,(4.154)J~2​(x)\displaystyle\widetilde{J}_{2}(x):=2​iπ​∫ℝrδ,1​(z)​e2​i​z​x​(Nδ,−,11​(x;z)−1)​𝑑z.\displaystyle:=\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{\delta,1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\left(N_{\delta,-,11}(x;z)-1\right)\,dz.(4.155)

By Plancherel’s theorem,‖J~1‖L2,1​(ℝ−)≤C​‖rδ,1‖Hz1.\|\widetilde{J}_{1}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{-})}\leq C\|r_{\delta,1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}.(4.156)

From (4.102),Nδ,−,11​(x;z)−1=𝒫−​(rδ,2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​Nδ,+,12​(x;z)).N_{\delta,-,11}(x;z)-1=\mathcal{P}^{-}\left(r_{\delta,2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{\delta,+,12}(x;z)\right).

Hence, by the adjoint relation of the Plemelj projections,J~2​(x)=−2​iπ​∫ℝrδ,2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​Nδ,+,12​(x;z)​𝒫+​(rδ,1​(z)​e2​i​z​x)​(z)​𝑑z.\widetilde{J}_{2}(x)=-\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{\delta,2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{\delta,+,12}(x;z)\mathcal{P}^{+}\left(r_{\delta,1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\right)(z)\,dz.(4.157)

The Fourier argument used in Proposition4.13givessupx∈(−∞,x0)‖⟨x⟩​𝒫+​(rδ,1​(z)​e2​i​z​x)‖Lz2≤‖rδ,1‖Hz1,x0∈ℝ−.\displaystyle\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\left\|\langle x\rangle\mathcal{P}^{+}\left(r_{\delta,1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\right)\right\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\leq\|r_{\delta,1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}},\qquad x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{-}.(4.158)

Combining this estimate with (4.111), we findsupx∈(−∞,x0)|⟨x⟩2​J~2​(x)|\displaystyle\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\left|\langle x\rangle^{2}\widetilde{J}_{2}(x)\right|≤C​‖rδ,2‖Lz∞​supx∈(−∞,x0)‖⟨x⟩​Nδ,+,12​(x;⋅)‖Lz2​supx∈(−∞,x0)‖⟨x⟩​𝒫+​(rδ,1​(z)​e2​i​z​x)‖Lz2\displaystyle\leq C\|r_{\delta,2}\|_{L_{z}^{\infty}}\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\|\langle x\rangle N_{\delta,+,12}(x;\cdot)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\sup_{x\in(-\infty,x_{0})}\left\|\langle x\rangle\mathcal{P}^{+}\left(r_{\delta,1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx}\right)\right\|_{L_{z}^{2}}≤C​‖rδ,1‖Hz12.\displaystyle\leq C\|r_{\delta,1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}^{2}.(4.159)

Therefore,‖J~2‖L2,1​(ℝ−)≤C​‖rδ,1‖Hz12.\|\widetilde{J}_{2}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{-})}\leq C\|r_{\delta,1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}^{2}.

Using (4.117), we first obtain‖(qP​T)x‖L2,1​(ℝ−)≤C​(‖rδ,1‖Hz1+‖rδ,1‖Hz12).\|(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{-})}\leq C\left(\|r_{\delta,1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}+\|r_{\delta,1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}}^{2}\right).

Consequently, by (4.121), and by absorbing the
quadratic factor into the constant on bounded subsets of the scattering
data space, we conclude that‖(qP​T)x‖L2,1​(ℝ−)≤C​(‖r1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖r2‖Hz1∩Lz2,1),\|(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{-})}\leq C\left(\|r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|r_{2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}\right),(4.160)

whereCCdepends on the small norm bound and on a fixed bound forr1,r2r_{1},r_{2}inHz1∩Lz2,1H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}.

Combining (4.128),
(4.151),
(4.136),
(4.160), and
(4.121), we have‖qP​T‖H1,1​(ℝ)≤C​(‖r1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖r2‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖z−1​r1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1).\|q^{PT}\|_{H^{1,1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C\left(\|r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|r_{2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|z^{-1}r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}\right).(4.161)

In particular, since(qP​T)x​(x)=−qx​(−x)(q^{PT})_{x}(x)=-q_{x}(-x), reflection preserves theL2,1L^{2,1}-norm and hence‖qx‖L2,1​(ℝ)=‖(qP​T)x‖L2,1​(ℝ)≤C​(‖r1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖r2‖Hz1∩Lz2,1).\|q_{x}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})}=\|(q^{PT})_{x}\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C\left(\|r_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|r_{2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}\right).(4.162)

Similarly, the large-zzreconstruction formula
(4.114) givesei​m+​(x)−i2​m−​(x)​∂x(qx​(x)​ei2​m−​(x))\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}m_{+}(x)-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}\partial_{x}\left(q_{x}(x)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x)}\right)=−1π​∫ℝrδ,2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​Nδ,+,22​(x;z)​𝑑z\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{\delta,2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}N_{\delta,+,22}(x;z)\,dz=:I~3(x)+I~4(x),\displaystyle=:\widetilde{I}_{3}(x)+\widetilde{I}_{4}(x),(4.163)

whereI~3​(x)\displaystyle\widetilde{I}_{3}(x):=−1π​∫ℝrδ,2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​𝑑z,\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{\delta,2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}\,dz,(4.164)I~4​(x)\displaystyle\widetilde{I}_{4}(x):=−1π​∫ℝrδ,2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x​(Nδ,+,22​(x;z)−1)​𝑑z.\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{\delta,2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx}\left(N_{\delta,+,22}(x;z)-1\right)\,dz.(4.165)

Through similar analysis as before, we can obtain that‖qx​x​x​(x)‖L2​(ℝ−)≤C​(‖z−1​r1‖H1∩L2,1+‖r2‖H1∩L2,1),\|q_{xxx}(x)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{-})}\leq C\left(\|z^{-1}r_{1}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}}+\|r_{2}\|_{H^{1}\cap L^{2,1}}\right),(4.166)

and‖∂x(qx​(x)​ei2​m+​(x))‖L2,1​(R−)≤𝒞​(‖r2‖H1+‖r2‖H12).\|\partial_{x}\left(q_{x}(x)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(x)}\right)\|_{L^{2,1}(\mathrm{R}^{-})}\leq\mathcal{C}(\|r_{2}\|_{H^{1}}+\|r_{2}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}).(4.167)

Combined (4.166), (4.167) with (4.161), (4.146) and (4.147), we have‖q‖H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)≤C​(‖r1‖𝒲​(ℝ)+‖r2‖𝒲​(ℝ))\|q\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C\left(\|r_{1}\|_{\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})}+\|r_{2}\|_{\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})}\right)(4.168)

which completes the proof.
 ∎

From Proposition4.15, we can get the following proposition:

## Proposition 4.16.

Suppose thatr1,2∈𝒲​(ℝ)r_{1,2}\in\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})and satisfies the small norm inequality (3.4), then mapping𝒲​(ℝ)∋(r1,r2)⟼q∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ),\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})\ni(r_{1},r_{2})\longmapsto q\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}),(4.169)

is Lipschitz continuous.

## Proof.

Supposer1,2,r~1,2∈𝒲​(ℝ)r_{1,2},\tilde{r}_{1,2}\in\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})satisfy the small norm inequality (3.4), and‖r1,2‖𝒲​(ℝ),‖r~1,2‖𝒲​(ℝ)≤ρ\|r_{1,2}\|_{\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})},\|\tilde{r}_{1,2}\|_{\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\rho(4.170)

for someρ>0\rho>0. Denote the corresponding potentials byqqandq~\tilde{q}respectively.

From the reconstruction formulas (4.122), (4.137) on the positive half-line, and (4.148), (4.163) on the negative half-line, we can represent the differenceq​(x)−q~​(x)q(x)-\tilde{q}(x)in terms of the differences of the reflection coefficients and the corresponding Beals-Coifman solutions.

By utilizing the resolvent identity for the integral Fredholm equations, similar to the technique used for the Jost solutions in (2.48), the differences of the RH solutionsN​(x;z)−N~​(x;z)N(x;z)-\tilde{N}(x;z)and the transformed solutionsNδ​(x;z)−N~δ​(x;z)N_{\delta}(x;z)-\tilde{N}_{\delta}(x;z)can be bounded by the differences of their respective jump matrices, which in turn are linearly controlled byr1,2−r~1,2r_{1,2}-\tilde{r}_{1,2}.

Repeating almost the exact same estimates as in the proof of Proposition4.15, we deduce that there exists a positive constantCCdependent onρ\rhosuch that‖q−q~‖H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)≤C​(‖z−1​(r1−r~1)‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖r1−r~1‖Hz1∩Lz2,1+‖r2−r~2‖Hz1∩Lz2,1)≤C​(‖r1−r~1‖𝒲​(ℝ)+‖r2−r~2‖𝒲​(ℝ)).\begin{split}\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})}&\leq C\left(\|z^{-1}(r_{1}-\tilde{r}_{1})\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|r_{1}-\tilde{r}_{1}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|r_{2}-\tilde{r}_{2}\|_{H_{z}^{1}\cap L_{z}^{2,1}}\right)\\
&\leq C\left(\|r_{1}-\tilde{r}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})}+\|r_{2}-\tilde{r}_{2}\|_{\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})}\right).\end{split}(4.171)

Using this representation for the difference betweenqqandq~\tilde{q}, the uniform bound ensures the Lipschitz continuity of the mapping (4.169).
 ∎

## 5Time evolution and global reconstruction

In the previous sections the time variable was regarded as a fixed parameter.
We now introduce the time evolution of the scattering data and then reconstruct
the solutionq​(x,t)q(x,t)from the corresponding time-dependent RH problem.
Throughout this section we writeΘ​(x,t;λ):=λ2​x−t4​λ2,z=λ2.\Theta(x,t;\lambda):=\lambda^{2}x-\frac{t}{4\lambda^{2}},\qquad z=\lambda^{2}.(5.1)

Then the exponential factors in the jump matrices are obtained by replacingλ2​x\lambda^{2}xwithΘ​(x,t;λ)\Theta(x,t;\lambda).

## 5.1Time evolution of the scattering data

From the Lax pair (2.2), the scattering relation for the Jost functions isψ−​(x,t;λ)=ψ+​(x,t;λ)​ei​Θ​(x,t;λ)​σ^3​S​(λ),S​(λ)=(a​(λ)b˘​(λ)b​(λ)a˘​(λ)),λ∈Σλ,\psi^{-}(x,t;\lambda)=\psi^{+}(x,t;\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Theta(x,t;\lambda)\hat{\sigma}_{3}}S(\lambda),\qquad S(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}a(\lambda)&\breve{b}(\lambda)\\
b(\lambda)&\breve{a}(\lambda)\end{pmatrix},\qquad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda},(5.2)

Equivalently, if one keeps thexx-part scattering conventionψ−​(x,t;λ)=ψ+​(x,t;λ)​ei​λ2​x​σ^3​S​(t;λ),\psi^{-}(x,t;\lambda)=\psi^{+}(x,t;\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}x\hat{\sigma}_{3}}S(t;\lambda),

thenS​(t;λ)=e−i​t4​λ2​σ^3​S​(0;λ).S(t;\lambda)=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{4\lambda^{2}}\hat{\sigma}_{3}}S(0;\lambda).(5.3)

Hencea​(t;λ)=a​(λ),a˘​(t;λ)=a˘​(λ),b​(t;λ)=b​(λ)​ei​t2​λ2,b˘​(t;λ)=b˘​(λ)​e−i​t2​λ2.\displaystyle a(t;\lambda)=a(\lambda),\quad\breve{a}(t;\lambda)=\breve{a}(\lambda),\quad b(t;\lambda)=b(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2\lambda^{2}}},\quad\breve{b}(t;\lambda)=\breve{b}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2\lambda^{2}}}.(5.4)

Consequently, the reflection coefficients on thezz-plane satisfyr1​(t;z)=r1​(z)​e−i​t2​z,r2​(t;z)=r2​(z)​ei​t2​z.r_{1}(t;z)=r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z}},\qquad r_{2}(t;z)=r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z}}.(5.5)

In particular,r1​(t;z)​r2​(t;z)=r1​(z)​r2​(z),r_{1}(t;z)r_{2}(t;z)=r_{1}(z)r_{2}(z),(5.6)

so the scalar RH functionδ​(z)\delta(z)defined in RH Problem4.10is independent oftt.

## Proposition 5.1.

LetT>0T>0be fixed. Suppose thatr1,r2∈𝒲​(ℝ).r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R}).Then, for everyt∈[−T,T]t\in[-T,T],r1​(t;⋅),r2​(t;⋅)∈𝒲​(ℝ).r_{1}(t;\cdot),\ r_{2}(t;\cdot)\in\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R}).Moreover, there exists a constantCT>0C_{T}>0such that‖r1​(t;⋅)‖𝒲+‖r2​(t;⋅)‖𝒲≤CT​(‖r1‖𝒲+‖r2‖𝒲).\|r_{1}(t;\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{W}}+\|r_{2}(t;\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{W}}\leq C_{T}\left(\|r_{1}\|_{\mathcal{W}}+\|r_{2}\|_{\mathcal{W}}\right).(5.7)

## Proof.

Since the exponential factors in (5.5) have modulus one
forz∈ℝz\in\mathbb{R}, we have‖rj​(t;⋅)‖Lz2=‖rj‖Lz2,‖rj​(t;⋅)‖Lz2,1=‖rj‖Lz2,1,‖rj​(t;⋅)‖Lz2,−2=‖rj‖Lz2,−2,j=1,2.\|r_{j}(t;\cdot)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}=\|r_{j}\|_{L_{z}^{2}},\quad\|r_{j}(t;\cdot)\|_{L_{z}^{2,1}}=\|r_{j}\|_{L_{z}^{2,1}},\quad\|r_{j}(t;\cdot)\|_{L_{z}^{2,-2}}=\|r_{j}\|_{L_{z}^{2,-2}},\quad j=1,2.(5.8)

For theH1H^{1}-norm, differentiating (5.5) gives∂zr1​(t;z)\displaystyle\partial_{z}r_{1}(t;z)=e−i​t2​z​(∂zr1​(z)+i​t2​z2​r1​(z)),\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z}}\left(\partial_{z}r_{1}(z)+\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z^{2}}r_{1}(z)\right),(5.9)∂zr2​(t;z)\displaystyle\partial_{z}r_{2}(t;z)=ei​t2​z​(∂zr2​(z)−i​t2​z2​r2​(z)).\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z}}\left(\partial_{z}r_{2}(z)-\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z^{2}}r_{2}(z)\right).(5.10)

Thus, fort∈[−T,T]t\in[-T,T],‖∂zrj​(t;⋅)‖Lz2≤‖∂zrj‖Lz2+T2​‖z−2​rj‖Lz2,j=1,2.\|\partial_{z}r_{j}(t;\cdot)\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\leq\|\partial_{z}r_{j}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}+\frac{T}{2}\|z^{-2}r_{j}\|_{L_{z}^{2}},\qquad j=1,2.(5.11)

This proves (5.7).

∎

## 5.2The time-dependent RH problems

Using (5.4), the RH problem on theλ\lambda-plane
becomes the following one.

## Riemann-Hilbert Problem 5.2.

Find a matrix functionΦ​(x,t;λ)\Phi(x,t;\lambda)with the following properties:
- 1.

Analyticity:Φ​(x,t;λ)\Phi(x,t;\lambda)is analytic inℂ∖Σλ\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma_{\lambda}.
- 2.

Jump condition:Φ+​(x,t;λ)=Φ−​(x,t;λ)​(I+V​(x,t;λ)),λ∈Σλ,\Phi_{+}(x,t;\lambda)=\Phi_{-}(x,t;\lambda)\left(I+V(x,t;\lambda)\right),\qquad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\lambda},(5.12)

whereV​(x,t;λ)=(0r˘​(λ)​e2​i​Θ​(x,t;λ)−r​(λ)​e−2​i​Θ​(x,t;λ)−r​(λ)​r˘​(λ)).V(x,t;\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}0&\breve{r}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}\Theta(x,t;\lambda)}\\[3.99994pt]
-r(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}\Theta(x,t;\lambda)}&-r(\lambda)\breve{r}(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}.(5.13)
- 3.

Normalization:Φ​(x,t;λ)=I+𝒪​(λ−1),as​λ→∞.\Phi(x,t;\lambda)=I+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-1}),\,\,{\rm as}\,\,\lambda\to\infty.

In thezz-plane, the time-dependent RH problem takes the form

## Riemann-Hilbert Problem 5.3.

FindN​(x,t;z)N(x,t;z)such that:
- 1.

Analyticity:N​(x,t;z)N(x,t;z)is analytic inℂ∖ℝ\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}.
- 2.

Jump condition:The boundary values satisfyN+​(x,t;z)=N−​(x,t;z)​(I+R​(x,t;z)),z∈ℝ,N_{+}(x,t;z)=N_{-}(x,t;z)\left(I+R(x,t;z)\right),\qquad z\in\mathbb{R},(5.14)

whereR​(x,t;z)=(0r1​(z)​e2​i​z​x−i​t2​zr2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x+i​t2​zr1​(z)​r2​(z)).R(x,t;z)=\begin{pmatrix}0&r_{1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx-\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z}}\\[3.99994pt]
r_{2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx+\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z}}&r_{1}(z)r_{2}(z)\end{pmatrix}.(5.15)
- 3.

Normalization:N​(x,t;z)=I+𝒪​(z−1)N(x,t;z)=I+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})asz→∞z\to\infty.

Becauser1​(t;z)​r2​(t;z)=r1​(z)​r2​(z)r_{1}(t;z)r_{2}(t;z)=r_{1}(z)r_{2}(z), the functionδ​(z)\delta(z)defined by (4.89) is unchanged under the time evolution.
Thus the transformed reflection coefficients arerδ,1​(t;z)=rδ,1​(z)​e−i​t2​z,rδ,2​(t;z)=rδ,2​(z)​ei​t2​z.r_{\delta,1}(t;z)=r_{\delta,1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z}},\qquad r_{\delta,2}(t;z)=r_{\delta,2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z}}.(5.16)

The time-dependentδ\delta-transformed RH problem is thereforeNδ,+​(x,t;z)=Nδ,−​(x,t;z)​(I+Rδ​(x,t;z)),N_{\delta,+}(x,t;z)=N_{\delta,-}(x,t;z)\left(I+R_{\delta}(x,t;z)\right),

withRδ​(x,t;z)=(rδ,1​(z)​rδ,2​(z)rδ,1​(z)​e2​i​z​x−i​t2​zrδ,2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x+i​t2​z0).R_{\delta}(x,t;z)=\begin{pmatrix}r_{\delta,1}(z)r_{\delta,2}(z)&r_{\delta,1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx-\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z}}\\[3.99994pt]
r_{\delta,2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx+\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z}}&0\end{pmatrix}.(5.17)

The reconstruction formulae now becomeei​m+​(x,t)−i2​m−​(x,t)​∂x(qx​(x,t)​ei2​m−​(x,t))=−1π​∫ℝrδ,2​(z)​e−2​i​z​x+i​t2​z​Nδ,+,22​(x,t;z)​𝑑z,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}m_{+}(x,t)-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x,t)}\partial_{x}\left(q_{x}(x,t)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{-}(x,t)}\right)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}r_{\delta,2}(z)\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}zx+\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z}}N_{\delta,+,22}(x,t;z)\,dz,(5.18)

andqP​T​(x,t)​e−i2​m+​(x,t)=δ+−1​(0)π​∫ℝz−1​rδ,1​(z)​e2​i​z​x−i​t2​z​Nδ,−,11​(x,t;z)​𝑑z.q^{PT}(x,t)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(x,t)}=\frac{\delta_{+}^{-1}(0)}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}z^{-1}r_{\delta,1}(z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}zx-\frac{\mathrm{i}t}{2z}}N_{\delta,-,11}(x,t;z)\,dz.(5.19)

## 5.3Verification of the reconstructed solution

In this subsection, we verify that the potential reconstructed from the
time-dependent RH problems satisfies the nonlocal Fokas–Lenells equation.
Throughout the subsection we setY​(x,t;λ):=E​(x,t)​Φ​(x,t;λ),E​(x,t):=ei2​m+​(x,t)​σ3.\displaystyle Y(x,t;\lambda):=E(x,t)\Phi(x,t;\lambda),\qquad E(x,t):=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+}(x,t)\sigma_{3}}.(5.20)

From the definition ofΦ\Phi, the small-λ\lambdaexpansion of the
Jost functions in (2.85), we haveY​(x,t;λ)=I+i​λ​Q​(x,t)+λ2​D​(x,t)+𝒪​(λ3),λ→0,Y(x,t;\lambda)=I+\mathrm{i}\lambda Q(x,t)+\lambda^{2}D(x,t)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3}),\qquad\lambda\to 0,(5.21)

whereD​(x,t)D(x,t)is diagonal. In particular,Yx​Y−1\displaystyle Y_{x}Y^{-1}=i​λ​Qx+𝒪​(λ2),\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\lambda Q_{x}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),(5.22)Yt​Y−1\displaystyle Y_{t}Y^{-1}=𝒪​(λ),\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\lambda),(5.23)Y​σ3​Y−1\displaystyle Y\sigma_{3}Y^{-1}=σ3+2​i​λ​Q​σ3−2​λ2​Q2​σ3+𝒪​(λ3).\displaystyle=\sigma_{3}+2\mathrm{i}\lambda Q\sigma_{3}-2\lambda^{2}Q^{2}\sigma_{3}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3}).(5.24)

## Proposition 5.4.

LetΦ​(x,t;λ)\Phi(x,t;\lambda)be the solution of the time-dependent RH
Problem5.2. Defineη​(x,t;λ):=E​(x;t)​Φ​(x,t;λ)​ei​Θ​(x,t;λ)​σ3.\eta(x,t;\lambda):=E(x;t)\Phi(x,t;\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Theta(x,t;\lambda)\sigma_{3}}.(5.25)

Thenη​(x,t;λ)\eta(x,t;\lambda)satisfies thexx-part of the Lax pair
(1.8), namelyηx=(i​λ2​σ3+i​λ​Qx)​η.\eta_{x}=\left(\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}\sigma_{3}+\mathrm{i}\lambda Q_{x}\right)\eta.(5.26)

## Proof.

Multiplying the jump relation (5.12) byei​Θ​σ3\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Theta\sigma_{3}}from the right givesΦ+​ei​Θ​σ3=Φ−​ei​Θ​σ3​J0​(λ),\Phi_{+}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Theta\sigma_{3}}=\Phi_{-}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Theta\sigma_{3}}J_{0}(\lambda),

whereJ0​(λ)=(1r˘​(λ)−r​(λ)1−r​(λ)​r˘​(λ))J_{0}(\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}1&\breve{r}(\lambda)\\
-r(\lambda)&1-r(\lambda)\breve{r}(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}(5.27)

is independent ofxxandtt. Henceη+=η−​J0\eta_{+}=\eta_{-}J_{0}, andηx​η−1\eta_{x}\eta^{-1}has no jump acrossΣλ\Sigma_{\lambda}. Therefore
the possible singularities ofηx​η−1\eta_{x}\eta^{-1}can only occur atλ=0\lambda=0andλ=∞\lambda=\infty.

We first analyze the behavior at infinity. FromΦ​(x,t;λ)=I+λ−1​Φ(1)​(x,t)+𝒪​(λ−2),λ→∞,\Phi(x,t;\lambda)=I+\lambda^{-1}\Phi^{(1)}(x,t)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-2}),\qquad\lambda\to\infty,

andΘx=λ2\Theta_{x}=\lambda^{2}[cf. (5.1)], we obtainηx​η−1=i2​m+,x​σ3+E​(Φx​Φ−1+i​λ2​Φ​σ3​Φ−1)​E−1.\eta_{x}\eta^{-1}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+,x}\sigma_{3}+E\left(\Phi_{x}\Phi^{-1}+\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}\Phi\sigma_{3}\Phi^{-1}\right)E^{-1}.(5.28)

Thusηx​η−1=i​λ2​σ3+i​λ​E​[Φ(1),σ3]​E−1+𝒪​(1),λ→∞.\eta_{x}\eta^{-1}=\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}\sigma_{3}+\mathrm{i}\lambda E[\Phi^{(1)},\sigma_{3}]E^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(1),\qquad\lambda\to\infty.(5.29)

The large-λ\lambdareconstruction formula givesE​[Φ(1),σ3]​E−1=Qx.E[\Phi^{(1)},\sigma_{3}]E^{-1}=Q_{x}.(5.30)

Consequently,ηx​η−1−i​λ2​σ3−i​λ​Qx=𝒪​(1),λ→∞.\eta_{x}\eta^{-1}-\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}\sigma_{3}-\mathrm{i}\lambda Q_{x}=\mathcal{O}(1),\qquad\lambda\to\infty.(5.31)

At the origin, usingη=Y​ei​Θ​σ3\eta=Y\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Theta\sigma_{3}},Θx=λ2\Theta_{x}=\lambda^{2}, and (5.22)–(5.24), we haveηx​η−1=Yx​Y−1+i​λ2​Y​σ3​Y−1=i​λ​Qx+i​λ2​σ3+𝒪​(λ2),λ→0.\eta_{x}\eta^{-1}=Y_{x}Y^{-1}+\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}Y\sigma_{3}Y^{-1}=\mathrm{i}\lambda Q_{x}+\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}\sigma_{3}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),\qquad\lambda\to 0.(5.32)

DefineFx​(λ):=ηx​η−1−i​λ2​σ3−i​λ​Qx.F_{x}(\lambda):=\eta_{x}\eta^{-1}-\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}\sigma_{3}-\mathrm{i}\lambda Q_{x}.

By the preceding discussion,FxF_{x}has no jump, is removable atλ=0\lambda=0, and is bounded at infinity. Liouville’s theorem implies
thatFxF_{x}is constant. Since (5.32) givesFx​(λ)=𝒪​(λ2)F_{x}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})asλ→0\lambda\to 0, the constant
is zero. This proves (5.26). ∎

## Proposition 5.5.

The functionη​(x,t;λ)\eta(x,t;\lambda)defined by (5.25)
satisfies thett-part of the Lax pair (1.8), namelyηt=(−i4​λ2​σ3+12​λ​Q​σ3+i2​Q2​σ3)​η.\eta_{t}=\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4\lambda^{2}}\sigma_{3}+\frac{1}{2\lambda}Q\sigma_{3}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}Q^{2}\sigma_{3}\right)\eta.(5.33)

## Proof.

The same conjugation of the jump relation shows thatηt​η−1\eta_{t}\eta^{-1}has no jump acrossΣλ\Sigma_{\lambda}. Its possible
singularities are again only atλ=0\lambda=0andλ=∞\lambda=\infty.
Moreover,ηt​η−1=i2​m+,t​σ3+E​(Φt​Φ−1+i​Θt​Φ​σ3​Φ−1)​E−1.\eta_{t}\eta^{-1}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}m_{+,t}\sigma_{3}+E\left(\Phi_{t}\Phi^{-1}+\mathrm{i}\Theta_{t}\Phi\sigma_{3}\Phi^{-1}\right)E^{-1}.(5.34)

Asλ→∞\lambda\to\infty, we haveΘt=−1/(4​λ2)\Theta_{t}=-1/(4\lambda^{2})andΦ=I+𝒪​(λ−1)\Phi=I+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-1}). Henceηt​η−1=𝒪​(1),λ→∞.\eta_{t}\eta^{-1}=\mathcal{O}(1),\qquad\lambda\to\infty.(5.35)

Nearλ=0\lambda=0, we writeηt​η−1=Yt​Y−1+i​Θt​Y​σ3​Y−1,Θt=−14​λ2.\eta_{t}\eta^{-1}=Y_{t}Y^{-1}+\mathrm{i}\Theta_{t}Y\sigma_{3}Y^{-1},\qquad\Theta_{t}=-\frac{1}{4\lambda^{2}}.

Using (5.23) and (5.24), we getηt​η−1=−i4​λ2​σ3+12​λ​Q​σ3+i2​Q2​σ3+𝒪​(λ),λ→0.\eta_{t}\eta^{-1}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4\lambda^{2}}\sigma_{3}+\frac{1}{2\lambda}Q\sigma_{3}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}Q^{2}\sigma_{3}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda),\qquad\lambda\to 0.(5.36)

DefineFt​(λ):=ηt​η−1+i4​λ2​σ3−12​λ​Q​σ3−i2​Q2​σ3.F_{t}(\lambda):=\eta_{t}\eta^{-1}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4\lambda^{2}}\sigma_{3}-\frac{1}{2\lambda}Q\sigma_{3}-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}Q^{2}\sigma_{3}.

ThenFtF_{t}has no jump, the singularity atλ=0\lambda=0is
removable by (5.36), andFt=𝒪​(1)F_{t}=\mathcal{O}(1)at
infinity by (5.35). HenceFtF_{t}is constant by
Liouville’s theorem. Since (5.36) givesFt​(λ)=𝒪​(λ)F_{t}(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}(\lambda)asλ→0\lambda\to 0, this constant
is zero. Therefore (5.33) holds. ∎

## Corollary 5.6.

The potentialq​(x,t)q(x,t)reconstructed from the time-dependent RH problem
satisfies the nonlocal Fokas–Lenells equation (1.7)

## Proof.

By Propositions5.4and5.5,η\etasatisfiesηx=X​η,ηt=T​η,\eta_{x}=X\eta,\qquad\eta_{t}=T\eta,

whereX=i​λ2​σ3+i​λ​Qx,T=−i4​λ2​σ3+12​λ​Q​σ3+i2​Q2​σ3.X=\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}\sigma_{3}+\mathrm{i}\lambda Q_{x},\qquad T=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4\lambda^{2}}\sigma_{3}+\frac{1}{2\lambda}Q\sigma_{3}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}Q^{2}\sigma_{3}.

The compatibility conditionηx​t=ηt​x\eta_{xt}=\eta_{tx}is equivalent toXt−Tx+[X,T]=0.X_{t}-T_{x}+[X,T]=0.(5.37)

UsingQ​σ3=−σ3​QQ\sigma_{3}=-\sigma_{3}Q,Qx​σ3=−σ3​QxQ_{x}\sigma_{3}=-\sigma_{3}Q_{x}, andQ2=q​qP​T​IQ^{2}=qq^{PT}I, a direct computation givesXt−Tx+[X,T]=i​λ​Qx​t−i​λ​Q−λ​Q2​Qx​σ3.\displaystyle X_{t}-T_{x}+[X,T]=\mathrm{i}\lambda Q_{xt}-\mathrm{i}\lambda Q-\lambda Q^{2}Q_{x}\sigma_{3}.(5.38)

Therefore, (5.37) with (5.38) is equivalent toQx​t−Q+i​Q2​Qx​σ3=0.Q_{xt}-Q+\mathrm{i}Q^{2}Q_{x}\sigma_{3}=0.(5.39)

SinceQ=(0qP​Tq0),Q2​Qx​σ3=q​qP​T​(0−(qP​T)xqx0),Q=\begin{pmatrix}0&q^{PT}\\
q&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad Q^{2}Q_{x}\sigma_{3}=qq^{PT}\begin{pmatrix}0&-(q^{PT})_{x}\\
q_{x}&0\end{pmatrix},

the(2,1)(2,1)-entry of (5.39) gives exactlyqx​t−q+i​q​qP​T​qx=0.q_{xt}-q+\mathrm{i}qq^{PT}q_{x}=0.

The(1,2)(1,2)-entry gives the corresponding equation forqP​Tq^{PT}. Thus the reconstructed potential is a solution of
(1.7). ∎

## 6Existence of global solutions to the nFL equation

## Proposition 6.1.

Letq0∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q_{0}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})satisfy the
small-norm condition (3.4). Then, for everyT>0T>0, there exists a unique
solutionq∈C​([−T,T],H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ))q\in C\left([-T,T],H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\right)(6.1)

to the Cauchy problem of the nonlocal Fokas–Lenells equation. Moreover, on bounded subsets of the initial data space, the solution mapq0⟼q​(⋅,t)∈C​([−T,T],H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ))q_{0}\longmapsto q(\cdot,t)\in C\left([-T,T],H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\right)(6.2)

is Lipschitz continuous.

## Proof.

Starting fromq0q_{0}, Proposition3.10gives
reflection coefficients:r1​(0;⋅),r2​(0;⋅)∈𝒲​(ℝ).r_{1}(0;\cdot),r_{2}(0;\cdot)\in\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R}).The time evolution is defined by (5.5). By Proposition5.1, for everyt∈[−T,T]t\in[-T,T],r1​(t;⋅),r2​(t;⋅)∈𝒲​(ℝ).r_{1}(t;\cdot),r_{2}(t;\cdot)\in\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R}).Moreover, the productr1​(t;z)​r2​(t;z)r_{1}(t;z)r_{2}(t;z)is independent oftt, so
the scalar functionδ​(z)\delta(z)and the coercivity estimates used in
the solvability of the RH problem remain valid for allt∈[−T,T]t\in[-T,T].

For each fixedtt, solving the time-dependent RH problem5.3and applying the reconstruction formulae
(5.18)–(5.19) gives a potentialq​(x,t)q(x,t). Proposition4.15then yields‖q​(⋅,t)‖H3∩H2,1\displaystyle\|q(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{3}\cap H^{2,1}}≤C​(‖r1​(t;⋅)‖𝒲+‖r2​(t;⋅)‖𝒲)\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|r_{1}(t;\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{W}}+\|r_{2}(t;\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{W}}\right)≤CT​(‖r1​(0;⋅)‖𝒲+‖r2​(0;⋅)‖𝒲).\displaystyle\leq C_{T}\left(\|r_{1}(0;\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{W}}+\|r_{2}(0;\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{W}}\right).(6.3)

By the direct scattering estimates Proposition3.10, the right-hand side is controlled by
theH3∩H2,1H^{3}\cap H^{2,1}-norm ofq0q_{0}. Thus, we have the a priori estimate‖q​(⋅,t)‖H3∩H2,1≤C​(T)​‖q0‖H3∩H2,1.\|q(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{3}\cap H^{2,1}}\leq C(T)\|q_{0}\|_{H^{3}\cap H^{2,1}}.(6.4)

We next prove the continuity ofqqwith respect tott. By
Proposition3.10, we haverj∈𝒲​(ℝ),j=1,2.r_{j}\in\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R}),\qquad j=1,2.(6.5)

For convenience, writeΘj​(t,z):=eθj​i​t2​z,θ1=−1,θ2=1.\Theta_{j}(t,z):=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\theta_{j}\mathrm{i}t}{2z}},\qquad\theta_{1}=-1,\quad\theta_{2}=1.(6.6)

Then the time evolution (5.5) can be written asrj​(t;z)=rj​(z)​Θj​(t,z),j=1,2.r_{j}(t;z)=r_{j}(z)\Theta_{j}(t,z),\qquad j=1,2.(6.7)

Fort1,t2∈[0,T]t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T], setΔj(z):=rj(t2;z)−rj(t1;z)=rj(z)[Θj(t2,z)−Θj(t1,z)].\Delta_{j}(z):=r_{j}(t_{2};z)-r_{j}(t_{1};z)=r_{j}(z)\left[\Theta_{j}(t_{2},z)-\Theta_{j}(t_{1},z)\right].(6.8)

Since|Θj​(t,z)|=1|\Theta_{j}(t,z)|=1, the weightedL2L^{2}-parts follow from
the dominated convergence theorem:‖Δj‖Lz2,1+‖Δj‖Lz2,−2⟶0,t2→t1,j=1,2.\|\Delta_{j}\|_{L_{z}^{2,1}}+\|\Delta_{j}\|_{L_{z}^{2,-2}}\longrightarrow 0,\qquad t_{2}\to t_{1},\qquad j=1,2.(6.9)

It remains to estimate the derivative part. Direct differentiation gives∂zΔj​(z)\displaystyle\partial_{z}\Delta_{j}(z)=∂zrj​(z)​[Θj​(t2,z)−Θj​(t1,z)]−θj​i2​z2​rj​(z)​[t2​Θj​(t2,z)−t1​Θj​(t1,z)].\displaystyle=\partial_{z}r_{j}(z)\left[\Theta_{j}(t_{2},z)-\Theta_{j}(t_{1},z)\right]-\frac{\theta_{j}\mathrm{i}}{2z^{2}}r_{j}(z)\left[t_{2}\Theta_{j}(t_{2},z)-t_{1}\Theta_{j}(t_{1},z)\right].(6.10)

Letε>0\varepsilon>0, by the absolute continuity of theL2L^{2}-integral,
there existsδ>0\delta>0such that∑j=12(2​‖∂zrj‖Lz2​(|z|<δ)+T​‖z−2​rj‖Lz2​(|z|<δ))<ε.\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(2\|\partial_{z}r_{j}\|_{L_{z}^{2}(|z|<\delta)}+T\|z^{-2}r_{j}\|_{L_{z}^{2}(|z|<\delta)}\right)<\varepsilon.(6.11)

Using (6.10) and|Θj​(t,z)|=1|\Theta_{j}(t,z)|=1, we obtain∑j=12‖∂zΔj‖Lz2​(|z|<δ)<ε.\sum_{j=1}^{2}\|\partial_{z}\Delta_{j}\|_{L_{z}^{2}(|z|<\delta)}<\varepsilon.(6.12)

On the region|z|≥δ|z|\geq\delta, the phase factors are smooth intt.
Moreover,|Θj​(t2,z)−Θj​(t1,z)|≤Cδ​|t2−t1|,|z|≥δ,\left|\Theta_{j}(t_{2},z)-\Theta_{j}(t_{1},z)\right|\leq C_{\delta}|t_{2}-t_{1}|,\qquad|z|\geq\delta,(6.13)

and|t2​Θj​(t2,z)−t1​Θj​(t1,z)|≤Cδ,T​|t2−t1|,|z|≥δ.\left|t_{2}\Theta_{j}(t_{2},z)-t_{1}\Theta_{j}(t_{1},z)\right|\leq C_{\delta,T}|t_{2}-t_{1}|,\qquad|z|\geq\delta.(6.14)

Therefore, by (6.10),∑j=12‖∂zΔj‖Lz2​(|z|≥δ)≤Cδ,T​|t2−t1|​∑j=12(‖∂zrj‖Lz2+‖z−2​rj‖Lz2).\sum_{j=1}^{2}\|\partial_{z}\Delta_{j}\|_{L_{z}^{2}(|z|\geq\delta)}\leq C_{\delta,T}|t_{2}-t_{1}|\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(\|\partial_{z}r_{j}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}+\|z^{-2}r_{j}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\right).(6.15)

Combining (6.12) and
(6.15), and then lettingt2→t1t_{2}\to t_{1}, we get∑j=12‖∂zΔj‖Lz2⟶0,t2→t1.\sum_{j=1}^{2}\|\partial_{z}\Delta_{j}\|_{L_{z}^{2}}\longrightarrow 0,\qquad t_{2}\to t_{1}.(6.16)

Together with (6.9), this proves∑j=12‖rj​(t2;⋅)−rj​(t1;⋅)‖𝒲⟶0,t2→t1.\sum_{j=1}^{2}\|r_{j}(t_{2};\cdot)-r_{j}(t_{1};\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{W}}\longrightarrow 0,\qquad t_{2}\to t_{1}.(6.17)

Finally, using the Lipschitz continuity of the inverse scattering map in
Proposition4.16, we obtain‖q​(⋅,t2)−q​(⋅,t1)‖H3∩H2,1\displaystyle\|q(\cdot,t_{2})-q(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H^{3}\cap H^{2,1}}≤Cρ​∑j=12‖rj​(t2;⋅)−rj​(t1;⋅)‖𝒲⟶0,t2→t1.\displaystyle\leq C_{\rho}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\|r_{j}(t_{2};\cdot)-r_{j}(t_{1};\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{W}}\longrightarrow 0,\qquad t_{2}\to t_{1}.(6.18)

Henceq∈C​([−T,T],H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)).q\in C\left([-T,T],H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\right).(6.19)

Letq0,q~0∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q_{0},\widetilde{q}_{0}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})satisfy‖q0‖H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ),‖q~0‖H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)≤U\|q_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})},\|\widetilde{q}_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq Ufor someU>0U>0. From (6.3) and Lipschitz continuity in Proposition3.10and Proposition4.16, we have‖q−q~‖C​([−T,T],H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ))=‖q​(⋅;t∗)−q~​(⋅;t∗)‖H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)≤C1​(U)​(‖r1​(⋅;t∗)−r1~​(⋅;t∗)‖𝒲+‖r2​(⋅;t∗)−r2~​(⋅;t∗)‖𝒲)≤C2​(U,T)​(‖r1​(⋅;0)−r1~​(⋅;0)‖𝒲+‖r2​(⋅;0)−r2~​(⋅;0)‖𝒲)≤C​(U,T)​‖q0−q~0‖H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ),\begin{split}\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{C\left([-T,T],H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\right)}&=\|q(\cdot;t^{*})-\tilde{q}(\cdot;t^{*})\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})}\\
&\leq C_{1}(U)\left(\|r_{1}(\cdot;t^{*})-\widetilde{r_{1}}(\cdot;t^{*})\|_{\mathcal{W}}+\|r_{2}(\cdot;t^{*})-\widetilde{r_{2}}(\cdot;t^{*})\|_{\mathcal{W}}\right)\\
&\leq C_{2}(U,T)\left(\|r_{1}(\cdot;0)-\widetilde{r_{1}}(\cdot;0)\|_{\mathcal{W}}+\|r_{2}(\cdot;0)-\widetilde{r_{2}}(\cdot;0)\|_{\mathcal{W}}\right)\\
&\leq C(U,T)\|q_{0}-\tilde{q}_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})},\end{split}(6.20)

wheret∗∈[−T,T]t^{*}\in[-T,T]andC​(U,T)C(U,T)is a polynomial function with respect toTTfrom (5.11).
This rigorously proves the mappingH3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)∋q0⟼q∈C​([−T,T],H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ))H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\ni q_{0}\longmapsto q\in C\left([-T,T],H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\right)

is Lipschitz continuous.

Finally, Propositions5.4and5.5show that the reconstructed function satisfies the Lax pair, hence by
Corollary5.6it solves the nonlocal Fokas–Lenells
equation. Uniqueness follows from the uniqueness of the RH problem and the
Lipschitz continuity of both the direct and inverse scattering maps.
 ∎

## Theorem 6.2.

Letq0∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q_{0}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})satisfy the
small-norm condition (3.4).
Then the Cauchy problem of the nonlocal Fokas–Lenells equation admits a
unique global solutionq∈C​(ℝ,H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)).q\in C\left(\mathbb{R},H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\right).(6.21)

Furthermore, the solution mapH3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)∋q0​(x)⟼q​(x;t)∈C​(ℝ,H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ))H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\ni q_{0}(x)\longmapsto q(x;t)\in C\left(\mathbb{R},H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\right)(6.22)

is Lipschitz continuous.

## Proof.

The idea of the proof follows from the argument of[41]. Based on Proposition6.1, asC​(T)C(T)depends onTTand‖r1,2​(0;z)‖𝒲​(ℝ)\|r_{1,2}(0;z)\|_{\mathcal{W}(\mathbb{R})}grows at most in a polynomial order with respect to‖q0‖H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)\|q_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})}, we hence reach the conclusion that the local solution exists in the spaceC​([0,T],H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ))C\left([0,T],H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})\right)for an arbitrary fixedT>0T>0. Then the global existence of the solution can be directly asserted from the a priori estimation in (6.4).

It remains to prove the Lipschitz continuity of the solution map.
Letq0,q~0∈H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)q_{0},\widetilde{q}_{0}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})satisfy‖q0‖H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ),‖q~0‖H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)≤U\displaystyle\|q_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})},\|\widetilde{q}_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq U(6.23)

for someU>0U>0.

Defined​(q,q~):=∑n=1∞‖q−q~‖n2n​(1+‖q−q~‖n),‖q−q~‖n:=‖q−q~‖C​([−n,n],H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)).d(q,\widetilde{q}):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\|q-\widetilde{q}\|_{n}}{2^{n}\left(1+\|q-\widetilde{q}\|_{n}\right)},\qquad\|q-\widetilde{q}\|_{n}:=\|q-\widetilde{q}\|_{C([-n,n],H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}))}.(6.24)

Then, by (6.20), we obtaind​(q,q~)\displaystyle d(q,\widetilde{q})≤∑n=1∞C​(U,n)​‖q0−q~0‖H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ)2n​(1+C​(U,n)​‖q0−q~0‖H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ))\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{C(U,n)\|q_{0}-\widetilde{q}_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})}}{2^{n}\left(1+C(U,n)\|q_{0}-\widetilde{q}_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})}\right)}(6.25)≤∑n=1∞C​(U,n)2n​‖q0−q~0‖H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ).\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{C(U,n)}{2^{n}}\|q_{0}-\widetilde{q}_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})}.

SinceC​(U,n)C(U,n)grows at most polynomially innn, the series∑n=1∞C​(U,n)2n\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{C(U,n)}{2^{n}}

is convergent. Hence there exists a constantC​(U)>0C(U)>0such thatd​(q,q~)≤C​(U)​‖q0−q~0‖H3​(ℝ)∩H2,1​(ℝ).d(q,\widetilde{q})\leq C(U)\|q_{0}-\widetilde{q}_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{2,1}(\mathbb{R})}.(6.26)

Thus, the proof of Theorem6.2is achieved.
